Verified, not assumed. The pinned linter exits 1 on a copy with one description un-quoted and 0 on the contract as it now stands. A gate nobody has watched fail is not known to be a gate.

Consequence. openapi.yaml can be circulated for the confirm/decline of T-038 without the first thing ZC's tooling reports being our punctuation. Any new stat type added to the contract (CLAUDE.md §5 rule 6) is linted on the same push that adds it.

Not closed by this. The schema-drift obligation in the openapi.yaml header still stands: the per-type data schemas are inlined rather than $ref'd to docs/schemas/*.schema.json, and nothing yet asserts the two agree. redocly lint checks that this file is a *valid* OpenAPI document; it cannot know that LtestatsData here and ltestats-v1.schema.json there describe the same type. That check is still owed before the contract is frozen — and DL-116, one day earlier, is the evidence that these two sources do drift.

DL-124 — Guard P2 is enforced by the build, because writing it down first was not enough

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · D-12, DL-114, STA-03A, DEVELOPMENT.md §7, DATA-SENSITIVITY-INVENTORY.md §7 · T-066, T-024, T-021

Decision. Three parts.

1. SafeFailure is the only way a failure may enter a stats-writer log line. describe() returns the causal chain of exception type names plus the SQLState when one is present — DataIntegrityViolationException <- SQLException sqlState=23505 — and no message and no stack, ever. SQLState is validated as five alphanumerics before it is included. 2. WriterLogsCarryNoThrowableTest fails the build if any stats-writer main source hands a throwable to a logger. The allow-list is empty and every future entry needs a written reason. 3. The architecture guards' scanned sources are declared as Gradle task inputs.

Why the rule had to be narrowed to be implementable. P2 as adopted said *"log the exception type and message, never the driver's DETAIL: line"*. That cannot be built: for a PSQLException the detail is part of the message, and there is no substring of it that is reliably value-free. Our own exceptions are no safer — nothing stops a processor throwing new PermanentFailureException("uid=… failed the schema"), and T-057 is about to add exactly that kind of throw. So the implemented rule is stricter than the adopted one: no message at all.

What survives is more useful than what was removed. SQLState is the diagnostic that matters — 23505 is a unique violation, 22003 a numeric overflow, 23514 a violated CHECK — and it is a five-character code from the standard, carrying no values by construction. The stack is not lost so much as relocated: the record itself is on the dead-letter queue with its own headers, under whatever access control the DLQ has (ADR-002 §12), rather than under the log-retention policy that does not exist yet.

The correction this entry exists to record. DEVELOPMENT.md §7 said on 4 September that *"stats-writer has no ERROR or WARN path at all until T-024 and T-057"*, and the 5 September status page repeated it as "the rule and the breach arrived together — two statements added by T-024". Both were wrong. There were four sites, and the oldest predated the rule: LorastatsBatchWriter had logged LOGGER.error(…, failure) on the INSERT failure path since T-021 — the precise path P2 names — and PartitionMaintenance had a second. T-024 then added two more by copying the shape, which is exactly how this kind of defect propagates.

Why the Gradle inputs are part of the decision and not housekeeping. The guard passed on its first negative test. It had not run: :stats-common:test was UP-TO-DATE, because Gradle cannot see that the task reads other modules' sources. That was true of StatTypeFanOutTest and VersionIsNotADiscriminatorTest as well, so all three architecture guards were silently skippable on an incremental build — a gate that passes because it never executed, which is DL-100's failure in a different costume. Verified by reintroducing the defect: green and UP-TO-DATE before the input declaration, red after, with no --rerun-tasks.

Scope, deliberately. stats-input still logs broker exceptions with their stacks. An AmqpConnectException carries a host and a port, not a record, and the Writer is the only module holding a database connection and a decoded payload at the same time.

Cost. An unexpected RuntimeException in the Writer now reaches the log as a type chain rather than a stack. That is a real loss of diagnostic detail on the paths where the cause is our own code rather than the database, and it was accepted: the alternative is a rule with an exception in it, and the exception is what the next author copies.

DL-129 — Configuration is audited by the build, because a duplicate key is silent

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · DL-100, DL-116, ADR-002 §12, ADR-003 D6, ADR-007 §1 · T-060, T-025

Decision. Six defects in stats-writer's configuration and in two Gradle files are fixed, and the one that could recur is now enforced by PropertiesDeclareEachKeyOnceTest, which fails the build if any module's application.properties declares a key twice.

Defect Why it mattered
Four retry keys declared twice — tier1/2/3-delay and max-retries, in two separate comment blocks A .properties file silently keeps the last definition. The values happened to agree, so nothing misbehaved; tune the first occurrence and nothing changes, with no warning from the parser, Spring or the IDE
stats.writer.dlq.alert-depth.default=0 read by nothing A key that looks live is one someone edits expecting an effect. Exactly the hazard DL-100 named when it insisted the validation-scope key be called intended-scope — and the payload-gate key was deleted for that reason hours earlier, so leaving this one preserved the inconsistency the deletion was meant to remove
Two wrong DL- citations — T-057 cited DL-125, T-063 cited DL-126, both off by one Introduced by the 8 Sep edit itself. A decision log is only navigable if its citations resolve
The MAX_PRE_CREATE_DAYS comment sat above the cron It documents pre-create-days; as placed it read as a bound on schedule
stats-common/build.gradle.kts called the device field gateway The SRS name that no production record has ever used. ADR-007 §1 makes the wire name the name everywhere, and CLAUDE.md §6 lists this as a trap — the build file was still teaching the wrong one
stats-writer/build.gradle.kts called Flyway "PROVISIONAL pending Q1a" Q1a closed 18 Aug 2026. The same module's application.properties already said "final since Q1a closed", so one module contradicted itself in two files

Why a guard for the duplicate and not for the rest. The other five are one-off staleness, caught by reading. The duplicate is a different species: it is invisible by construction. Nothing in the toolchain reports it, both halves were individually correct, and it survived every review of a file whose own comments insist these values are *"externalised here and NEVER hard-coded"*. That is the profile of a defect that comes back, so it gets a test rather than a note.

The guard needed a second fix to be real. stats-common's test task declared only src/main/java as an input, so editing a properties file left :stats-common:test UP-TO-DATE and the new guard would not have run — the identical trap the existing inputs.files block was written for, one directory across. src/main/resources is now declared too. The guard was then verified the way DL-124's was: the duplicate was reintroduced, the build went red, and it was removed again.

Rejected. *Leaving the duplicate because the values agree* — that is the argument for every latent defect, and it is only true until someone edits one of them. *Renaming alert-depth.default to intended- something* — T-060 will introduce the keys it actually reads; a placeholder that has to be renamed to stay honest is worth less than the comment that replaced it. *A .editorconfig or lint rule* — this repository's convention is a JUnit guard that fails the build (DL-113, DL-124), because that is what runs in CI without anyone opting in.

Consequence. 483 tests. T-060 inherits a file with no misleading threshold in it, and a guard that will fail if its new keys are ever added twice.

DL-130 — Configuration metadata is generated, and eleven keys that bound to nothing are parked

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · DL-100, DL-129, STA-23A, ADR-003, ADR-006 · T-025, T-060, Phase 4

Decision. spring-boot-configuration-processor is added to the three application modules as an annotationProcessor, so each one generates META-INF/spring-configuration-metadata.json from its @ConfigurationProperties classes. Two live keys that are read *by name* rather than bound — stats.writer.stat-type (@Value and Environment) and stats.retention.pre-create-on-startup (@ConditionalOnProperty) — are described in a hand-written additional-spring-configuration-metadata.json, because the processor cannot see either.

Why this is not cosmetic. The symptom was an IDE marking every custom stats.* key as an unknown property. The cost is not the squiggle: it is that a file in which every warning is false is a file where a true warning is never read. And there were true ones — eleven keys that bound to nothing at all:

Module Keys Disposition
stats-writer stats.retention.default-days Parked. STA-23A requires it and 90 days is decided, but RetentionProperties binds only three components and T-025 has not been built
stats-writer delete-batch-size, fallback-schedule, max-batches-per-run, max-run-duration Removed. They configured the batched-DELETE path to take *"if partitioning is not approved"* — and it was approved. ADR-003 makes detach-and-drop the mechanism and CLAUDE.md §6 lists row-by-row deletion at this volume as a trap. They tuned a design the project decided against
stats-input stats.input.metrics.converter-ts-present Removed. A metric name is not configuration: it already exists as IngestMetrics.CONVERTER_TS_PRESENT, so this was a second copy of one truth, free to drift
stats-api stats.security.audience, read-role, tenant-claim, stats.api.default-page-size, max-page-size Parked. No @ConfigurationProperties class exists in that module, so all five resolved to nothing while reading as live security and paging controls. read-role is the worst of them to leave live-looking — it is the control that keeps tenant isolation working

Parking follows this repository's own rule, stated at the top of stats-input/application.properties: *a value that is decided but unread must fail closed (absent) rather than fail open (a plausible-looking guess)*, and stats.retention.lorastats-days was already commented out that way. The decided values stay recorded; they simply stop pretending to be wired.

The fix had a trap of its own, and it failed silently. annotationProcessor is a separate configuration and does not inherit the Boot BOM that the root build imports into implementation and testImplementation. So the version-less catalog entry resolved against nothing: ./gradlew dependencies --configuration annotationProcessor reported FAILED, the processor never ran, no metadata was generated — and the compile still succeeded. The only symptom would have been an IDE that still called every property unknown, i.e. no visible change at all, which is exactly how a fix gets declared done. The root build now adds the platform to annotationProcessor too, and the outcome was verified by reading the generated file rather than by the build going green: 15 described properties in stats-writer, 10 in stats-input, and zero live stats.* keys left undescribed in any module.

Rejected. *Suppressing the warnings in IDE settings* — that hides the false ones and the true ones together, which is the whole problem. *Deleting the parked keys outright* — default-days and the Keycloak values are decided answers, and re-deriving them costs more than a commented line. *Keeping the fallback-DELETE keys "just in case"* — a fallback to a rejected design is a new decision when it is needed, not four keys nobody got round to removing. *A build guard asserting every key has metadata* — the prefixes are not all string literals (LorastatsConsumerProperties uses a constant), so the check would be brittle; with the noise gone the IDE is now itself the feedback loop, which is what DL-129's duplicate-key guard could not rely on.

Consequence. 483 tests, configuration cache still reused. T-025 un-parks default-days; Phase 4 un-parks the five stats-api keys and adds the @ConfigurationProperties classes that make them real.

DL-134 — Record correlation uses standard AMQP properties and scoped MDC

7 Sep 2026, merged 10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · D1, ADR-001, NFR-02, P1/P2, DL-124 · T-005

Decision. Input generates one traceId at the ingest boundary, returns it as X-Trace-Id, and places it in AMQP correlationId; the validated record uid goes in AMQP messageId. Writer restores both before parsing and preserves them on every retry and DLQ copy. The record fields uid, statType, tenant, clsbox and errorCode live in a scoped MDC, while each application log statement owns a bounded event key and each module adds its stable module field through the structured JSON formatter.

Why standard properties. Custom x-stats-* correlation headers would create a second message contract for values AMQP already names. Standard properties are visible to broker tooling, survive hand-off without touching the byte-identical body, and can be restored before a malformed body is parsed.

Why placeholders exist. Size, rate and malformed-JSON failures happen before a record can be trusted. Those events use the bounded literal unknown for unavailable record fields rather than copying unvalidated strings into logs or silently changing the JSON shape.

Enforcement. StructuredLoggingContractTest requires an event field on every application-owned log statement, the stable module formatter setting in all three apps, and no direct body/payload/data expression handed to a logger. Runtime tests assert the actual ECS line, correlation hand-off and context cleanup. P1 and P2 remain stricter: ltestats/netstats payloads never reach a log at any level, and Writer failures use SafeFailure.describe() rather than messages or stacks.

On merge (10 Sep 2026). Two things changed between the branch and main. This entry was written as DL-126, a number main had meanwhile given to the payload gate (T-057); it is renumbered here rather than leaving two entries under one ID — the failure DL-131 had just repaired. And the move to the SLF4J fluent API would have silently switched off WriterLogsCarryNoThrowableTest (DL-124): its regex recognised only log.error("…", e), so log.atError().log("…", e), .addKeyValue("cause", e) and .setCause(e) would all have passed the build while printing the same stack. The guard now walks fluent statements with balanced parentheses and checks each sink's direct arguments, so SafeFailure.describe(failure) still passes and a bare throwable does not; a self-check asserts each shape it claims to catch. Every plain log statement that had landed on main after this branch was cut — T-057's gate, T-025's retention and guard — was converted to carry its event field, which is what StructuredLoggingContractTest requires of it.

DL-135 — Database capability is proved by PostgreSQL, not application convention

8 Sep 2026, merged 10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-32, ADR-006 · T-009

*Written as DL-127, renumbered on merge: main had meanwhile assigned DL-127 to the lorastats dialects (T-063).*

Decision. The PostgreSQL bootstrap explicitly states the stats owner role's Writer/Flyway capabilities and grants stats_ro only CONNECT, schema USAGE, and table SELECT. Default privileges apply both role contracts to tables and partitions created later. stats_ro receives no sequence or temporary-table capability, cannot inherit stats, and there is no stats_input database identity.

Credential boundary. The shared development .env remains the single local input, but Compose overrides database variables to empty in stats-input, removes the Writer credential from stats-api, and removes the API credential from stats-writer. A module therefore receives only the database capability its architecture permits; merely choosing another Spring datasource username cannot recover a stronger secret from the process environment.

Proof. DatabaseRolesIntegrationTest starts PostgreSQL 17 with the exact production init SQL, creates a table after bootstrap, and connects as both identities. stats_ro must read that future table, while INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, and CREATE must each fail in PostgreSQL with SQLSTATE 42501. DatabaseCapabilityIsolationTest guards Input's absent database dependency/configuration and the Compose secret overrides. The failure is consequently a database error, not a code-review promise.

Boundary retained. The official PostgreSQL image creates POSTGRES_USER as its bootstrap/database owner, and PostgreSQL 17 refuses to demote that bootstrap identity in its own init session. Separating a cluster administrator from stats would change the approved one-name image/Writer setup, credential rotation, migration ownership, and existing-volume upgrade procedure; that is a separate platform decision, not silently introduced by T-009.

DL-140 — OpenAPI schema parity is a separate CI gate from OpenAPI lint

16 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-047, STA-30A, DL-117

Decision. CI resolves and compares every inlined per-type OpenAPI data component against the authoritative validation schema in docs/schemas. Documentation-only annotations and OpenAPI integer formats do not define ingest validity and are excluded; required fields, property names, types, patterns, bounds, arrays and additionalProperties remain part of the comparison.

The two non-direct mappings are explicit code, not exceptions hidden in prose. Lorastats' three accepted wire dialects must continue to project to one identical canonical property and required-field set before that canonical shape is compared with LorastatsData. Netstats' JSON Schema patternProperties rule is represented in OpenAPI by the additional-interface schema, while x-interface-name-pattern carries the exact authoritative interface-name expression and is compared byte-for-byte.

Failure proof. The regression suite mutates every one of the five OpenAPI data components and asserts the gate reports drift. Separate mutations prove a change to the authoritative Ltestats validation pattern, divergence between Lorastats dialects and a Netstats interface-pattern change also fail. CI runs these mutation tests and then the real repository comparison before Redocly and Gradle.

Consequence. DL-117 and this gate cover different defects. Redocly answers “is this valid OpenAPI?”; schema parity answers “does this still describe what validation admits and what the API projects?” Passing one is never evidence that the other passed. The known obligation in the OpenAPI header is closed, and the contract can no longer drift from the validation catalogue without a failing push and pull request check.

DL-141 — The Compose stack starts for the first time and finds two latent defects: the images carried no schema catalogue, and the API's healthcheck could never pass

17 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · DL-120, ADR-006, D13, D-10 · T-038

What happened. The three application images had never been built before this date — every earlier run of docker compose used the infrastructure profile only. The first --profile apps up produced two failures that were not in any test because both live in the seam between the Gradle build and the container, which no test exercises.

Defect 1 — the jars shipped without docs/schemas. stats-common's processResources copies docs/schemas/*.schema.json into the jar as schemas/; that is how StatsSchemaValidator finds the catalogue. .dockerignore excluded docs and docker/Dockerfile never COPY'd it, and Gradle's from() on an absent directory copies nothing and says nothing. Every image built and every image failed at the first validator bean — Missing bundled schema resource: schemas/envelope-v1.schema.json — in the Writer, and would have in Input the moment it got its port. The fix is one COPY of the six schema files and a !docs/schemas/*.schema.json exception in .dockerignore. Fixtures and prose stay out of the context; the build runs -x test and does not need them. The general lesson is recorded in the Dockerfile comment: a resource copy that tolerates its source being absent is a build that cannot fail where the mistake is.

Defect 2 — stats-api answered 401 to its own readiness probe. The module carries spring-boot-starter-security and the OAuth2 resource server ahead of T-038, with the issuer URI already bound. Boot's fallback chain for that combination requires a bearer token on every request, /actuator/health/readiness included — the exact endpoint docker-compose.yml health-checks. The container started, ran, and stayed *unhealthy* forever. DL-120 predicted precisely this and kept the starter out of stats-input for that reason; in the API the starter is wanted, so the posture has to be written down rather than inherited.

ApiSecurityConfiguration is the smallest thing that is honestly a posture: the health endpoint (EndpointRequest.to(HealthEndpoint.class) — both probe groups, nothing else under /actuator) is permitAll; every other path is authenticated() behind the JWT resource server; no form login, no HTTP basic, stateless sessions. /actuator/prometheus and /actuator/info stay behind the token — scrape access is a T-038 decision, not a side effect of making a healthcheck green. HealthProbesAreUnauthenticatedTest asserts both halves, and the second half is the one that matters: a carve-out of /actuator/ or / would pass a test that only checked the probes.

What T-038 still owes. The stats.readOnly role, the tenant claim, the 403 rules in application.properties, an issuer health indicator, and the CSRF/405 posture for non-GET verbs (STA-32A). None of that is touched here; the chain is a placeholder with a correct default, not the resource server.

A local-environment note, not a decision. stats-input binds host port 8081, which on the development machine is also OpenProject's. They cannot run at the same time; the port is hard-coded in docker-compose.yml and was left so, because the collision is with a tool on one laptop and not with anything this project deploys.

DL-150 — A Writer log line never repeats a key the delivery's MDC scope owns; the guard fails the build

20 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · NFR-02, ADR-001, DL-114, DL-124, DL-134, T-005, T-066 · found by the same demo

Trigger. Four lines of Appender [CONSOLE] failed to append. java.lang.IllegalStateException: Duplicate nested pairs added under 'uid' in the Writer's console, then silence. Not four dropped lines: logback reports the first four appender failures to its status manager and then stops reporting them, while continuing to drop. Seven statements on the consume path added uid — some also statType and clsbox — with addKeyValue while WriterLogContext (DL-134) already had the same keys in the MDC for the whole delivery. Spring Boot's ECS formatter writes both sets into one JSON object and refuses the duplicate. Every payload_rejected, expired_on_arrival, upstream_status_not_ok and write_failed line the Writer has emitted since T-005 was discarded, with every counter reading correctly and the dead_lettered line — which never repeated a key — arriving normally beside the gap.

Why no test caught it. A test logger has no ECS encoder; the statements "worked" under StructuredLoggingContractTest, which checks for an event key and does not render. The failure exists only under the production appender, in a process nobody reads the logback status of. That is the definition of a rule that has to be enforced where the code is written.

Decision. The six MDC keys — traceId, uid, statType, tenant, clsbox, errorCode — are the scope's to set (WriterLogContext.open, bindRecord, errorCode) and no stats-writer statement adds them as a key-value pair. The seven sites are corrected; the lines lose nothing, because the scope was already carrying the same value. LogsDoNotShadowMdcKeysTest in stats-common's architecture package scans the module: the four delivery-only keys are forbidden everywhere; statType and errorCode are allowed only in the three files that provably run on the scheduler or poll thread with no delivery open (PartitionMaintenance, PartitionRetention, OperationalMetrics), each listed with its reason. EcsEncoderDropsDuplicateKeysTest in stats-writer characterises the mechanism against the real StructuredLogEncoder in ecs format, so the guard's justification is executable: if a Boot release ever stops throwing on a duplicate member, that test fails first and the guard can be reconsidered.

Rejected. *Renaming the pairs* (recordUid) — two names for one value on one line is the drift DL-134 removed. *Customising the formatter to prefer one side* — a formatter that silently resolves duplicates hides the next collision instead of reporting it. *Dropping the MDC in favour of pairs* — the scope exists so that statements in code that never sees the envelope (the retry router, the ACK path) still carry the identifiers.

Residual — closed 22 Sep 2026. stats-input renders ECS too (logging.structured.format.console=ecs), so the guard now covers both modules, each against its own scope's keys (IngestLogContext adds ingestSource). The rule was also sharpened: it is about the *thread*, not the key. A file that provably never runs under the scope — InputAmqpConfiguration's ReturnsCallback on the broker connection thread, the Writer's scheduler and poll threads — may add any of the keys, because there a pair is the only way to say which record the line is about; every other file adds none. The earlier two-tier key split (four keys forbidden everywhere, two allowed off-thread) was replaced by that single rule. Renamed LogsDoNotShadowMdcKeysTest; Input had no offending site, so no Input log line changed.

Phase two

The LoRa reference slice

One type end to end, then hardened through the September amendments

2.1 Match the production record

The production envelope uses uid, tenant, maintenant, clsbox, type, version, ts and data, with supported optional status and metadata. gateway and ts.processor from older documents became clsbox and ts.server. Timestamps use Unix seconds. version is preserved metadata, not a parser dispatch switch.

The decision

The production wire format is the contract

Decision. Use clsbox and ts.server; preserve version without using it to select a parser.

Why. The original SRS field names did not match Heimdal records.

Effect. ADR 007 supersedes the old names; the architecture guard prevents accidental version based dispatch.

DL-085 · DL-087 · DL-113

Input validates the envelope; Writer validates the payload against the type's schema. This keeps HTTP acceptance focused on a structurally valid routed record while payload processing belongs to the type specific writer. Consequently, a successfully accepted HTTP record may later fail Writer validation and enter its DLQ. We preserve the inbound message bytes across the Input to broker handoff; database representation is normalised later.

The decision

Two validation gates with different consequences

Decision. Input checks the envelope and publishes unchanged bytes; Writer checks the type payload.

Why. A payload failure after acceptance must leave a record available for investigation, rather than silently disappear.

Effect. A valid envelope can receive HTTP 200 and later enter its type DLQ if Writer rejects the payload.

DL-050 · DL-088 · DL-101 · DL-126

Unknown data members are accepted, retained in data_extra and metered. Invalid envelope fields are rejected according to the envelope schema. Optional device measurements remain optional when real gateways omit them. The database keeps typed fields for efficient reads while passthrough preserves supported envelope values and their original shapes. API reconstruction omits absent values rather than inventing null fields.

Three known LoRa dialects normalise to a common row; an unknown fourth dialect is a permanent failure. System statistics have their own numeric string rules; OMS payload counters are integers. One WireNumbers helper checks range and spelling before narrowing numbers, and all conversion refusals are permanent mapping failures. A discovered LoRa overflow previously retried for 35 minutes 30 seconds; unifying the helper made it dead letter on the first attempt like other unmappable payloads.

ts.device is genuine measurement time. ts.server is the canonical arrival clock. Input rejects a device clock more than the configured five minute tolerance ahead of server time, but accepts old device times so genuine outage replays reach the late arrival policy. ts.converter is accepted and preserved without timestamp correction. Rejecting it would have rejected real OMS traffic. Heimdal's timestamp correction flag is observed and metered; Stats App does not perform that upstream correction itself.

The decision

Preserve converter time and accept genuine old measurements

Decision. Do not transform device timestamps using ts.converter. Reject excessive future skew at Input, and apply retention to old records in Writer.

Why. Real OMS records carry converter time, and genuine offline device measurements may arrive weeks late.

Effect. The converter remains in passthrough; future skew is a 400 and old data follows the signed late arrival policy.

DL-052 · DL-086 · DL-106

Non OK upstream status is retained and metered, not automatically dead lettered. _id is a Mongo export artifact and is not a wire field. IPv6 is not admitted by the current network schema; rejection is visible and is the trigger to revisit that decision. Schema fixtures and parity checks protect these distinctions across Java validation and the published OpenAPI representation.

2.2 Prove one type from acceptance to storage

Every handoff has a clear success condition

Input uses mandatory publishing, a return callback and publisher confirms. HTTP success follows a confirmed routed publish; an unavailable broker, returned message or failed confirm yields 503. A schema error yields 400; request size and rate controls yield 413 and 429. The configured limit is 256 KB per request and 4,000 requests per second per Input instance. These are engineering defaults and must be sized with servlet capacity.

RabbitMQ has a durable quorum main queue, three retry queues and one DLQ per implemented stat type. Separate queues prevent a poison record of one type from blocking others. Writer ACKs after database commit. For a failed delivery, it confirms the retry or DLQ copy before acknowledging the original. If that handoff cannot be confirmed, the original remains available for requeue. Unknown failures default to retry; known permanent validation or mapping failures go directly to DLQ.

The decision

Confirm every handoff before forgetting the original

Decision. Use mandatory publishing and confirms; confirm retry or DLQ copies before ACKing failed deliveries.

Why. A missing route or failed copy would otherwise be reported as successful handling.

Effect. Broker failures cause visible backpressure; the original survives when a replacement cannot be confirmed.

DL-033 · DL-118 · DL-122

The decision · 6 October 2026

Show acceptance explicitly in the ingest response

Decision. After RabbitMQ confirms publication, both ingest doors return JSON {"status":"accepted","traceId":"…"}. The body trace ID matches X-Trace-Id; the configured success code remains 200 or 202.

Why. A visible acknowledgement makes successful ingest clear in Postman and carries the existing correlation identifier.

Effect. Acceptance describes the broker handoff. Writer persistence remains asynchronous; validation failures and publish failures still return Problem Details. The v4 bundle includes this change; the original v3 archive returns an empty body.

DL-163

Retry delays are externalised at 30 seconds, 5 minutes and 30 minutes. DLQs have no TTL and no onward route, preserving the last copy and avoiding an infinite retry cycle. Manual authorised redrive gets one processing attempt, not a new full retry budget. Graceful shutdown finishes the in hand delivery when possible and returns unfinished work to the broker; shutdown interruption is lifecycle, not record failure.

The decision

A DLQ is the last copy and must not expire

Decision. Give every type its own durable queues and a DLQ with no TTL or onward route.

Why. Automatic expiry destroys recovery evidence; an onward route can loop indefinitely.

Effect. Operators investigate and use guarded redrive; one poison type does not block another.

DL-030 · DL-031 · DL-032 · DL-034 · DL-138

Duplicates are harmless and visible

Each type table uses PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid). PostgreSQL's partitioned uniqueness rules require the partition key, and ON CONFLICT suppresses a repeated record. Suppression is metered separately from persistence. Tenant is deliberately not in this key, following the selected identity contract; a colliding same time and UID under another tenant is therefore suppressed rather than stored as a second identity. Tests cover that consequence. Individual inserts ship first behind an interface that can later support batches.

The decision

Duplicate identity includes measurement time

Decision. Use (ts_device, uid) as the primary key and meter suppressed conflicts.

Why. PostgreSQL partitioned uniqueness requires the partition key, and replays must not create another row.

Effect. Tenant is not in this key. A colliding timestamp and UID under another tenant is also suppressed and explicitly tested.

DL-043 · DL-044 · DL-048

Retention is a partition operation

Daily range partitions use measurement time. Default retention is 90 days, with type overrides available. At the projected volume, row by row deletion would create a continuous vacuum and bloat workload. Detaching and dropping an expired day removes it as a catalogue operation and aligns time range queries with partition pruning.

The decision

Delete whole days instead of individual rows

Decision. Partition daily on device time and detach then drop expired days.

Why. At the expected volume, row deletion creates continuous dead tuples, vacuum debt and index bloat.

Effect. Retention and query pruning use the same time key; Writer owns maintenance behind advisory locks.

DL-042 · DL-045 · DL-132

Writer creates every partition from the retention cutoff through seven days ahead. The earlier ahead only policy failed on legitimate historic data; whole window creation fixed it. Startup creation is fatal if it fails and completes before consumption begins. Per type advisory locks prevent replicas from running conflicting maintenance; losing the lock skips the pass and remains observable.

The decision

Precreate the entire retained window

Decision. Ensure every retained day plus the configured seven days ahead exists before consuming.

Why. Ahead only creation left valid historic records without a partition after deployment or a missed run.

Effect. The 20 September amendment repairs those gaps without changing the late arrival cutoff.

DL-096 · DL-149

Every retention pass repairs pending concurrent detach operations, then reclaims detached orphan tables before continuing. A crash between detach and drop otherwise leaves storage permanently stranded, while an unfinished detach can block every later retention run without breaking ingestion. Writer and retention share one cutoff function, so they cannot disagree about which days exist.

The signed late arrival policy skips a record older than that cutoff, increments records_expired_on_arrival, ACKs it and alerts on sustained occurrence. This is deliberate data loss outside the accepted window, not a hidden insertion failure. The caller's prior HTTP success cannot promise indefinite storage, which is why the policy and its alert are explicit.

The decision

Expired on arrival is an approved outcome

Decision. Skip records older than the shared retention cutoff, count them, ACK and alert on sustained occurrence.

Why. Creating old partitions indefinitely would defeat retention; hiding a skipped record would conceal loss.

Effect. Signed on 4 September and built on 10 September. The expiry guard and sweep use the same cutoff.

DL-083 · DL-112 · DL-133

The reference slice decisions

Expand a dated record for the complete rationale, alternatives, consequences and verification. These records belong to this phase. Later amendments remain visible in their historical context.

DL-030 — One durable queue per stat type; never a shared queue

Head-of-line blocking. On a shared queue, one poison netstats message stalls lorastats, ltestats, sysstats and omsstats behind it — five types sharing one failure domain and one scaling knob. Per-type queues also make consumer concurrency, prefetch and replica count independently tunable, which STA-11A/20A/26A require.

13 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-002, STA-10A

Decision. app_stats_input topic exchange, routing key = the exact stat type literal, and a dedicated durable main queue app_statsstore.<type> per type.

Why. Head-of-line blocking. On a shared queue, one poison netstats message stalls lorastats, ltestats, sysstats and omsstats behind it — five types sharing one failure domain and one scaling knob. Per-type queues also make consumer concurrency, prefetch and replica count independently tunable, which STA-11A/20A/26A require.

Rejected. One app_statsstore queue as the implementation brief named — shared failure domain and shared bottleneck, and it conflicts with STA-10/20. Input writing PostgreSQL directly — destroys the buffer and the single-writer architecture.

Consequence. There must be a test proving a poison message in one type does not delay another. Without it this decision is only an intention.

DL-031 — Three retry tiers plus a DLQ, all delays externalised

Three tiers separate three genuinely different failures: a momentary blip (30 s), a short dependency outage (5 min), and a longer one (30 min). A single tier forces one delay to serve all three, so it is either too aggressive for the outage or too slow for the blip. Immediate requeue for a persistent failure is a hot loop that burns broker and database capacity while achieving nothing.

18 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · Gates G1/G4, ADR-002

Decision. app_statsstore.<type>.retry.<tier> for tiers 1–3 and app_statsstore.<type>.dlq, sharing the main queue's prefix. Delays and the maximum attempt count live in application.properties — starting values 30 s / 5 min / 30 min. Immediate requeue is never used for a persistent failure.

Why. Three tiers separate three genuinely different failures: a momentary blip (30 s), a short dependency outage (5 min), and a longer one (30 min). A single tier forces one delay to serve all three, so it is either too aggressive for the outage or too slow for the blip. Immediate requeue for a persistent failure is a hot loop that burns broker and database capacity while achieving nothing.

Externalising the delays matters because the right values are an operational finding, not a design one — they will be tuned against a real incident, and a hard-coded value makes that a rebuild.

Rejected. One retry queue (the original position) — cannot express the three cases. A backoff multiplier — ADR-006 fixes three explicit tiers so the delay a message will experience is readable from configuration rather than computed.

Consequence. Adding a type adds four queues. All names derive from StatType.wireName() so it cannot leave a partially enumerated set behind.

DL-032 — Quorum queues for main, retry, and dead-letter

The dev and target broker is RabbitMQ 4, where classic mirrored queues no longer exist — quorum is *the* replicated durable queue type. Declaring messages persistent (STA-13A) means little if the queue holding them does not survive a node loss. Quorum supports both mechanisms the retry design depends on: queue-level x-message-ttl for tier delays and x-dead-letter-exchange for the bounce back.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-002 §8

Decision. Every queue declares x-queue-type: quorum.

Why. The dev and target broker is RabbitMQ 4, where classic mirrored queues no longer exist — quorum is *the* replicated durable queue type. Declaring messages persistent (STA-13A) means little if the queue holding them does not survive a node loss. Quorum supports both mechanisms the retry design depends on: queue-level x-message-ttl for tier delays and x-dead-letter-exchange for the bounce back.

Rejected. Classic queues — not replicated, and on RabbitMQ 4 the mirroring that used to compensate is gone. Streams — built for replay-from-offset consumption, not per-message ack with dead-lettering.

Consequence. Quorum queues hold more state resident; a synchronised burst leaves roughly 12,000 messages per type queued (Q13a), which is comfortable — and if measurement disagrees the lever is x-max-in-memory-length, not a change of queue type. A queue's type is fixed at declaration: changing one means deleting and redeclaring it, and a mismatched redeclaration is the RESOURCE_LOCKED startup failure.

DL-033 — Publish with mandatory=true and a ReturnsCallback

A publisher confirm does not close this hole. A confirm says the *broker* accepted the message, not that any queue wanted it. If a binding is missing — a new stat type deployed before its queue, a typo in a routing key, a half-applied topology change — the broker ACKs the publish and drops the message on the floor. Input would return 200, Heimdal would mark it delivered and never retry, and the record would be gone with every component reporting success.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-002 §9

Decision. Input publishes with the mandatory flag and registers a ReturnsCallback. A returned message is a failed acceptance: 503, records_unroutable{type} incremented, ERROR logged with the routing key.

Why. A publisher confirm does not close this hole. A confirm says the *broker* accepted the message, not that any queue wanted it. If a binding is missing — a new stat type deployed before its queue, a typo in a routing key, a half-applied topology change — the broker ACKs the publish and drops the message on the floor. Input would return 200, Heimdal would mark it delivered and never retry, and the record would be gone with every component reporting success.

Rejected. Confirms alone — precisely the gap above. Treating a return as a warning — the message is already gone; a warning makes the loss quiet rather than absent.

Consequence. There must be a test that publishes to a routing key with no bound queue and asserts 503 plus the counter. A test asserting only the confirm would pass while the record is silently discarded.

DL-034 — The DLQ carries no TTL and no onward route — enforced by the type system

A TTL'd dead-letter queue is silent data loss. A message that reached the DLQ has already survived three retry tiers — it is the only remaining copy of a record Heimdal believes it delivered and will never resend. Deleting it on a timer violates rule 9 outright. A DLQ that dead-letters *onward* is the same failure wearing a different name.

29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-002 §12, AmqpTopology

Decision. deadLetterQueueArguments() returns exactly {x-queue-type: quorum} and has no parameter to set a TTL or a dead-letter exchange with.

Why. A TTL'd dead-letter queue is silent data loss. A message that reached the DLQ has already survived three retry tiers — it is the only remaining copy of a record Heimdal believes it delivered and will never resend. Deleting it on a timer violates rule 9 outright. A DLQ that dead-letters *onward* is the same failure wearing a different name.

The design move worth noting is where this is enforced. It is not a comment and not a review checklist item: the method takes no arguments, so the policy is *unrepresentable* rather than merely documented. Bounding broker disk is an alerting and disposition problem — alert on depth > 0 and oldest-message age, manual disposition within 14 days.

Rejected. A long TTL "as a safety valve" — a safety valve that discards the last copy of the data is not one. Documenting the rule and trusting review — the next person adding a TTL would be doing something that looks entirely reasonable.

Consequence. Redrive is manual, by an authorised operations role, explicit per type, capped at 2× nominal, root cause first. And the trap for the runbook: a message that sat in the DLQ long enough becomes expired_on_arrival the moment it is redriven, because its ts.device has aged past the retention window. A redrive after ~90 days is a no-op that increments a counter — operators must know this before attempting one, or a failed recovery looks like a successful one.

DL-035 — Only broker-connected modules declare topology; stats-api has no broker connection

ZC's answer read *"every module have to declare the exchanges, queues and bindings."* Taken literally, that hands the read-only API a broker dependency it has no use for, and adds a third declaring module to a set where any argument mismatch is a RESOURCE_LOCKED startup failure rather than a warning. Read as *every broker-connected module*, the answer and the service boundaries agree.

29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-002 §11 (closes Q3a)

Decision. stats-input and stats-writer declare the topology, from the shared stats-common constants, identically and idempotently. stats-api holds no RabbitMQ connection at all.

Why. ZC's answer read *"every module have to declare the exchanges, queues and bindings."* Taken literally, that hands the read-only API a broker dependency it has no use for, and adds a third declaring module to a set where any argument mismatch is a RESOURCE_LOCKED startup failure rather than a warning. Read as *every broker-connected module*, the answer and the service boundaries agree.

Rejected. Literal compliance — a broker dependency in the API means its readiness probe, its failure modes and its restart behaviour all now include a system it never talks to. Waiting for a platform sign-off — T-032, the declaration-conflict test, proves at build time what a sign-off would only assert in prose.

Consequence. stats-api starting with no broker connection is itself an assertion. The platform acknowledgement is a courtesy; do not wait on it, and do not give the API a broker connection in the meantime.

DL-040 — PostgreSQL 17, one table per stat type

Answered, not deferred — the v1.0 SRS said MongoDB and the answer set changed it. One table per type follows from the same isolation argument as the per-type queues, and it is what makes DL-041's typed columns possible: a shared table would force jsonb or a union of every type's columns.

24 Aug 2026 · ANSWERED + RATIFIED · ADR-003, STA-21A

Decision. PostgreSQL 17 is the only V1 persistence technology. One table per stat type; types are never mixed.

Why. Answered, not deferred — the v1.0 SRS said MongoDB and the answer set changed it. One table per type follows from the same isolation argument as the per-type queues, and it is what makes DL-041's typed columns possible: a shared table would force jsonb or a union of every type's columns.

Rejected. MongoDB — decided against; do not introduce drivers, MongoRepository, TTL indexes, or the words "collection" and "document". A shared table with a type discriminator — kills typed columns and makes adding a type a change to existing types (STA-14A).

Consequence. Five tables, five sets of partitions, five migrations. Adding a type is additive across all of it.

DL-041 — Typed columns for four types; hybrid jsonb for netstats

jsonb stores every key name in every row. lorastats has 13 keys averaging ~14 characters — roughly 180 bytes per row of nothing but repeated key strings, on the order of 375 GB per type of pure overhead at steady state. Typed columns eliminate it and are directly indexable.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D2

Decision. Typed columns for lorastats, ltestats, sysstats, omsstats. For netstats, typed columns for the stable fields and jsonb for the interface map.

Why. jsonb stores every key name in every row. lorastats has 13 keys averaging ~14 characters — roughly 180 bytes per row of nothing but repeated key strings, on the order of 375 GB per type of pure overhead at steady state. Typed columns eliminate it and are directly indexable.

netstats is the exception on purpose, and the reason survives Q4a being answered. Its schema is deliberately open at both levels: any interface name is legal (eth1, ppp0), and unknown fields on an interface are accepted and preserved. Fixed eth0_* / wwan_* columns would silently drop an interface the schema permits — directly violating the D14 unknown-field policy. A jsonb map preserves them by construction, and no migration against two billion rows is needed the first time a gateway reports eth1.

Rejected. jsonb everywhere — the 375 GB/type overhead and JSON path scans on the dominant filters. Typed columns everywhere including netstats — silent data loss on any interface not anticipated.

Consequence. Counters are BIGINT, not INTEGER — the SRS says "integer", but these are monotonic counters over long device uptimes and int4 overflow is silent corruption; bigint costs four bytes. And because the schema's digit pattern caps at 19 digits, which is wider than signed BIGINT — a gap not expressible as a regex — the Writer must range-check before conversion (STA-04C). An unchecked overflow corrupts the value with no error anywhere.

DL-042 — Daily RANGE (ts_device) partitions; retention is detach-and-drop

At 115.2M records/day, retention is not a periodic cleanup — it is a second write path running permanently at full ingestion rate (267 rows/sec/type). Row-by-row deletion would generate ~115 million dead tuples per day across tables of ~2 billion rows each, which autovacuum will not keep up with: table and index bloat, lengthening deletes, degrading writer. Dropping a partition is a catalog operation — milliseconds, no dead tuples, no vacuum debt.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D1

Decision. Every stat-type table is PARTITION BY RANGE (ts_device) with daily partitions. Retention is DETACH PARTITION CONCURRENTLY then DROP TABLE — not row deletion.

Why. At 115.2M records/day, retention is not a periodic cleanup — it is a second write path running permanently at full ingestion rate (267 rows/sec/type). Row-by-row deletion would generate ~115 million dead tuples per day across tables of ~2 billion rows each, which autovacuum will not keep up with: table and index bloat, lengthening deletes, degrading writer. Dropping a partition is a catalog operation — milliseconds, no dead tuples, no vacuum debt.

Daily granularity gives 90 live partitions per type, 450 across five — comfortably within what the PG17 planner handles. Hourly gives 10,800 and buys nothing.

The alignment worth protecting: the retention basis and the dominant query pattern are *both* ts_device, so one partition key serves both — retention is a drop, and time-ranged queries prune partitions. Do not give that up lightly.

Rejected. Batched deletion — properties remain defined for a fallback path only. Monthly partitions — a 90-day window would drop three months at a time.

Consequence. Partitions must be pre-created ahead of ingestion or inserts fail (DL-027).

DL-043 — PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid), in that column order

Two hard constraints and one preference. A global unique index is not creatable on a partitioned table, so per-partition scoping is not a choice. ts_device must lead so the key includes the partition key and matches the mandatory (ts_device DESC, uid DESC) sort. Rule 13 originally said (uid, ts_device), which describes an object PostgreSQL cannot create here.

18 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · Gate G2, ADR-003, STA-21B

Decision. PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid) — ts_device first — scoped per partition.

Why. Two hard constraints and one preference. A global unique index is not creatable on a partitioned table, so per-partition scoping is not a choice. ts_device must lead so the key includes the partition key and matches the mandatory (ts_device DESC, uid DESC) sort. Rule 13 originally said (uid, ts_device), which describes an object PostgreSQL cannot create here.

Per-partition scoping is still sufficient, and this is the subtle part: a redelivered record carries the same ts_device, so it lands in the same partition and collides with the same key. That guarantee comes from ADR-004 — publication is byte-identical, so nothing rewrites a timestamp in flight.

Rejected. (uid, ts_device) — wrong order for both the partition key and the sort. Including tenant in the key — deliberately excluded, see DL-044.

Consequence. This decision is load-bearing on ADR-004. If anything ever transforms a record in flight, per-partition duplicate suppression stops working and the failure is silent. Check any future in-flight transformation proposal against this first. A secondary (tenant, uid) index carries the uid-only lookup, which cannot prune partitions.

DL-044 — Duplicate suppression is metered, never silent; tenant is out of the key

At-least-once delivery means duplicates are normal, not exceptional — a replay is duplicates by definition. Suppressing them without a counter would violate the no-silent-error-handling rule, and it would hide something worse: because tenant is deliberately not in the key, a cross-tenant uid collision would be absorbed as a duplicate. The counter is the only thing that would ever reveal one.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-005, STA-27A

Decision. INSERT … ON CONFLICT (ts_device, uid) DO NOTHING. The writer reads the affected-row count and increments records_duplicate_suppressed{type} on zero.

Why. At-least-once delivery means duplicates are normal, not exceptional — a replay is duplicates by definition. Suppressing them without a counter would violate the no-silent-error-handling rule, and it would hide something worse: because tenant is deliberately not in the key, a cross-tenant uid collision would be absorbed as a duplicate. The counter is the only thing that would ever reveal one.

The ON CONFLICT form is safe without a field-by-field comparison only because ADR-004 guarantees byte-identical publication (DL-043).

Rejected. ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE — rewrites a row with identical content and generates a dead tuple per duplicate. Including tenant in the key — makes a cross-tenant collision invisible instead of merely rare.

Consequence. Replay spikes this counter by design — do not alert on the count alone. A sustained non-replay increase is the cross-tenant signal, and if it ever appears, tenant goes into the key.

DL-045 — stats-writer owns retention and pre-creation, behind a per-type advisory lock

The writer is the only component permitted to write (rule 2) — giving retention to anything else creates a second writer. But horizontal writer scaling is a MUST (STA-06/26/39), so with three replicas you would otherwise get three concurrent retention jobs racing on the same partitions. pg_try_advisory_lock is the boring solution and adds no dependency.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D3

Decision. stats-writer owns partition creation and retention, guarded by pg_try_advisory_lock(hashtext('stats.retention:' || :statType)). A replica that does not acquire the lock skips the run and logs at DEBUG. The five types are staggered, not fired together.

Why. The writer is the only component permitted to write (rule 2) — giving retention to anything else creates a second writer. But horizontal writer scaling is a MUST (STA-06/26/39), so with three replicas you would otherwise get three concurrent retention jobs racing on the same partitions. pg_try_advisory_lock is the boring solution and adds no dependency.

Rejected. ShedLock or Quartz — both add a dependency and a schema for something one PostgreSQL built-in already does. A dedicated maintenance deployment (the ARD's proposal) — adds a deployment to operate and still needs the same lock; keep the lock, drop the instance (ADR-002 §10).

Consequence. Retention correctness now depends on the lock being taken on *every* path that touches partitions, pre-creation included.

DL-046 — schema_version is stored on every row; ingested_at is not added

Adding a column to a ~2 billion row table is genuinely expensive, and the moment a schema changes — which STA-14A guarantees will happen — the first question of any backfill is *which rows were written under which rules*. Without this column that question is answered by joining against deployment dates, which is to say it is not answered. Two bytes now against a table rewrite later is not a close call. NOT NULL with no default, so a row can never claim a version nothing verified.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D7

Decision. Add schema_version smallint NOT NULL, set from the schema that actually validated the record. Reject the ARD's ingested_at as a rename of the existing received_at.

Why. Adding a column to a ~2 billion row table is genuinely expensive, and the moment a schema changes — which STA-14A guarantees will happen — the first question of any backfill is *which rows were written under which rules*. Without this column that question is answered by joining against deployment dates, which is to say it is not answered. Two bytes now against a table rewrite later is not a close call. NOT NULL with no default, so a row can never claim a version nothing verified.

Rejected. Deriving the version from received_at and a deployment log — approximate, and wrong across a rolling deploy. ingested_at — the same value as received_at under a different name; a rename buys nothing and costs a migration.

Consequence. The Writer must pass through which schema validated the record, so validation and persistence are coupled by one small contract.

DL-047 — One Writer image, deployed once per stat type

Per-type consumer concurrency, prefetch and replica count are a MUST (STA-11A, STA-20A, STA-26A) and are only independently tunable if the instances are separate. It also means a crash loop caused by one type's payload cannot take down consumption of the other four — the per-type queue isolation argument carried one layer up.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D8

Decision. stats-writer stays a single Docker image, deployed once per stat type, with STAT_TYPE selecting the queue. stats.writer.stat-type=${STAT_TYPE:all} — all is a development convenience only; production sets exactly one.

Why. Per-type consumer concurrency, prefetch and replica count are a MUST (STA-11A, STA-20A, STA-26A) and are only independently tunable if the instances are separate. It also means a crash loop caused by one type's payload cannot take down consumption of the other four — the per-type queue isolation argument carried one layer up.

Rejected. A fourth image per type — Q2 fixed three images; this is a deployment topology, not a new artefact. One instance consuming all five in production — loses both the tuning and the isolation.

Consequence. Five writer deployments to operate. The all default is the one place this could go wrong in production, which is why it is called out here and in the properties file.

DL-048 — Individual inserts first, behind a batch-shaped interface

The two positions were never really opposed. D11 wanted individual writes to preserve per-record DLQ precision — a batch that fails wholesale cannot say which record was poison. The single-record fallback preserves exactly that. Building individual first behind the batch-shaped interface makes batching a benchmarked tuning change against real numbers, rather than a rewrite — and avoids optimising throughput before anything has been measured.

18 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · Gate G3 (amends D11)

Decision. Bounded micro-batching (100–500 records or 50–200 ms, whichever first) with single-record fallback is the target. Build individual inserts first, behind a batch-shaped interface.

Why. The two positions were never really opposed. D11 wanted individual writes to preserve per-record DLQ precision — a batch that fails wholesale cannot say which record was poison. The single-record fallback preserves exactly that. Building individual first behind the batch-shaped interface makes batching a benchmarked tuning change against real numbers, rather than a rewrite — and avoids optimising throughput before anything has been measured.

Rejected. Batching from the start — optimises before measuring and risks losing per-record DLQ precision. Individual writes permanently — will not hold the drain rate.

Consequence. The insert interface must be batch-shaped from the first commit. Getting this wrong is cheap now and expensive later.

DL-050 — No transformation: the published message is byte-identical to what was received

The v1.0 SRS describes an omsstats-specific ts.converter field and implies normalisation. That description is out of date — Heimdal already emits the canonical ts.server for omsstats exactly as for every other type, so there is nothing to normalise.

Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-004 · amended 29 Aug 2026 by ADR-007 §2

Decision. The Stats App performs no timestamp normalisation and no transformation of any kind. What is published to RabbitMQ is byte-identical to what arrived over HTTP.

Why. The v1.0 SRS describes an omsstats-specific ts.converter field and implies normalisation. That description is out of date — Heimdal already emits the canonical ts.server for omsstats exactly as for every other type, so there is nothing to normalise.

The consequence is larger than the decision. Byte-identical publication is what makes ADR-003's per-partition duplicate suppression work: a redelivered or replayed record carries the same ts_device, lands in the same partition, and collides with the same (ts_device, uid) key. If Input ever rewrote a timestamp, duplicate suppression would stop working and the failure would be silent.

Rejected. *Normalising* on ts.converter — deriving, rewriting or back-filling any timestamp from it. That is still explicitly out of scope and reopening it requires a new approved compatibility ADR. This is the half of ADR-004 that survives; it is not the same as rejecting the field.

AMENDED 29 August 2026 — ADR-007 §2 withdrew the rejection clause. This entry previously read: *"A record carrying ts.converter is invalid — 400, nothing published,"* implemented as "not": {"required": ["converter"]} on ts. The 29 Aug production sample carries ts.converter on 100% of records, so that rule rejected every record Heimdal sends. It is gone from envelope-v1.schema.json and must not be reinstated.

Consequence, as it now stands. ts.converter is a valid passthrough member — accepted, stored in the passthrough jsonb, returned verbatim, and never read. Its presence is metered as records_converter_ts_present{type} (T-059) so the day Heimdal stops sending it is visible rather than inferred. Accepting the field is not the same as acting on it: no code anywhere may branch on ts.converter, which is what keeps byte-identical publication — and therefore duplicate suppression — intact. See DL-088 and ADR-007 §2.

DL-051 — Unknown fields: accept and preserve in data, reject invalid envelope fields

The two halves serve different risks. data is the type-specific payload and it will grow — Q4a explicitly confirmed unanticipated netstats interfaces are legal — so rejecting unknown members there would break on Heimdal's next additive change, and *dropping* them silently would lose data we were told to expect. The envelope is a contract of seven fields with security meaning (tenant) and storage meaning (ts.device); an unrecognised field there is a signal that the sender and this system disagree about the contract.

24 Aug 2026 · ENGINEERING · decision D14 (renamed from D6)

Decision. Unknown fields inside data are accepted and preserved. Envelope fields are validated strictly.

Why. The two halves serve different risks. data is the type-specific payload and it will grow — Q4a explicitly confirmed unanticipated netstats interfaces are legal — so rejecting unknown members there would break on Heimdal's next additive change, and *dropping* them silently would lose data we were told to expect. The envelope is a contract of seven fields with security meaning (tenant) and storage meaning (ts.device); an unrecognised field there is a signal that the sender and this system disagree about the contract.

The renaming is itself part of the decision. This was labelled D6, but answer-set D6 already means byte-identical publication and ADR-003's D6 is the migration tool. Three meanings for one label is the failure ARD-ADR-nn was introduced to fix.

Rejected. Whatever Jackson happens to do by default — this is a real decision about data loss and contract drift, not a deserialiser setting. Strict everywhere — breaks on the first additive Heimdal change.

Consequence. data is a JsonNode (DL-020), and netstats interfaces are jsonb (DL-041). Both follow from this entry.

DL-052 — Clock skew is rejected at Input, in code, not in the schema

A ts.device in the future is not a plausible reading — it is a misconfigured clock, and the record it produces lands in a future partition where retention will never reach it. It has to be caught somewhere, and Input is the only place that can tell the sender.

29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-003 D9 (closes Q14a)

Decision. A record whose ts.device exceeds ts.server + stats.input.validation.max-clock-skew (default PT5M) is rejected at Input with 400 and the field code clock_skew_exceeded, metered as records_clock_skew_rejected{type}. Anything inside the tolerance is accepted.

Why. A ts.device in the future is not a plausible reading — it is a misconfigured clock, and the record it produces lands in a future partition where retention will never reach it. It has to be caught somewhere, and Input is the only place that can tell the sender.

Why *code* and not the schema: the check compares two fields, and JSON Schema cannot express a cross-field relation. That is also why the five edge/<type>-clock-skew-future.json fixtures stay schema-valid and carry $expect: "input-reject" — they are testing the Input rule, not the schema.

The tolerance exists because a few seconds of NTP drift is normal and must not be an outage. It is externalised, never hard-coded (the G4 precedent).

Rejected. Accepting and letting it land in a future partition — silent unbounded retention. Rejecting at the Writer — the sender has already been told 200 and will never retry. A schema rule — not expressible.

Consequence. This was filed as a question for someone else for weeks. It was never a product question, which is the note worth keeping: check whether a blocker is actually yours before waiting on it.

DL-053 — Validation errors never echo the offending value

Validator messages embed the value that failed. That value is attacker-controlled and is about to be placed in a response body — reflecting it turns the validator into a reflection gadget, and at minimum leaks internal schema detail to whoever is probing. The SafeMessage enum makes safety a type constraint rather than a review habit: constructing an unreviewed message is not possible.

24 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · STA-03A, StatsSchemaValidator, FieldError

Decision. Only JSON Pointers, machine-readable codes and reviewed SafeMessage constants cross the public boundary. NetworkNT's own messages are discarded. FieldError has no public constructor and accepts message text only through the SafeMessage enum. StatType.fromWireName throws "Unknown stat type literal" without the literal.

Why. Validator messages embed the value that failed. That value is attacker-controlled and is about to be placed in a response body — reflecting it turns the validator into a reflection gadget, and at minimum leaks internal schema detail to whoever is probing. The SafeMessage enum makes safety a type constraint rather than a review habit: constructing an unreviewed message is not possible.

Rejected. Sanitising the value before echoing — a filter that must be right every time, versus a design where the unsafe path does not exist. Passing NetworkNT messages through — convenient, and exactly the leak.

Consequence. Debugging a rejected record needs the JSON Pointer plus the code, not a quoted value. That trade is deliberate.

DL-063 — Input authentication is deferred by decision, behind a config switch with two guards

ZC could not yet say whether inbound auth is mTLS or OAuth2 client credentials. Blocking Input entirely would have stalled the reference vertical slice on a question that only changes a filter implementation. Structuring it as a mode makes the eventual answer a filter plus a config change, never a rewrite of the endpoint.

18 Aug 2026 · ANSWERED (Q8a) · ADR-006, stats-input/application.properties

Decision. Build Input without Heimdal auth. Auth is a config-selected filter: stats.input.auth.mode=none|mtls|oauth2, with no default in code, a WARN on every startup when none, and a test asserting the application refuses to start under the prod profile with none.

Why. ZC could not yet say whether inbound auth is mTLS or OAuth2 client credentials. Blocking Input entirely would have stalled the reference vertical slice on a question that only changes a filter implementation. Structuring it as a mode makes the eventual answer a filter plus a config change, never a rewrite of the endpoint.

The guards are the substance. No default in code means a deployment that does not set it fails to start — none has to be typed by a person. The prod-profile test is what actually stops it reaching production; the WARN is what stops it becoming invisible in dev.

Rejected. A none default — the missing-configuration case would silently become the insecure case. Blocking Phase 2 — stalls the slice on a filter.

Consequence. Risk accepted and recorded: until Q8a-2 closes, anything that can reach the Input port can write statistics. Acceptable on a local dev stack and nowhere else. Re-read this before any shared deployment.

DL-083 — Skipping a late-arriving record is deliberate data loss, and is alerted

No live partition exists for such a record, so the alternative is writing into a partition that does not exist. Writing a row that retention removes minutes later is worse than not writing it: it burns write capacity and makes the data appear to exist. This follows directly from retention keying on ts.device — a gateway offline for weeks delivers records that are near expiry on arrival, which is the accepted business rule, not a bug to fix by quietly switching to ts.server.

> RATIFIED 4 Sep 2026 — see [DL-112](#dl-112--late-arrival-skip-meter-ack-alert--signed); > BUILT 10 Sep 2026 — see DL-133. > Signed by Naman Vashishtha. T-026 is unblocked. DL-106 (Q15b: ts.device is > genuine device time) is what made the path real rather than hypothetical.

29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED 4 Sep 2026 (was PROPOSED) · ADR-003 D5b

Decision. A record arriving with ts_device older than the retention window is skipped, metered as records_expired_on_arrival, and ACKed — plus an alert when the counter is non-zero. T-026 stays BLOCKED pending product sign-off.

Why. No live partition exists for such a record, so the alternative is writing into a partition that does not exist. Writing a row that retention removes minutes later is worse than not writing it: it burns write capacity and makes the data appear to exist. This follows directly from retention keying on ts.device — a gateway offline for weeks delivers records that are near expiry on arrival, which is the accepted business rule, not a bug to fix by quietly switching to ts.server.

Why it is still open despite engineering agreement: it is a deliberate data-loss-by-design path. Engineering can say it is the only implementable option; only product can accept the loss.

Rejected. Silently discarding — violates the no-silent-error rule. Extending retention for late records — unbounded, and defeats detach-and-drop. Switching retention to ts.server — reverses an answered business rule.

Consequence. This is the same mechanism behind DL-034's redrive trap: a long-dead DLQ message becomes expired_on_arrival the moment it is redriven.

DL-085 — The production wire format is the contract; the SRS field names are withdrawn

Hasan supplied 15 real production records on 29 Aug 2026. Zero validated against the schemas as they stood, and the reason was that every envelope name came from SRS v1.0 and no production record has ever used one of them. Given that production is the wire format, one vocabulary end to end is the only shape with no translation step to get wrong.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §1 · Supersedes the envelope naming in SRS v1.0

Decision. The device identifier is clsbox and the arrival clock is ts.server, used identically on the wire, in the schema catalogue, in the Java model, in the PostgreSQL column, and in API responses. No rename layer anywhere.

Why. Hasan supplied 15 real production records on 29 Aug 2026. Zero validated against the schemas as they stood, and the reason was that every envelope name came from SRS v1.0 and no production record has ever used one of them. Given that production is the wire format, one vocabulary end to end is the only shape with no translation step to get wrong.

Rejected. Renaming at the Input boundary and keeping the SRS vocabulary downstream — a much smaller diff. Two specific reasons it lost. ADR-004 requires the published message to be byte-identical to what was received and ADR-003's per-partition duplicate suppression depends on that, so a rename breaks it. And a field-rename layer sitting beside tenant/maintenant is exactly the place CLAUDE.md §6 identifies as where a field is silently swapped — a failure that passes every test written against sample data where the two are equal, which is all of the sample data we have.

Consequence. 102 files changed. Affordable only because the three application modules are still empty shells; after Phase 2 this would have been a data migration. The *word* "gateway" survives in prose for the physical device — do not mechanically rewrite it.

DL-086 — ts.converter is accepted, ignored, and metered; ADR-004's rejection clause is withdrawn

ADR-004 declared such a record invalid and the envelope schema enforced it with not: {required: [converter]}. Production sends ts.converter on 100% of records. The rule rejected every record Heimdal sends.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §2 · Amends ADR-004

Decision. A record carrying ts.converter is valid. The member is stored, returned verbatim, and never read. Presence is metered as records_converter_ts_present{type}. ts.datastore and ts.expiresOn are treated the same way; ts.expiresOn is typed as a string, because production sends an ISO-8601 instant there rather than epoch seconds.

Why. ADR-004 declared such a record invalid and the envelope schema enforced it with not: {required: [converter]}. Production sends ts.converter on 100% of records. The rule rejected every record Heimdal sends.

Why the original reasoning was wrong, precisely. ADR-004 read ts.converter as evidence that the sender believes in a normalisation step this system does not perform. That reading was correct about the *sender* and wrong about the *remedy*. Heimdal does run a converter — status: ["conversion ok"] says so on every record — and the Stats App still performs no normalisation of its own. Rejecting the record punished us for upstream behaviour we had already decided to ignore. Accepting a field is not the same as acting on it, and conflating the two cost 100% of ingest.

Rejected. Keeping the rejection (rejects all traffic). Accepting without a metric (loses the signal for when Heimdal stops sending it).

Consequence. ADR-004's core decision — no timestamp normalisation — is unchanged and still binding. The fixture that asserted the old rule moved from invalid/ to valid/ and is now the regression guard for the whole envelope shape.

DL-087 — ts.server is the canonical arrival clock

It is Heimdal's own receipt clock, the direct analogue of the SRS's ts.processor, and the only one of the three that still means anything once the legacy Mongo datastore is retired.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §3

Decision. Of ts.server, ts.datastore and ts.converter — all present and identical in the sample — ts.server is canonical. Clock skew (ADR-003 D9, Q14a) compares ts.device against ts.server, and the API exposes ts.server as the processing time.

Why. It is Heimdal's own receipt clock, the direct analogue of the SRS's ts.processor, and the only one of the three that still means anything once the legacy Mongo datastore is retired.

Rejected. ts.datastore — the legacy store's write clock, describing a system we are replacing. Deferring the choice — would have blocked the clock-skew check and the API's processing-time field for no gain, since the three are identical anyway.

Consequence. Retention still keys on ts.device, unchanged (STA-23B). Note the open question this leaves: ts.device equals ts.server in 14 of 15 records, so it is not established that ts.device is genuinely device-assigned (ADR-007 Q15b).

DL-088 — Two validation gates: envelope at Input, payload at the Writer

The costs are asymmetric and irreversible in one direction. Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so a 400 issued for a payload disagreement destroys the record permanently; a dead-letter is recoverable by redrive. Production sends lorastats in three incompatible dialects today, so this is not hypothetical.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §5 · Refines STA-03A

Decision. stats-input validates the envelope only; a failure is a 400 and nothing is published. stats-writer validates the envelope and the per-type payload; a failure there is a dead-letter. In Java, StatsSchemaValidator.validateEnvelope() and .validate().

Why. The costs are asymmetric and irreversible in one direction. Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so a 400 issued for a payload disagreement destroys the record permanently; a dead-letter is recoverable by redrive. Production sends lorastats in three incompatible dialects today, so this is not hypothetical.

Rejected. Validating everything at Input (the previous behaviour) — turns every dialect disagreement into unrecoverable loss. Validating nothing at Input — gives up the 400 for genuinely malformed envelopes, which *is* the sender's bug to fix.

Consequence. "Invalid" no longer implies "400". Anything reading a fixture from invalid/ and asserting a 400 is now wrong. The fixture gate gained a $expect: "writer-dlq" marker that asserts both halves — passes the envelope, fails the payload — and validate-fixtures.py checks the envelope half explicitly.

DL-089 — One lorastats dialect is encoded; the others dead-letter

Three consecutive production records used three mutually incompatible shapes, and all three carried version: 1, so nothing on the record discriminates them. Bare/integer was chosen because it matches omsstats, which shares a converter family.

> SUPERSEDED 4 Sep 2026 by [DL-105](#dl-105--all-three-known-lorastats-dialects-are-accepted-and-normalised-to-one-row). > Q15a was answered: all three known dialects are accepted and normalised to one > row. Two findings below survive and are still binding — version is never a > parser discriminator, and a union that grows silently is a contract nobody > understands (DL-105 rules 1 and 4). The rest is history.

29 Aug 2026 · SUPERSEDED (was ACCEPTED, ON AN ASSUMPTION) · ADR-007 §4 · Q15a — answered 4 Sep 2026
