Decision. The lorastats schema encodes one payload dialect — bare field names, integer values. The two other dialects observed in production — lora_-prefixed names, and bare names with string values — are dead-lettered.

Why. Three consecutive production records used three mutually incompatible shapes, and all three carried version: 1, so nothing on the record discriminates them. Bare/integer was chosen because it matches omsstats, which shares a converter family.

Rejected. A permissive union accepting every spelling and both value types. It would have made all 15 records pass — which is the problem. It silently ratifies a contract nobody understands, and a union only ever grows. Dead-lettering makes the disagreement visible and recoverable instead.

Consequence. This is an assumption, not an answer, and it dead-letters roughly two thirds of lorastats. Two things follow. version must never be used to select a parser. And ADR-002 §12 forbids a DLQ TTL, so at 115.2M records/day this accumulates on the order of 15M messages/day with no expiry — which makes Q15a urgent rather than routine, and makes DLQ depth alerting a Phase-2 blocker. Do not resolve a DLQ spike by widening the schema; that is the option this entry rejected.

DL-090 — A field absent because the device is broken is optional, not invalid

netstats required gw/ip/mask/dns on every interface, derived from one healthy sample. Production sends a wwan that never came up — need_init: "qmi", a fail_count, and no address at all — so the schema dead-lettered exactly the record operations most needs to see. That is a wrong requirement, not a dialect disagreement.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §6

Decision. Where production shows a field legitimately absent because of device state, the requirement is corrected. Where production shows two incompatible encodings of the same thing, one is encoded and the rest dead-letter (DL-089). Under the first rule: netstats interfaces now require only tx/rx; sysstats gains optional temp, rtt, wan_if, rebootcause, timespan; ltestats numerics become strings and the field becomes access_Technology.

Why. netstats required gw/ip/mask/dns on every interface, derived from one healthy sample. Production sends a wwan that never came up — need_init: "qmi", a fail_count, and no address at all — so the schema dead-lettered exactly the record operations most needs to see. That is a wrong requirement, not a dialect disagreement.

Rejected. Treating these as dialect cases and dead-lettering them — would discard broken-device telemetry, which is the highest-value telemetry there is. Blanket permissiveness — strictness still earns its place: an address that *is* present must still be well-formed IPv4, and counters must still be digits.

Consequence. The ltestats corrections are transcription fixes, not widenings: each of the two — integer-instead-of-string, and the lowercase access_technology — alone dead-lettered 100% of ltestats. Before making a field required, ask what the record looks like when the thing it describes is *failing*.

DL-091 — The typed view must be lossless

ADR-004 requires byte-identical publication and ADR-003's per-partition duplicate suppression depends on it. A typed view that silently dropped ts.expiresOn would break that without failing anything — the classic silent failure this project keeps finding. NON_NULL is what lets the pre-ADR-007 fixture corpus still round-trip: without it every one of those records would gain four null members and stop matching what was received.

29 Aug 2026 · ACCEPTED · ADR-007 §2 · Enables ADR-004

Decision. StatsEnvelope and Timestamps model every production member, including version, status, ts.datastore, ts.converter and ts.expiresOn, and carry @JsonInclude(NON_NULL) so absent members do not serialise as null. Timestamps also carries @JsonIgnoreProperties(ignoreUnknown = true).

Why. ADR-004 requires byte-identical publication and ADR-003's per-partition duplicate suppression depends on it. A typed view that silently dropped ts.expiresOn would break that without failing anything — the classic silent failure this project keeps finding. NON_NULL is what lets the pre-ADR-007 fixture corpus still round-trip: without it every one of those records would gain four null members and stop matching what was received.

Rejected. Ignoring the passthrough members and letting additionalProperties carry them — works for the raw JSON path but breaks the moment anything round-trips through the typed view. Typing expiresOn as epoch seconds — throws on every production record, because it is an ISO-8601 string.

Consequence. Guarded by StatsEnvelopeTest.preservesTheWholeProductionTimestampBlockThroughTheTypedView and .omitsAbsentOptionalMembersRatherThanSerialisingThemAsNull. In PostgreSQL the unread members land in a single nullable passthrough jsonb column (ADR-003) rather than three columns, because nothing queries them.

DL-093 — The lorastats index set is corrected to ADR-003 in V3

V2 shipped (ts_device DESC, uid DESC), which the planner cannot use: PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid) already provides an equivalent btree and PostgreSQL answers a DESC/DESC ordering from it with a backward scan. Measured on PG17 against these exact migrations, dropping it leaves Index Only Scan Backward using lorastats_pkey, and the planner never selected it while it existed. It was a second full btree maintained on every insert — 23.04M rows per type per day — buying nothing.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-011, ADR-003 "Required table and index direction", STA-21C, rule 3 · Corrects V2

Decision. V3__lorastats_adr003_indexes.sql drops idx_lorastats_tsdevice_uid and creates the two indexes ADR-003 specifies: idx_lorastats_tenant_clsbox_device_uid (tenant, clsbox, ts_device DESC, uid DESC) and idx_lorastats_tenant_uid (tenant, uid). Both are declared on the partitioned parent so PostgreSQL propagates them to every partition.

Why. V2 shipped (ts_device DESC, uid DESC), which the planner cannot use: PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid) already provides an equivalent btree and PostgreSQL answers a DESC/DESC ordering from it with a backward scan. Measured on PG17 against these exact migrations, dropping it leaves Index Only Scan Backward using lorastats_pkey, and the planner never selected it while it existed. It was a second full btree maintained on every insert — 23.04M rows per type per day — buying nothing.

Meanwhile neither ADR-003 index existed. The dominant tenant-scoped page planned as a pkey backward scan with a discard filter: 2,444 rows read and thrown away to return 50, on one day of 200k rows and 50 tenants; a production partition holds ~23M. The uid-only lookup planned as a pkey walk on a non-leading column: 989 buffer hits for one row on one partition, and GET /v1/stats/{type}/{uid} supplies no time bound, so it touches all 90 live partitions. tenant leads both because the tenant predicate is mandatory on every query (rule 3, STA-35).

Rejected. Editing V1/V2 — they are applied migrations and are immutable. Keeping the V2 index alongside the new ones "just in case" — it is provably unreachable, and an unused index at this write volume is a permanent cost with no upside.

Consequence. CREATE INDEX cannot be CONCURRENTLY on a partitioned parent. The tables are empty at this migration so the lock is free, but applying the same change to a populated cluster needs a maintenance window, or per-partition concurrent builds followed by ATTACH. The migration test asserts the resulting index set and its propagation to a child partition, because the previous test asserted the opposite — hasSize(2), no index containing tenant, and a count of zero for both ADR-003 index names — which had made the wrong shape a green build.

DL-094 — passthrough stores the wire's own shape and units

ADR-004's byte-identical publication only matters if the record survives to storage. The first insert implementation mapped neither column, so every production record — all of which carry ts.converter, and most a version — was stored having lost them, and nothing failed: all five members are optional, so no schema test could see it. Storing the wire's own units is what makes the stored value reconstruct the received one; normalising to millis or an ISO instant here would quietly change what a round trip returns.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-058, ADR-004, ADR-007 §2, ADR-003 D7

Decision. The Writer populates record_version from the envelope's version, and passthrough from ts.datastore, ts.converter, ts.expiresOn and status, shaped as {"ts": {...}, "status": [...]}. Timestamps are stored as epoch seconds and expiresOn as the ISO-8601 string production sends. Both columns stay NULL when the record carries none of it.

Why. ADR-004's byte-identical publication only matters if the record survives to storage. The first insert implementation mapped neither column, so every production record — all of which carry ts.converter, and most a version — was stored having lost them, and nothing failed: all five members are optional, so no schema test could see it. Storing the wire's own units is what makes the stored value reconstruct the received one; normalising to millis or an ISO instant here would quietly change what a round trip returns.

Rejected. Three separate columns — ADR-003 already rejected that: nothing queries them. Flattening to {"datastore": ..., "converter": ...} — loses which members were inside ts, so the reconstruction is a guess.

Consequence. schema_version is still the literal 1. ADR-003 D7 says it must come from the schema that actually validated the record, and until T-057 puts the payload gate in the Writer nothing has validated it. That is a known, commented gap that T-057 closes — not a decision to leave it.

> CLOSED 8 September 2026 — DL-126. T-057 landed and the literal is gone: > ValidatedRecord carries the label from ValidationResult.schemaVersion() through to > the column, so it now names the contract that admitted the row rather than the one its > author had in mind. The two answers agree only while there is one schema.

DL-095 — D14b: unknown data members are preserved in data_extra, and metered

D14 (DL-051) requires unknown data members to be accepted and preserved, and lorastats-v1.schema.json is additionalProperties: true — so such a member is valid and must survive. ADR-003 D2 gives lorastats typed columns, which have nowhere to put one, so the Writer dropped it silently. That is the same outcome D2 rejected in writing when it chose a jsonb interface map for netstats over fixed eth0_* columns; the argument does not stop applying because the type is different.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · D14b, DL-051, ADR-003 D2, T-021/T-022 · Closes the D14b row in claude/CLAUDE.md §7

Decision. Each typed stat-type table gains a nullable data_extra jsonb. The Writer routes any data member the schema does not name into it, verbatim, and increments records_unmapped_data_field{type}. Unindexed; never read by the application.

Why. D14 (DL-051) requires unknown data members to be accepted and preserved, and lorastats-v1.schema.json is additionalProperties: true — so such a member is valid and must survive. ADR-003 D2 gives lorastats typed columns, which have nowhere to put one, so the Writer dropped it silently. That is the same outcome D2 rejected in writing when it chose a jsonb interface map for netstats over fixed eth0_* columns; the argument does not stop applying because the type is different.

The metric matters as much as the column. A member appearing in data_extra means Heimdal and this schema have diverged, which is a signal — but only if someone can see it. Preserved-and-invisible is how the divergence gets found a year later.

Rejected. Dropping and documenting it — the option D2 already rejected. A full data jsonb alongside the typed columns — reintroduces the ~375 GB/type key-name overhead D2 exists to avoid, on every row, to serve the rare one. Rejecting the record — an unknown member is explicitly valid under D14; dead-lettering it would break on Heimdal's next additive change.

Consequence. NULL on the overwhelming majority of rows, where it costs one null-bitmap bit — key names are stored only for records that actually carry one, so this does not become D2's problem by another route. ADD COLUMN with no default is catalog-only on PG11+, which is what makes it affordable to add to the other four types later. Every new stat-type table must include the column; a type that omits it silently reopens the drop.

DL-096 — Partition pre-creation runs at startup, before the listeners, and fatally

The cron (0 30 3 * * *) was the only trigger, so a module deployed at any other hour came up against a parent table with no partitions and stayed that way for up to a day. Every insert fails with no partition of relation ... found for row; the T-020 manual-ACK contract correctly withholds the ACK, so nothing is lost — and that is precisely what makes it dangerous. The consumer wedges once its prefetch window is entirely unacknowledged, and it does not recover when the cron finally fires, because those deliveries stay unacked until the process restarts. A whole stat type stops ingesting, quietly, exactly the silent failure ADR-003 D1 demands be loud.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-012, ADR-003 D1/D3, STA-23C

Decision. PartitionBootstrap is a SmartLifecycle at a phase below Spring AMQP's listener containers. It ensures the deployment's partitions before anything consumes, and a failure is fatal — the context does not start. The retention cron still runs and keeps the horizon rolling. stats.retention.pre-create-on-startup=false disables it for deployments where a separate job owns partition DDL.

Why. The cron (0 30 3 * * *) was the only trigger, so a module deployed at any other hour came up against a parent table with no partitions and stayed that way for up to a day. Every insert fails with no partition of relation ... found for row; the T-020 manual-ACK contract correctly withholds the ACK, so nothing is lost — and that is precisely what makes it dangerous. The consumer wedges once its prefetch window is entirely unacknowledged, and it does not recover when the cron finally fires, because those deliveries stay unacked until the process restarts. A whole stat type stops ingesting, quietly, exactly the silent failure ADR-003 D1 demands be loud.

SmartLifecycle rather than @PostConstruct or ApplicationReadyEvent: the work has to land after Flyway and before the listener containers start. @PostConstruct is not ordered against the Flyway initializer without a bean-name @DependsOn; ApplicationReadyEvent fires *after* finishRefresh(), which is where the containers already started. Only the lifecycle phases make it a guarantee, and a test asserts the phase against a real container instead of trusting a remembered constant.

Rejected. Starting anyway and letting the cron fix it — that is the wedge above. Creating partitions lazily on the first insert failure — DDL on the consume path, racing across replicas, in the one code path that is already failing. pg_partman — never a dependency (ADR-003 D1).

Consequence. A database that is unreachable at boot is now a startup failure rather than a running process that cannot work. For a module whose entire job needs the database, crash-loop-with-a-clear-cause is the better failure, and readiness stays down. A deployment configured for a stat type whose migration has not landed is named explicitly at startup rather than surfacing as a confusing DDL error.

DL-100 — A counter nobody can scrape is not a metric, and config that describes an unbuilt gate must say so

All three read as statements of fact and were statements of intent.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-022, T-023, T-058, T-027, T-006, T-013, T-057 · DN-18

Decision. Three things, all of the same shape — a claim in the repository that nothing made true:

1. micrometer-registry-prometheus is a runtime dependency of all three application modules, and stats-writer gains spring-boot-starter-web. ManagementSurfaceTest scrapes /actuator/prometheus and asserts every Writer counter is present. 2. STATS_DLQ_ALERT_DEPTH_LORASTATS is documented in .env.example, with why lorastats cannot share the alert-depth.default=0 the other four types use. 3. stats.*.validation.scope is renamed to validation.intended-scope in both modules, with a NOT YET ENFORCED banner naming the task that will build the gate (T-013 for Input, T-057 for the Writer). The ADR-007 §5 reasoning stays with the key.

Why. All three read as statements of fact and were statements of intent.

spring-boot-starter-actuator brings micrometer-core, which registers a Counter and cannot export one; with no registry implementation Spring Boot falls back to SimpleMeterRegistry and the metric never leaves the JVM. stats-writer had no servlet container at all, so server.port=8082, management.endpoints.web.exposure.include and management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled were all inert and the readiness healthcheck already committed in docker/docker-compose.yml could never pass. Nothing failed: the module started, writes committed, and the unit tests asserting counter.count() stayed green. That is the point — records_duplicate_suppressed is the only thing standing between ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING and silence (STA-27A, ADR-005, DL-095), and un-exported it is exactly the silent loss it was built to reveal.

stats.writer.validation.scope=envelope-and-payload was worse than absent. No code reads it and StatsSchemaValidator is wired into neither module, so the key asserted that the Writer's payload gate was live when every payload still reaches the INSERT unvalidated. Someone acting on it in the other direction — moving validate() to Input — turns a redrivable dead-letter into a 400 Heimdal will never retry, which is ADR-007 §5's whole subject and FLOW.md §8 window 12.

Rejected. *Deleting the validation-scope keys* — the ADR-007 reasoning attached to them is worth more than the tidiness, and these properties files are deliberately a design ledger (stats.exchange.name and stats.amqp.retry.* are unread too). *Building the gates now* — that is T-013 and T-057, and pulling them into a cleanup would ship the Input gate without the publisher-confirm path it belongs to. *A separate management port on the Writer* — it would diverge from the other two modules and break the committed compose healthcheck for no gain, since no controller exists to isolate it from and a test now asserts none appears.

Consequence. stats-writer runs a servlet container that serves actuator and nothing else; theWebServerCarriesNoApplicationEndpoint fails if a controller ever lands there, which keeps rule 1 and rule 2 intact. T-027 stays PARTIAL — this made the three existing counters observable, it did not add the missing ones. T-006 stays PARTIAL: probes are now reachable, but readiness is still Boot's default composite rather than the per-module dependency set D13 asks for.

Note for anyone writing the next metrics test. Spring Boot disables metrics export under @SpringBootTest — its context customizer injects management.defaults.metrics.export.enabled=false. A scrape test therefore needs @AutoConfigureObservability, or it 404s against a perfectly correct application and "proves" the opposite of what it was written for.

DL-101 — Input's gate is the envelope, and the config that says so now refuses to start if changed

4 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-013, T-059, T-027 · ADR-007 §5, ADR-003 D9, DL-088

Decision. stats-input gets its POST endpoint, and with it four things:

1. EnvelopeGate calls StatsSchemaValidator.validateEnvelope() and nothing else. A failure is a 400 with RFC 9457 Problem Details, and nothing is published (rule 7). The per-type payload is not looked at. 2. InputValidationProperties binds stats.input.validation.intended-scope and the application refuses to start on any value but envelope. DL-100 renamed the key and gave it a NOT YET ENFORCED banner; this is the enforcement it named. 3. The clock-skew check (Q14a, ADR-003 D9) is one-sided. ts.device ahead of ts.server beyond stats.input.validation.max-clock-skew is a 400 with the field code clock_skew_exceeded at /ts/device, metered as records_clock_skew_rejected{type}. A ts.device in the past is accepted, however old. 4. stats.input.endpoint.path and .success-status are externalised, defaulting to the contract's draft /stats and 200, with the status constrained in code to the two values heimdal-input-contract-v1.md §2 admits.

Why the payload is not validated here. This is the decision the whole module is shaped around, and it is counter-intuitive enough to be worth restating: Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx. A 400 issued because data disagreed with a per-type schema is therefore permanent data loss — the sender is told the record is bad and never sends it again. The same disagreement caught at the Writer is a dead-letter, which is redrivable once the schema is fixed. Production sends lorastats in three mutually incompatible dialects today, all carrying version: 1, so roughly two-thirds of lorastats depends on this asymmetry being right.

EnvelopeGateTest asserts it directly: all four invalid/lorastats-*.json fixtures — including the two byte-faithful production records — are accepted by Input. If that assertion ever flips to expecting a 400, the module has started destroying records.

Why the scope property is a startup guard rather than a comment. A property that only documents intent is one edit away from being wrong, and the edit that widens this gate looks like a tightening — "validate properly at the boundary" is the instinct of every reviewer who has not read ADR-007 §5. Failing at startup puts the argument in front of whoever makes that change, in a class that explains why.

Why the skew rule is one-sided. Rejecting a future ts.device is forced: partition pre-creation is finite, so a gateway claiming 2038 either demands unbounded partitions or fails deep in the Writer where the caller can no longer be told. Rejecting an *old* ts.device would be the opposite of the design — retention keys on ts.device (STA-23B) precisely so an offline gateway's readings expire on when they were measured. A record from a gateway that was down for a month is the record operations most needs; what happens to it is the Writer's late-arrival policy (D5b), not a 400.

Not in this change. Publishing. StatsPublisher is a port with a deliberate stand-in that always answers NOT_CONFIRMED, so a record that passes the gate is answered 503 and Heimdal retries. That is the honest state until T-014: rule 8 allows a 2xx only after a real publisher confirm, and a module that publishes nothing must never claim success. The stand-in is @ConditionalOnMissingBean, so T-014 is additive.

Metrics added (T-027, T-059): records_accepted, records_validation_failed, records_clock_skew_rejected, records_converter_ts_present, records_publish_failed — all labelled by type, with unknown for records whose type could not be read. An absent series and a zero series look identical on a dashboard, and a flood of unparseable bodies is exactly when the series has to exist.

DL-105 — All three known lorastats dialects are accepted and normalised to one row

Dead-lettering two thirds of lorastats was only defensible while the dialects were unexplained. Q15a explains them — three shapes are in the field simultaneously, all carrying version: 1, and no retirement timeline has been given. At that point the DLQ was not rejecting bad data; it was rejecting data, roughly 15M messages a day, into a queue ADR-002 §12 forbids ever expiring.

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q15a) + RATIFIED · ADR-007 §4 (amended), DL-089 (superseded) · T-063, T-057, T-060

Decision. Hasan's answer to Q15a is *"make it work for all the known dialects."* DL-089 is superseded. The lorastats payload schema accepts all three dialects observed in production:

Dialect Field names Value types Example
A bare integer "rx_nr_pkts": 176
B bare string "rx_nr_pkts": "176"
C lora_-prefixed integer "lora_rx_nr_pkts": 176

Four rules bound the widening, and each one is load-bearing:

1. Detection is structural, never version. All three carry version: 1. DL-089's one durable finding survives intact: anything that branches on version to select a parser is wrong. 2. All three normalise to the same typed columns. One stored shape, one API shape. The dialect a record arrived in is not a column and is not queryable — it is a metric label, records_lorastats_dialect{dialect}, which gives the population view without pushing a three-way branch onto every consumer. 3. Coercion is explicit and range-checked. Dialect B's strings are parsed per-field and range-checked against the signed BIGINT maximum *before* conversion — the rule Q4a already set for netstats counters. A value that does not parse, or that overflows, dead-letters. It is never silently zeroed, truncated or defaulted. 4. An unknown fourth dialect still dead-letters. "All the known dialects" is exactly three. The schema is a closed enumeration of three, not an open union: a new spelling surfaces in the DLQ rather than being absorbed.

Why. Dead-lettering two thirds of lorastats was only defensible while the dialects were unexplained. Q15a explains them — three shapes are in the field simultaneously, all carrying version: 1, and no retirement timeline has been given. At that point the DLQ was not rejecting bad data; it was rejecting data, roughly 15M messages a day, into a queue ADR-002 §12 forbids ever expiring.

What changed in the reasoning, precisely. DL-089 rejected a permissive union because *"it would have made all 15 records pass, and that is exactly the problem — it silently ratifies a contract nobody understands."* That was correct while the contract was unknown. Accepting a contract we now understand, with a closed enumeration and a named owner for each dialect, is a different act. Rules 1 and 4 exist so that the *unbounded* half of DL-089's objection — that a union only ever grows — still cannot happen.

Rejected. *Storing the dialect distinctly* — costs a discriminator column and gives the API three shapes for one stat type, for a distinction with no business meaning; rule 2's metric label answers the only real question ("how much of the fleet is on C?") for free. *Coercing dialect B with a permissive parse that falls back to zero* — silent corruption, and the exact shape of the int4-wrapping trap (trap 11). *Opening the schema to any spelling* — that is the union DL-089 rejected and rule 4 still rejects. *Branching on version* — three dialects, one version.

Consequence. T-063 is new and sits in Phase 2 ahead of the lorastats writer path: widen the schema to the three-dialect enumeration, add fixtures moving invalid/lorastats-dialect-prefixed.json and invalid/lorastats-dialect-string-values.json from invalid/ to valid/, and implement structural detection with range-checked coercion. Trap 20 becomes more load-bearing, not less — we now branch on structure, and version sits there looking exactly like the discriminator it is not. Trap 22 largely dissolves: the lorastats DLQ stops carrying ~15M routine messages/day, so T-060's per-type thresholds are still wanted but for ordinary reasons rather than to stop one type drowning the signal. ADR-007 §4 is amended and its Consequences section's DLQ volume warning no longer applies.

Residual. Q15a's second half — *are the other dialects being retired?* — is still unanswered. It blocks nothing. It only determines whether we can ever delete a parser, and rule 2's metric label is what will eventually show a dialect going to zero in the field.

> BUILT 8 September 2026 — see DL-127. Everything above is the decision; DL-127 is > what the code does and what it cost. Two implementation facts belong with this entry: > dialect C is not "everything prefixed" — the production record mixes fifteen > lora_ counters with a bare fsk_received and a bare timespan — and normalisation > has to run before the column mapping, or data_extra swallows the prefixed > counters and leaves every typed column null.

DL-106 — ts.device is genuine device time, so every consequence of retention-on-device is real

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q15b) · STA-23B, ADR-003, ADR-003 D9 · D5b/DL-112, T-026, T-052

Decision. ZC confirms ts.device is assigned by the device, and the record is sent to Heimdal as soon as the device records it. Retention keying on ts.device (STA-23B) is therefore keying on measurement time, not on arrival time.

Why the sample looked like the opposite. ts.device equalled ts.server exactly in 14 of 15 production records, and differed by 1 second in the fifteenth — which reads as an arrival stamp. It is not. All 15 came from healthy, online gateways sending immediately, so the two clocks coincide by construction. The sample shows the healthy path; it is not evidence about the absence of the unhealthy one. Fifteen records from one moment cannot demonstrate that no gateway is ever offline.

Consequence. Everything that rested on this being real is real, and none of it is theoretical:

* The offline-backfill trap (trap 5). A gateway offline for three weeks delivers records already 21 days into their 90-day window. * The late-arrival policy (D5b, DL-112, T-026). A gateway offline longer than the retention window delivers records with no live partition at all. * The redrive-age trap (trap 18). A DLQ message redriven after ~90 days is a no-op that increments a counter, not a recovery. * The clock-skew gate (ADR-003 D9). A device clock can genuinely run ahead of Heimdal's, which is what the skew tolerance is for.

Boundary tests either side of the retention window, keyed on ts_device, are required rather than defensive. Q15b closes.

DL-107 — _id is a Mongo export artifact and never appears on the wire

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q15c) · D14, DL-095, ADR-007 · Fixtures

Decision. ZC confirms Heimdal stores records in its own MongoDB and that whoever produced the 29 Aug sample dumped straight out of that database. _id is an artifact of the export and is never POSTed. No _id handling is built.

Consequence. D14 is unchanged and remains the safety net — this answer removes a reason to write code, not the policy that made the code unnecessary. An unknown *envelope* member is still rejected at the Input gate; an unknown *data* member is still preserved into data_extra (DL-095). Any fixture derived from the 29 Aug dump must have _id stripped, or it tests the exporter rather than the wire.

The thing to carry. Heimdal's own store being MongoDB is not permission to reintroduce Mongo anywhere in this repository. It is upstream's storage. The rule in CLAUDE.md §3 stands unchanged: no drivers, no MongoRepository, no TTL indexes, no "collection"/"document" vocabulary. Q15c closes.

DL-108 — A non-OK status is metered and stored, never dead-lettered

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q15d) + ENGINEERING · ADR-007 §1, D14, DL-102 · T-064, T-050

Decision. ZC confirms status can report a failed conversion, not only ["conversion ok"]. The Writer therefore:

* Stores the record. A non-OK status is upstream's verdict on its own conversion, not a failure of our validation. * Meters it as records_upstream_status_not_ok{type}, incremented whenever status is anything other than exactly ["conversion ok"]. The comparison is value-agnostic on purpose. * Logs at WARN with uid, type, clsbox and the status array — and never the payload, under DL-102's level gate. * Keeps status a passthrough member. Stored and returned verbatim; nothing branches on it.

Why value-agnostic. We do not have the vocabulary of failure strings. A check that recognises only the values we happen to know about goes quiet on the ones we do not — which is the failure mode the whole "nothing is ever silently dropped" rule exists to prevent. Comparing against the one known-good value inverts that: anything unrecognised is counted, by definition.

Rejected. *Dead-lettering a non-OK status* — the record is valid, was correctly delivered, and a redrive produces the identical result forever; that is a DLQ entry that can never be drained, in a DLQ with no TTL. *Waiting for the vocabulary before metering* — the counter does not need it, and the counter is what will name the actual values. *A metric label carrying the status value* — an unbounded label set sourced from an upstream vocabulary we do not control is a Prometheus cardinality bomb.

Consequence. T-064 is new. Q15d closes as asked: we now have a signal where previously we would have stored records upstream already knew were bad, with nothing on our side saying so. T-050's alert set gains the counter.

Residual. The vocabulary of non-OK status values is unknown. It blocks nothing — the counter is value-agnostic — and it is worth asking on the next round with Hasan only to decide whether any *specific* failure deserves different handling. The counter will have enumerated the real values by then in any case.

DL-112 — Late arrival: skip, meter, ACK, alert — signed

> BUILT 10 Sep 2026 — see [DL-133](#dl-133--a-record-past-its-window-is-skipped-and-acked-and-the-sweep-and-the-guard-share-one-cutoff). > RetentionGuard implements it, and the guard reads the same cutoff as the sweep that > drops the partition. The alert is still not built — that half is T-050.

4 Sep 2026 · RATIFIED (D5b) · ADR-003, DL-083, DL-106, STA-23B · T-026, T-052, T-050

Decision. Signed by Naman Vashishtha on 4 September 2026, alongside DL-111. A record arriving with ts_device older than the retention window is skipped, metered as records_expired_on_arrival, and ACKed, with an alert when the counter is non-zero. DL-083 moves from PROPOSED to ratified and T-026 is unblocked.

This is deliberate data loss, and the signature is what makes it a decision rather than a defect. It is also the only implementable option: no live partition exists for such a record, so the alternative is writing a row into a partition that does not exist. Writing it into a freshly created partition that retention then removes minutes later is worse — it costs the write, the partition and the delete, and it looks like success.

Why it is signed now and not on 29 August. DL-106 removed the one reason to hold it. While Q15b was open it remained possible that ts.device was stamped on arrival, in which case no record could ever be late and this policy governed a path production never takes — signing a data-loss policy for a hypothetical path is how a policy stops being read carefully. Q15b's answer makes the path real.

Why the alert, and not the counter alone. A systemic gateway backlog and a harmless one-off are the same number in a raw count. The alert is what separates them.

Consequence. T-026 moves BLOCKED → TODO, with boundary tests either side of the retention window. Trap 18 is the same code path reached from the DLQ: a message redriven after ~90 days increments records_expired_on_arrival and looks like a successful recovery unless T-052's runbook says otherwise. D5b leaves the open-questions sheet.

DL-115 — clsbox is stored in one canonical case, by normalisation and not by rejection

The ARD specified gateway CHAR(12) NOT NULL CHECK (gateway ~ '^[0-9A-F]{12}$'). V1 renamed the column to clsbox under ADR-007 §1 and dropped the constraint; nothing replaced the guarantee. So 04b64810652b and 04B64810652B — one physical device — were two distinct keys in idx_lorastats_tenant_clsbox_device_uid.

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · ARD §7.1, Q14, STA-21C, ADR-003, ADR-007 §1 · T-011, T-022, T-040

Decision. Four parts, and the split between them is the decision:

1. The Writer uppercases clsbox before the INSERT (LorastatsBatchWriter, Locale.ROOT). 2. V5 adds CHECK (clsbox ~ '^[0-9A-F]{12}$') as the backstop, so the invariant belongs to the table rather than to one class. ADR-003's *Required table and index direction* carries it, and every new stat-type table must — like data_extra. 3. The envelope schema keeps ^[0-9A-Fa-f]{12}$. A lowercase record is never 400'd and never dead-lettered. 4. The API filter is clsbox = upper(:clsbox), never upper(clsbox) = ….

Why. The ARD specified gateway CHAR(12) NOT NULL CHECK (gateway ~ '^[0-9A-F]{12}$'). V1 renamed the column to clsbox under ADR-007 §1 and dropped the constraint; nothing replaced the guarantee. So 04b64810652b and 04B64810652B — one physical device — were two distinct keys in idx_lorastats_tenant_clsbox_device_uid.

That is the quiet kind of wrong. clsbox is half the dominant query pattern (Q14), and openapi.yaml promises the filter matches case-insensitively. A mixed-case estate therefore returns a silently partial page: rows stored, query well-formed, response 200, and the missing rows indistinguishable from a device that reported nothing. It is the twelfth entry in the same family as the tenant/maintenant trap — a filter that is *almost* right returns *almost* the data, and nothing anywhere raises.

No test could have caught it. Every clsbox in docs/schemas/fixtures/ was uppercase until valid/lorastats-clsbox-lowercase.json was added with this change.

Why normalise rather than reject. Enforcing the ARD's CHECK *without* normalising would dead-letter a valid measurement over hex case — the same mistake netstats made by requiring ip/gw/mask/dns on a modem that never came up, and the one DL-105 has just finished reversing for the lorastats dialects. Rejecting at Input would be worse still: Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so a 400 would loop forever on a record that can never satisfy it.

Why not a functional index. (tenant, upper(clsbox), ts_device DESC, uid DESC) would preserve the received bytes exactly. It was rejected: it changes the index shape ADR-003 specifies, and every future per-type table would have to repeat the functional form or silently lose the index — reopening this defect one type at a time.

What is given up. The as-received case. Hex case carries no meaning, so nothing a reader can use is lost, but it is still a normalisation: the exact bytes are recoverable only from the published message, not from the row. ADR-004's byte-identical publication is untouched — Input republishes the received bytes and never reads this column.

Consequence. LorastatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest asserts both spellings answer one exact-match lookup on the covering index, and that the table refuses a non-canonical value when the Writer is bypassed. T-040 must use clsbox = upper(:clsbox); a predicate of the form upper(clsbox) = … seq-scans every live partition and is a review failure.

DL-118 — A 2xx follows the confirm, and Input declares the exchange and nothing else

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-04A, STA-06A, rule 8, ADR-002 §9/§11, ADR-005 · T-014, T-015, T-032

Decision. Four parts.

1. ConfirmingStatsPublisher reads the confirm off CorrelationData.getFuture() with a bounded wait (stats.amqp.confirm-timeout, 5 s). CONFIRMED is returned on an ack with no return, and on nothing else. Nack, timeout, interrupt and AmqpException all become NOT_CONFIRMED, therefore 503. 2. An ack carrying a returned message is a FAILED acceptance — records_unroutable{type} plus the ERROR log with the routing key, and 503 (ADR-002 §9). records_publish_failed{type} moves as well: this counter is the *cause*, that one is the *outcome*. 3. stats-input declares the app_stats_input exchange and no queues. 4. stats.exchange.name becomes stats.amqp.exchange, is bound, and is checked against AmqpTopology.INPUT_EXCHANGE — any other value refuses to start.

Why waitForConfirms is not used. It is the simple-mode API and this template runs correlated. Mixed, it answers about a batch the calling thread never published — a green test and a 200 for a record nobody holds.

Why Input declares no queues. ADR-002 §11 puts declaration in the broker-connected modules; it does not say each declares every object, and the distinction earns its keep twice. A queue Input declares for a type whose Writer is not deployed is an undrained quorum queue nothing alerts on, because depth alerting belongs to the Writer (§12). More seriously, declaring the queues would make the missing-binding case unreachable — and that case is the *entire* reason mandatory is set. A confirm says the broker accepted the message, not that a queue wanted it; a half-applied topology change must surface as a return, not be papered over by the publisher declaring its own consumer's queue.

Why the exchange name is checked rather than merely bound. Pointing Input at an exchange no Writer binds to is accepted, broker-confirmed, and silently dropped — every component reporting success while the record ceases to exist. The property exists so a deployment can *see* the name, not choose it. Unbound, it was documentation with nothing reading it, which is the state DL-101 was written about.

Verified, not assumed. The framework guarantee this rests on — that Spring AMQP sets the returned message on the CorrelationData before completing its future — is asserted against a real broker with a genuinely unbound routing key in IngestPublishIntegrationTest, not taken from the reference documentation. The timeout path asserts elapsed time, because "waits until the socket dies" also returns NOT_CONFIRMED and would look identical in a test that only checked the outcome.

Consequence. UnconfirmedStatsPublisher drops out through its own @ConditionalOnMissingBean and PublisherConfiguration was not edited, as it was designed for. That condition is evaluated in configuration-registration order, which for a component scan follows class name: InputAmqpConfiguration sorts before PublisherConfiguration. A rename could invert it silently and leave a module that starts cleanly and publishes nothing, so PublisherSelectionTest asserts there is exactly one StatsPublisher and it is the confirming one.

Still open. T-032 — the cross-module declaration-conflict test — is not closed by this. IngestPublishIntegrationTest pins Input's half (name, durable, non-auto-delete, no arguments); the Writer's half is still a second hand-built TopicExchange, because AmqpTopology deliberately holds no Spring AMQP types and stats-common has no broker dependency. A mismatch is RESOURCE_LOCKED for whichever module boots second.

DL-119 — Byte-identity is proved with a fixture built to fail, kept out of the normative set

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-004, ADR-005, STA-25A · T-016

Decision. Input publishes with RabbitTemplate.send and a hand-built Message, never convertAndSend, and the proof is stats-input/src/test/resources/byte-identity/lorastats-adversarial-formatting.json — a record posted through the endpoint and compared byte-for-byte with what arrives on the queue.

Why the fixture is deliberately ugly. Keys out of canonical order, mixed indentation, é where Jackson would emit a literal é, the signed BIGINT maximum, and no trailing newline. A well-formed record round-trips through Jackson unchanged, so a test using one passes against a publisher that re-encodes every record — it asserts field equivalence while claiming byte identity. The assertion was removed once and the test confirmed failing; an identity test that cannot fail is decoration.

Why it is not in docs/schemas/fixtures/. It is a *serialisation* fixture, not a schema-conformance one. That set is gated by validate-fixtures.py on count and MANIFEST.md entry, and a file whose entire purpose is its byte layout has no business in a directory somebody may reasonably reformat.

Why this matters beyond tidiness. ADR-005's duplicate outcome is ON CONFLICT (ts_device, uid) DO NOTHING. That is only correct if a redelivered record *is* the same record. Re-encoding at publish makes a replayed record a different sequence of bytes carrying the same identity — which still suppresses, but silently changes what was stored versus what was sent, and there is no longer any artifact that can prove which.

DL-120 — The inbound auth seam is a servlet filter, not Spring Security — yet

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · Q8a, Q8a-2, ADR-006, 2B-ANSWERS §3 · T-017

Decision. stats.input.auth.mode selects the mechanism, with no default in code. none registers a pass-through filter on the ingest path and WARNs on every startup; mtls and oauth2 fail while the context is building; the prod profile refuses none outright, in ProductionAuthModeGuard. spring-boot-starter-security is not added yet.

Why not the starter now. Adding it changes the default posture of the entire application — every actuator endpoint included, and the readiness probe docker-compose health-checks with it — before the mechanism Q8a-2 will choose is even known. What T-017 owes is the seam. A filter registered on one URL pattern is the smallest thing that is honestly a seam, and the starter arrives *with* the OAuth2 implementation, where its defaults can be argued against a real requirement.

Why an unimplemented mode fails at startup rather than per request. The alternative — accept oauth2, let requests through until someone implements it — produces an endpoint that reports itself as authenticated and is not. That is strictly worse than none, which is at least honest about what it is.

Why none occupies the seam rather than registering nothing. A mode that quietly registers no filter is indistinguishable, at every later reading, from a mode whose filter failed to register — and the difference between those two is whether the endpoint is open by decision or by accident.

Filter order is a decision: size (10) → auth (20) → rate limit (30). Size first because it is a header comparison that reads nothing. Rate limiting after authentication so that, once Q8a-2 lands, an unauthenticated caller cannot spend a legitimate sender's budget. None of the three touch /actuator/.

Risk restated, unchanged. Until Q8a-2 closes, anything that can reach the Input port can write statistics. Acceptable on a local dev stack and nowhere else — and now enforced by a context that will not build under prod.

DL-121 — Request limits are engineering defaults in properties, and 413/429 join the shared catalogue

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-06B, D-03, DL-109, contract §2 · T-018

Decision. stats.input.limits.max-request-bytes (256 KB) and stats.input.limits.max-requests-per-second (4,000, per instance) are enforced by two filters. ErrorCode gains PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE (413) and TOO_MANY_REQUESTS (429). The read timeout is server.tomcat.connection-timeout, not an application property.

Why now, with D-03 open. D-03 fixes no numbers — the contract's response matrix says 413 and "429 or 503, exact policy pending". An unbounded ingest endpoint is wrong at every value D-03 can return, so waiting was never the safe option. The values live in application.properties and the record carries no default of its own, so ZC's answer is a property change with a decision-log entry behind it.

The trap this avoids. The obvious implementation is server.tomcat.max-http-form-post-size, and it does nothing here: that limit applies to form content and Heimdal sends application/json. A limit that is configured, documented, and never fires is worse than no limit, because nobody looks at it again. Content-Length is checked before a byte is read; a chunked body has no declared length, so the stream is wrapped and PayloadTooLargeException travels out through IngestExceptionHandler — which also unwraps it from HttpMessageNotReadableException, because answering that 400 would tell Heimdal to fix a record that is not malformed.

Why one error catalogue and not two. Both codes appear only in the inbound contract, so an Input-local enum was the obvious alternative. Two catalogues means two type-URI bases, and a caller matching on type would have to know which module answered before it could parse the error. ErrorCode's javadoc naming only openapi.yaml is what made a second one look necessary — the javadoc was the bug.

Why the rate limit is per instance. The cluster ceiling is this value times the replica count, and that is the number to reason about when sizing against the 2× replay cap. A shared limiter puts a network round trip and a new dependency in front of every ingest. DL-109 records that Heimdal has agreed the cap and that we cannot enforce it: this filter and rule 8's 503 backpressure are the two controls we own. A 429 is a non-2xx, so the record is resent — the limit sheds load, it does not drop records.

requests_too_large and requests_rate_limited carry no type label, which is the one exception to the per-type rule in IngestMetrics. Both fire before the body is read, so the type is genuinely unknown rather than unreadable, and type="unknown" already means "we parsed and could not tell" — reusing it would merge a flood of oversized requests into the series an operator watches for malformed records.

DL-122 — The failure arm is built, and the copy is confirmed before the original is ACKed

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-12, STA-25, STA-25A, rule 5, rule 9, ADR-002 §4/§12, ADR-005, G1/G4 · T-024, T-020, T-057, T-060

Decision. stats-writer now has the whole failure arm. Five parts.

1. Three retry tiers and a per-type DLQ are declared — app_statsstore.<type>.retry.1..3 with the configured TTL, app_statsstore.<type>.dlq with no expiry of any kind, and app_stats_dlx carrying both journeys. 2. FailedDeliveryRouter publishes the onward copy and waits for its confirm. The consumer ACKs the original only after that call returns. If the copy cannot be confirmed the router throws, nothing is ACKed, and the broker keeps the only copy. 3. Attempts are counted in our own header, x-stats-attempt, not in RabbitMQ's x-death. 4. FailureClassifier sends permanent failures straight to the DLQ — an explicit PermanentFailureException, a Jackson parse failure, or a DataIntegrityViolationException. Everything else is retried. 5. Delays and the ceiling are properties — stats.amqp.retry.tier{1,2,3}-delay (30 s / 5 min / 30 min) and stats.amqp.max-retries=3.

The ordering is the decision, and it is the eleventh loss window. ACK-then-publish is the tempting order and it opens a gap in which the original is gone and the copy does not exist. A crash inside that gap loses the record with the broker, the database and Heimdal all reporting success — no exception, no counter, no queue depth, nothing. UnconfirmedHandoffException exists so the safe order cannot be undone by accident, and LorastatsConsumerTest asserts it as an *order* rather than as two calls that both happened.

Why our own attempt header. Half of this loop is broker-driven — a tier's TTL expiring dead-letters the message back to the main queue, which does append x-death — and half is ours, an explicit publish that does not. Counting attempts from a field only one half maintains gives a number that resets, and a record that never reaches the DLQ retries forever. The header is also why a stale x-death is stripped from the copy: two accounts of one history is worse than one.

Why permanent failures skip the tiers. 35½ minutes of queue time per record, and at 115.2m records/day a systematic permanent failure retried three times is three times the broker traffic and three times the delay before DLQ depth is actionable. The default runs the other way on purpose: anything not recognised as permanent is retried, because wrong-and-retry costs 35½ minutes and wrong-and-dead-letter costs a person, a root cause and a redrive.

A duplicate is not a failure. ON CONFLICT (ts_device, uid) DO NOTHING absorbs it and meters it long before an exception exists (ADR-005). Nothing in this arm sees one.

The body is never touched. The original bytes are republished verbatim; only headers change. ADR-004's byte-identical publication has to survive the retry loop, because a redriven record must still collide on (ts_device, uid) when it finally lands.

Two things this changed that were previously asserted the other way. LorastatsConsumerIntegrationTest.aProcessingFailureLeavesTheRecordWithTheBroker… was correct for the code that existed — with no arm, a failure stayed unacknowledged and was requeued on channel close, so the same record was redelivered forever. It now asserts the new contract. And stats-writer/application.properties no longer says the retry queues are undeclared.

Not closed by this. The payload gate (T-057) is still the missing producer of permanent failures — PermanentFailureException is the channel it will use. DLQ depth and oldest-message-age alerting is T-060, so the DLQ is real but nobody is watching it yet, and ADR-002 §12's 14-day disposition has no trigger until that lands.

DL-123 — T-032 is closed by construction, not by a two-context test

5 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · Q3a, ADR-002 §8/§11, trap 8 · T-032, T-002

Decision. Three parts, and together they are T-032.

1. AmqpTopology now owns the whole declaration, not just names and arguments. ExchangeDefinition and QueueDefinition carry durability, auto-delete and exclusivity as well. Both broker-connected modules build their Spring AMQP objects from them. 2. AmqpDeclarationAssert in stats-testsupport checks a declared object against its definition, and both modules' tests call it — one implementation, two modules. 3. stats-api asserts the absence: the AMQP client types are not loadable from its classpath in any scope.

Why not the obvious test. T-032 was written as a declaration-*conflict* test: start both modules against one broker and watch the second fail. It cannot be written that way here without a test-scope dependency from one application module to another, which claude/DEVELOPMENT.md §2 forbids for the good reason that test scope is one edit away from compile scope. Moving the values into shared definitions makes the conflict *impossible* rather than *detected*, which is strictly better: a detected conflict is still a conflict somebody has to diagnose.

What was actually wrong. Names and argument maps were already shared (T-002). The durability and auto-delete flags were not: each module passed them positionally into its own new TopicExchange(...) and new Queue(...). They are exactly as fatal — RabbitMQ rejects a mismatched declaration with RESOURCE_LOCKED and that module fails to start, in whichever environment happens to boot them in the wrong order, which is rarely the one where the change was made. Quorum sharpens it again: a queue's type is fixed at declaration, so correcting one means deleting and redeclaring it (ADR-002 §8).

Why the stats-api half is a classpath assertion. "No ConnectionFactory bean" can only be written by a test that can name the type, which means putting spring-amqp on that module's test classpath — and the moment it is there, someone can promote it to implementation and the test still passes. Asserting the class cannot be loaded fails the day the dependency appears.

Proved by hitting it. RetryAndDeadLetterIntegrationTest originally shortened the tier delays to one second and failed on the shared broker with inequivalent x-message-ttl arguments — the exact trap, met by the test written to prove the arm. It now uses the production delays and provokes exhaustion with an attempt header instead.

Residual. Four lines per module still turn a definition into a Spring object. Removing even those would mean either a broker client in stats-common — which its five responsibilities (D15) exclude — or a sixth module. The assertion covers them; the duplication is deliberate and bounded.

DL-126 — The payload gate is built, and it is not configurable

The failure arm built on 5 September (DL-122) was real and idle: nothing produced a permanent failure, so three retry tiers, a per-type DLQ and a confirmed hand-off existed with no producer. The gate is the producer. It is also the second half of the split ADR-007 §5 decided in August, and the half that was missing was the one with teeth — until now every payload reached the INSERT unvalidated and a mismatch surfaced as a *write* failure, classified transient, which spent 35½ minutes in the tiers before reaching the DLQ anyway.

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-03A, STA-12A, ADR-007 §5, DL-100, DL-122, DL-124 · T-057, T-064, T-063

Decision. stats-writer validates the per-type payload before the INSERT, through a type-agnostic PayloadGate that parses the body once, calls StatsSchemaValidator.validate(), binds the envelope and returns a ValidatedRecord. A failure throws PermanentFailureException, so the delivery skips all three retry tiers and reaches app_statsstore.<type>.dlq on its first attempt. Four things go with it:

1. There is no property that turns the gate off. stats.writer.validation .intended-scope is deleted, not promoted. It existed to record the decided design while the gate was unbuilt and was named "intended" precisely so it could not be mistaken for a live control (DL-100). ADR-007 §5 offers no second scope for a switch to select, and a key that can make "every payload reaches the INSERT unvalidated" true is a one-line change away from silently disabling a data-safety control. 2. StatsBatchWriter takes ValidatedRecord, not StatsEnvelope. The gate becomes a signature rather than a convention: a writer that accepted a bare envelope could be called with an unvalidated one. The schema label rides along into schema_version, which ADR-003 D7 wants to mean *the contract that admitted this row* — a compiled-in constant answers *the contract its author had in mind*, and the two agree only until a v2 exists, at which point every stored row claims the wrong one and nothing fails. 3. The exception message is a constant. SafeFailure's own javadoc named new PermanentFailureException("uid=… failed the schema") as the thing T-057 was expected to write, and DL-124 makes it impossible for a throwable to reach a stats-writer log line. The diagnosis is published separately: a WARN carrying the FieldError list, which is value-free by construction — JSON Pointers, machine codes and reviewed SafeMessage constants — plus the record itself, intact, on the DLQ. 4. records_payload_rejected{type,reason} is a new counter, separate from records_dead_lettered{type,reason}. That one is tagged with the root exception class, which cannot separate "the payload does not match its schema" from "a table invariant was violated" — and those call for different responses: the first is an upstream conversation, the second is ours. The reason tag was added on review, because the first cut made the same mistake one level down: it counted a routing fault — a record whose own type is not the type of the queue it arrived on — under a name that says "payload". Both throw PermanentFailureException, so records_dead_lettered sees one class name for both and this counter is the only place the two stay distinguishable. The tag set is closed at three constants — payload_schema, type_mismatch and unmappable — so it cannot grow into a cardinality problem the way a free-text reason would.

unmappable was the third correction, and it came from the same mistake a third time. records_write_failed is described as *"records whose write failed"* and its log line said write FAILED — but once parameters() was hoisted out of the transaction (see DL-127), a record in an unknown dialect never reaches PostgreSQL at all. Counting it as a write failure sends whoever watches that counter to a database that is behaving perfectly, while upstream quietly ships a shape nobody recognises. LorastatsBatchWriter.write now has two stages with two failure meanings: binding the row, which is payload work and is counted here, and the INSERT, which is the database's turn and keeps records_write_failed to itself. The regression test that pins it, countsAnUnmappablePayloadApartFromAFailedWrite, asserts both counters at once — asserting either alone passes for the wrong reason. metersAFailedWrite was rewritten at the same time: it used to prove this counter with a record missing a required member, which never reaches the database, so it agreed with itself while meaning two different things. It now uses a ts_device with no partition, which is a real rejection by PostgreSQL.

Why. The failure arm built on 5 September (DL-122) was real and idle: nothing produced a permanent failure, so three retry tiers, a per-type DLQ and a confirmed hand-off existed with no producer. The gate is the producer. It is also the second half of the split ADR-007 §5 decided in August, and the half that was missing was the one with teeth — until now every payload reached the INSERT unvalidated and a mismatch surfaced as a *write* failure, classified transient, which spent 35½ minutes in the tiers before reaching the DLQ anyway.

Why the gate is type-agnostic. Nothing in PayloadGate names a stat type. Rule 6 requires that adding a type be additive, and a gate written inside LorastatsMessageProcessor becomes five copies of itself during the Phase 3 fan-out — at which point the fifth copy is the one that quietly omits the check. The sysstats processor of T-035 is a sibling of the lorastats one, not a copy of its validation.

Why the body is parsed once. The same tree is validated and then bound. Parsing twice costs a second parse on each of 115.2m records a day, and — the part that matters — lets the tree that passed and the object that was written drift apart, so the row stored would not be the row that was validated.

Rejected. *Promoting intended-scope to a live key* — see rule 1; a scope switch on a gate whose only correct value is "on" is a foot-gun with a config file for a trigger. *Keeping write(List<StatsEnvelope>) and validating by convention* — the convention is exactly what was missing for three weeks. *Putting the field pointers in the exception message* — they are safe today, and the rule that stops a message reaching a log has to be absolute or it is a judgement call made again at every new throw site. *Validating at Input instead* — window 12 of FLOW.md §8: the record is already accepted, confirmed and published, and Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so a 400 there is unrecoverable loss.

Consequence. T-064 hangs on this code path and lands with it. T-063's rules 3 and 4 both need PermanentFailureException to be a live route to the DLQ, which is why the Tasks sheet recorded T-063 as depending on T-057 while Next Up ranked T-063 first: both were right about different halves, and the two shipped together. T-060 can now be sized — what reaches the lorastats DLQ from today is real failure, not routine dialect disagreement. The Writer's ERROR line no longer claims a failed write "goes to the retry arm": a permanent one does not.

DL-127 — All three lorastats dialects are built, and the fourth still dead-letters

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING implementing ANSWERED (Q15a) · DL-105, DL-089 (superseded), ADR-007 §4 (amended), DL-113, DL-095 · T-063, T-060, T-062

Decision. DL-105 is implemented. docs/schemas/lorastats-v1.schema.json becomes a oneOf of exactly three dialect branches, and LorastatsDialects normalises all three into one canonical payload before LorastatsBatchWriter maps a single column. Each of DL-105's four rules is now a mechanism rather than a sentence:

1. Structural detection. Which required key-set is present — bare or lora_-prefixed — and what type its values hold. VersionIsNotADiscriminatorTest (DL-113) was written before this code and forbids the new file from naming the bare identifier at all; it passes with no new whitelist entry, which was the acceptance criterion. 2. One stored shape. Proved in the table, not asserted in prose: allThreeDialectsLandAsOneIdenticalRow writes the same counters in three dialects against real PostgreSQL and compares the rows. The dialect is the metric label records_lorastats_dialect{dialect} — whose name is composed from StatType.wireName(), because a "lorastats" literal in main source fails StatTypeFanOutTest — and all three series are registered at startup so a dialect going to zero in the field is visible rather than absent. 3. Range-checked coercion, in that order. A dialect-B string is parsed to BigInteger, compared against Long.MAX_VALUE, and only then narrowed. Overflow or a bad parse dead-letters. Never zeroed, never truncated, never defaulted. 4. Closed at three. A fourth spelling matches no branch and dead-letters, in the schema and independently in the detector.

The detail the prose loses, and the reason to read the fixture first. Dialect C is not "everything prefixed". The production record of 29 August carries fifteen lora_-prefixed counters alongside a bare fsk_received and a bare timespan. A branch or a detector written from DL-105's description rather than from the record rejects the traffic it was written to accept. Both now encode the record.

Normalisation happens before the column mapping, and that ordering is load-bearing. data_extra is computed from the *normalised* residue. Taken from the wire shape instead, every dialect-C record would drop fifteen lora_* members into data_extra, leave the typed columns NULL, and increment records_unmapped_data_field — a counter whose entire job is to say "Heimdal added a field" — on ordinary traffic every ten minutes. The row would be valid JSON, the write would succeed, and the defect would surface as a column that is always null. aPrefixedDialectRecordFillsColumnsAndLeavesDataExtraNull is the guard.

One round trip is deliberately no longer byte-exact. A dialect-B or dialect-C record rebuilds from storage with bare names and integral values. ADR-004's byte-identical guarantee is about publication — what Input republishes and what the retry arm hands on, which is what keeps (ts_device, uid) suppression correct — and storage was always value-preserving rather than encoding-preserving (ADR-003 D2). Rule 2 is what makes one API shape possible for a type that arrives in three.

The fixture gate learned a third marker. writer-range-reject marks a record the catalogue admits and the Writer refuses on the BIGINT range check. Neither existing marker fits: writer-dlq means "fails its per-type schema" and this record does not. It is to the Writer what input-reject is to Input — both mark a rule that is arithmetic or cross-field rather than structural, and DL-105 rule 3 had no assertion vocabulary until now. EXPECTED_ASSERTIONS moves 88 → 91 and the Java parity expansion 81 → 84; the marker count stayed at seven while its composition changed completely.

Rejected. *anyOf instead of oneOf* — the three branches are disjoint by name and value type, so an overlap is a modelling error, and oneOf surfaces it as a validation failure instead of silently picking the first match. *Typing dialect B's timespan as either a string or an integer* — no production record exercises it, a dialect is internally consistent by definition, and a mixed record is a shape nobody has explained; rule 4 sends those to the DLQ where a person sees them. *Letting the detector fall back to a "best guess" dialect* — a record neither the schema nor the detector can place belongs on the DLQ, not in a column chosen by a fallback. *Stripping lora_ from unrecognised members* — renaming an unknown field on the way to a column that does not exist is inventing a mapping; it keeps its spelling and goes to data_extra verbatim (D14b).

Consequence. Roughly 15m messages/day stop accumulating in a queue ADR-002 §12 forbids ever expiring. The two production dialect fixtures move invalid/ → valid/ and lose their writer-dlq markers; invalid/lorastats-dialect-unknown.json and valid/lorastats-dialect-string-overflow.json take their place at the two boundaries the widened schema creates. T-060 is unblocked and can now be sized against real volume. T-062 inherits the coercion helper rather than writing a second one — sysstats' timespan: "300" and netstats' 19-digit counter strings are the same rule applied to other types, and two helpers written a month apart is how the range check goes missing from one of them.

Residual, unchanged. Q15a's second half — *are the other dialects being retired?* — is still unanswered and still blocks nothing. records_lorastats_dialect{dialect} is what will show one going to zero, and it is now emitting.

DL-128 — A non-OK upstream status is metered at the gate, and absence is not a failure

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING implementing ANSWERED (Q15d) · DL-108, DL-102, ADR-007 §1, D14 · T-064, T-050

Decision. DL-108 is implemented in PayloadGate, immediately after validation and before the write: when status is present and is anything other than exactly ["conversion ok"], increment records_upstream_status_not_ok{type} and log at WARN with uid, type, clsbox and the status array. The record is stored regardless and is never dead-lettered. status stays a passthrough member — this reads it to count it, and nothing branches on it.

An absent status is not counted, and that is the decision DL-108 left open. Read literally, "anything other than exactly ["conversion ok"]" includes absence. But status is optional, and every fixture written before 29 August 2026 omits it — so counting absence would light the counter on ordinary traffic and drown the signal it exists to carry. *"Upstream did not say"* and *"upstream said it failed"* are different facts, and only the second is what Q15d asked for. Recording it here rather than leaving it to the code is the point: it is exactly the kind of unstated default that DL-108's own value-agnostic argument warns about.

Why the comparison is against the one known-good value. We do not have the vocabulary of failure strings. A check that recognises only the values we happen to know about goes quiet on the ones we do not, which is the failure mode the whole "nothing is silently dropped" rule exists to prevent. Comparing against the single good value inverts it: anything unrecognised is counted, by definition. metersAStatusValueNobodyHasSeenBefore is the test that holds that property, using a value no production record has sent.

Why it is at the gate rather than in the writer. One place, once per delivery, with the whole envelope in hand and before any transaction opens. Putting it in LorastatsBatchWriter would repeat it five times across the Phase 3 fan-out, and putting it after the INSERT would miss the records suppressed as duplicates.

Rejected. *Dead-lettering a non-OK status* — the record is valid and was correctly delivered, and a redrive produces the identical verdict forever, in a DLQ with no TTL. *A metric label carrying the status value* — an unbounded label set sourced from an upstream vocabulary we do not control is a Prometheus cardinality bomb. *Waiting for the vocabulary before metering* — the counter does not need it, and the counter is what will name the actual values. *Logging the payload alongside it* — status is an envelope member, which DL-108 names explicitly; the DL-102 level gate that keeps payloads at DEBUG does not extend to it, and does not become weaker because of this line.

Consequence. T-050's alert set gains the counter — a Mukesh-owned hand-off, not a change here. valid/lorastats-status-not-ok.json is a new fixture and carries no $expect marker, because nothing anywhere rejects it. Without this we would store records upstream already knew were bad with nothing on our side saying so, which is the whole of Q15d.

Residual, unchanged. The vocabulary of non-OK status values is still unknown. It blocks nothing, the counter is value-agnostic, and by the next round with Hasan the counter will have enumerated the real values in any case.

DL-132 — Retention is detach-and-drop, and every run begins by repairing the last one

The alternative to partitioning was never on the table — claude/CLAUDE.md §6 lists row-by-row deletion at 115.2m records/day as a trap, and D1 chose RANGE (ts_device) partly so retention could be a catalogue operation. What T-025 adds is the discovery that the mechanism has a failure mode which *presents as nothing at all*, and a class that assumed the previous run finished would have inherited it.

10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-003 D1/D3/D4, STA-23A, STA-23C, DL-096, DL-130 · T-025, T-031, T-051, T-054, T-055

Decision. PartitionRetention implements ADR-003 D1: for every daily partition wholly older than the retention window, ALTER TABLE … DETACH PARTITION … CONCURRENTLY followed by DROP TABLE. Never row deletion. It runs once daily on the existing stats.retention.schedule, under the same pg_try_advisory_lock pre-creation already uses (D3), and stats.retention.default-days=90 is un-parked and bound at last (DL-130 predicted this).

Four things are decided alongside the mechanism.

One maintenance pass, in one order. PartitionMaintenance.maintainOnSchedule() runs pre-creation and then retention, per type, and PartitionMaintenanceScheduler is the only @Scheduled entry point. Two scheduled methods on one cron expression would have run in whatever order Spring registered them, and the order is load-bearing: tomorrow's partition is created before yesterday's is dropped.

Every run finalises an abandoned detach first. This is the part that is not in ADR-003, because it is a property of PostgreSQL rather than of the design, and it was found by probing PostgreSQL 17 before the class was written:

* ALTER TABLE … DETACH PARTITION … CONCURRENTLY cannot run inside a transaction block — a hard ERROR. PartitionSession forces auto-commit for it, and nothing in the sweep opens a transaction. * An interrupted detach leaves pg_inherits.inhdetachpending = true, and from that moment every DETACH CONCURRENTLY on that parent fails with partition "…" already pending detach. * Meanwhile INSERT and CREATE TABLE … PARTITION OF keep working perfectly.

So a run killed by a rolling deploy, an OOM or a statement timeout wedges retention for that stat type *permanently*, ingestion looks completely healthy, and the only symptom is disk. That is a twelfth entry in the shape of FLOW.md §8, and it is silent. Beginning each run with DETACH … FINALIZE turns it into a cost of one day.

The gap between the detach and the drop is swept. After the detach the table still exists, standalone, still holding its rows, with relispartition false. A crash there leaks a day of storage that nothing would ever reclaim, so the sweep drops leftovers it can name, date and place outside the window — counted as retention_orphans_dropped, its own series rather than a tag, because a non-zero value means an earlier run *died* and an ordinary drop counter would read as retention working.

Nothing is dropped that has not been proved droppable. The name must be one PartitionNaming produced, the catalogue bound must be exactly the bound that name implies, and the day must be wholly outside the window. A hand-made child, a name that does not parse, a bound that disagrees — all logged and kept. Dropping is irreversible short of a per-partition restore (D5), so the safe direction is to retain something that could have gone, never the reverse.

Why. The alternative to partitioning was never on the table — claude/CLAUDE.md §6 lists row-by-row deletion at 115.2m records/day as a trap, and D1 chose RANGE (ts_device) partly so retention could be a catalogue operation. What T-025 adds is the discovery that the mechanism has a failure mode which *presents as nothing at all*, and a class that assumed the previous run finished would have inherited it.

RetentionProperties gains three things and one of them is a change of shape. default-days has no default in code: retention deletes data, so a deployment that omits the key must fail to start rather than adopt a window somebody guessed — the same reasoning that leaves stats.input.auth.mode undefaulted. Per-type overrides become stats.retention.days-by-type.<wire-name>, replacing the recorded-but-never-live stats.retention.<type>-days; the map is validated against StatType.wireName() at startup, so a typo fails the context instead of silently reverting that type to 90 days. That is DL-100, DL-129 and DL-130's shared lesson applied *before* the key goes live rather than after. stagger implements D3's "stagger the five types" as an offset derived from the type, so one mechanism covers both shapes: five deployments firing on one cron in production, and five passes inside one process in all mode.

Rejected. *A stats.retention.enabled kill switch* — schedule=- already stops the cron, and an unrequested off-switch on a mechanism ADR-003 makes mandatory is the intended-scope hazard again (DL-100). *A cap on partitions dropped per run* — a detach and a drop are catalogue operations, and a cap would leave partitions past the window attached with nothing saying so. *Letting retention discover partitions by parsing pg_get_expr alone* — the bound is verified, but the name is what makes a partition recognisable as ours; accepting anything whose bound merely looks like a day would put a hand-made table in scope. *Copying the lock and session protocol into a second class* — D3 names one lock, and two spellings of hashtext('stats.retention:' || …) that drifted would leave the two jobs mutually invisible, running concurrently and mostly getting away with it. PartitionSession is extracted so the key cannot disagree with itself.

Consequence. 523 tests, 0 failures. PartitionRetentionIntegrationTest proves the mechanism against real PostgreSQL 17 — including the pending-detach state, staged by cancelling a detach that a second session is blocking, which is the only way to prove the recovery exists. Every drop in that class runs through DETACH CONCURRENTLY, so a sweep that ever acquired a transaction would fail the suite rather than degrade quietly. T-051, T-054 and T-055 are unblocked — they measure what this built. PartitionPrecreator keeps its public API, so T-012's tests are unchanged; PartitionPrecreationScheduler is renamed PartitionMaintenanceScheduler, because a class that now runs retention as well should not be named for half of what it does.

DL-133 — A record past its window is skipped and ACKed, and the sweep and the guard share one cutoff

10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING implementing SIGNED (D5b) · DL-112, DL-106, ADR-003 option B, STA-23B, ADR-002 §12 · T-026, T-031, T-050, T-052

Decision. D5b is implemented. RetentionGuard runs between the payload gate and the write: a record whose ts.device is already past the retention window is skipped, records_expired_on_arrival{type} is incremented, a WARN is logged carrying envelope members only, and the delivery is acknowledged. It is never dead-lettered.

The guard and the sweep read one cutoff, and that is the decision inside the decision. RetentionWindow is the single definition; PartitionRetention and RetentionGuard both derive from it. Two independent calculations would fail in opposite directions and only one of them noisily: a guard even a day more permissive than the sweep admits a record into a partition retention has just dropped, the INSERT fails, the record dead-letters, and every redrive re-derives the identical verdict forever in a queue ADR-002 §12 forbids ever expiring. Written as one function, the disagreement is unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely.

The arithmetic leans deliberately: cutoffDay = today(UTC) − retentionDays, a record is expired when ts.device < cutoffDay 00:00Z, and a partition is droppable when its day is *before* cutoffDay. The boundary belongs to the retained side on both sides of the comparison. Over-retaining by one day costs one partition; under-retaining deletes records still inside the window the business asked for, and nothing would report it.

Why it returns false where PayloadGate throws. The two gates differ in consequence, so they differ in signature. A payload failure throws PermanentFailureException, which is this module's channel to the DLQ. This one has to end in a broker ACK, and an exception is the one thing that cannot produce one — LorastatsConsumer would hand it to FailedDeliveryRouter instead.
