Making the two look alike is how the skip would eventually acquire a dead-letter it must not have.

Why the gate runs first and the guard second. A record that is both too old *and* malformed dead-letters, where the schema mismatch can be diagnosed, rather than being discarded as merely late. The reverse order silently swallows the more informative of the two failures.

The five late-arrival fixtures were wrong, and quietly. edge/<type>-late-arrival.json each said the record is *"DLQ'd without retry"* — the opposite of the signed policy — and that the policy was *"still pending product sign-off"*, which stopped being true on 4 Sep 2026. Worse, none carried an $expect marker, so all five asserted only "the schema accepts this" and said nothing about a signed, deliberate data-loss path. That is precisely the gap input-reject was invented to close for the clock-skew fixtures, where an undecided question had "accept" baked into ten green assertions. A fourth marker, writer-skip, now states it: schema-valid, accepted by Input, published, consumed, and then deliberately not stored. Markers 7 → 12, assertions 91 → 96, and no fixture added, moved or removed.

Why "never dead-letter" is worth this much text. The record is *valid*. A dead-letter would describe it wrongly, occupy a queue with no TTL and manual disposition, and be rejected on every redrive because the record can only have got older. It is also trap 18 read from the other end: a DLQ message redriven after roughly ninety days lands in this class, increments this counter and stores nothing — a no-op that looks like a recovery. The redrive runbook (T-052) has to say so outright.

Rejected. *A DEFAULT catch-all partition* (ADR-003 option A) — degrades pruning and grows unwatched. *Clamping ts.device to the oldest live partition* (option C) — falsifies the time series. *Silently dropping the record* — this is deliberate data loss by a signed decision, which is exactly why it is metered and logged rather than quiet. *Rate-limiting the WARN* — a flood of warnings about a systemic gateway backlog is the correct noise, and a stateful suppressor is the kind of cleverness that hides the second incident. *Putting the counter in WriterGateMetrics* — that class answers "what was wrong with this record", and nothing is wrong with this one.

Consequence. 523 tests, 0 failures. LorastatsPersistenceConsumerIntegrationTest.aRecordPastItsRetentionWindowIsSkippedAndAckedRatherThanDeadLettered proves it end to end against a real broker and a real database: the counter moves, the main queue drains, the DLQ stays empty, and no row is written. RetentionWindowTest asserts the boundary at the exact second on both sides — the assertion T-031 asks for, alongside the integration test that proves the same boundary through a dropped partition. The alert is not built. The counter is exported and registered at zero from startup, but nothing watches it; that is T-050, and it is the half of D5b that separates a systemic backlog from a harmless one-off.

DL-136 — Meter delivery attempts separately from committed inserts

10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-27A, STA-25A, ADR-005 · T-027

*Written as DL-128, renumbered on merge: main had meanwhile assigned DL-128 to the upstream-status metering (T-064).*

Decision. The listener counts records_consumed{type} before decoding and times each attempt as records_processing_duration{type} through processing, any confirmed failure hand-off and the ACK attempt — recorded on failure exits too, so a redelivery counts again. Persistence increments records_persisted{type} only after the transaction returns with an inserted row; a duplicate stays its own outcome. records_postgres_errors{type} separates database-access and transaction failures from the broader records_write_failed{type} by inspecting only exception *types* in a bounded cause chain — no driver message becomes a label. Neither exception messages nor record identifiers become metric labels.

The measurement boundaries are recorded in docs/contracts/writer-metrics-v1.md.

DL-137 — Broker observations are polled and cached; a scrape performs no I/O

10 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-27A, NFR-05, DL-104, DL-132, DL-133 · T-027, T-050, T-060

*Written as DL-129, renumbered on merge: main had meanwhile assigned DL-129 to the configuration audit.*

Decision. OperationalMetrics exposes per-type queue_depth, dlq_depth and consumer_availability from passive AMQP queue inspection — no management-plugin credential, no new dependency — read on stats.metrics.poll-interval and cached, so a Prometheus scrape reads memory. A failed observation writes NaN, drops broker_metrics_collection_success and leaves broker_metrics_last_success_timestamp_seconds where it was: a queue the broker cannot report on and an empty queue must never read the same, and an alert has to check freshness before treating a zero as healthy. Replica observations describe one shared resource — aggregate with max, never sum. dlq_depth is the series T-060 watches. retention_period_days{type} is read from RetentionWindow.

What the branch built and the merge removed — and why. T-027 was cut before T-025 and T-026 landed, and it carried its own answers to both:

* A second retention cutoff, RecordRetentionPolicy: now − days as an instant, with the boundary on the *expired* side ("at or before"), reading the retired stats.retention.<type>-days keys. DL-133 exists because two cutoffs that drift by a day admit a record into a partition the sweep has just dropped, and this one differed from RetentionWindow in both the day and the boundary direction. Dropped; retention_period_days reads the one definition instead. * A late-arrival skip inside LorastatsMessageProcessor, and a type != expectedType check beside it. Both already exist on main as RetentionGuard (DL-133) and the PayloadGate type_mismatch reason (DL-126), in the order that matters — gate, then guard — so a late record that is *also* malformed dead-letters where it can be diagnosed rather than being discarded as merely late. Dropped as duplicates. * PartitionLagReader and retention_lag_seconds, a catalogue query on a 30-second poll. DL-132 rejected exactly this in favour of the sweep-reported retention_oldest_partition_age_days: a polled gauge keeps reporting a healthy number from a replica whose sweep never ran, and it hard-coded the public schema. Dropped.

What survives is what T-027 was for: the broker gauges and the delivery/persistence counters, which T-050 and T-060 need and which nothing on main provided. Main-queue depth remains a diagnostic, not the oldest-message-age alert T-050 requires — under jitter (DL-104) a cyclic depth spike is evidence the jitter answer is wrong, not the buffer working.

DL-138 — Manual DLQ redrive gets one processing attempt, never fresh retry tiers

11 September 2026 · T-052 · STA-12A, ADR-002 §12

A dead-letter queue is inert: no application component consumes or redrives it automatically. Records remain available with their original bytes and safe failure metadata until an authorised operator explicitly redrives or disposes of them. A redrive requires a change identifier, dedicated per-type credentials and an explicit assertion that the root cause has been resolved.

The manual copy preserves x-stats-attempt, x-stats-failure and x-stats-permanent, adds traceable redrive metadata, and receives one processing attempt. x-stats-manual-redrive=true makes any repeat failure bypass all retry tiers and return directly to the same type's DLQ. This marker is necessary because permanent failures can reach the DLQ on attempt one; preserving only the attempt count would let a transient repeat failure enter tier two.

The original DLQ delivery is ACKed only after the main-queue copy is publisher-confirmed. The operation is bounded, per type and capped at 2,667 records/second. Records at or beyond the 90-day device-time retention boundary are refused because their processing outcome would be expired_on_arrival, not database recovery. The complete access, audit, pilot, stop and verification procedure is docs/runbooks/dlq-redrive.md.

DL-145 — The Heimdal inbound contract gets an OpenAPI twin, and both contracts render onto one reviewable page

18 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-007 §5, DL-117, DL-118…DL-121, DL-139 · STA-01…STA-06, T-014…T-018

Trigger. Management confirmed on 17 September 2026 that Heimdal delivers over HTTP — the transport the endpoint was built for — and asked what the Stats App provides for ZC to configure. The answer existed, but not in a shape anyone could hand over: heimdal-input-contract-v1.md still carried its August placeholders (*"path — confirm against the deployed Datahub convention"*, *"200 or 202, exact code pending"*, *"replay rate — our proposal, not yet agreed"*), the read API had a linted OpenAPI contract while the ingest side had only prose, and DL-117 had decided the rendered HTML is generated rather than committed — which meant, in practice, that nobody ever generated it.

Decision.

1. docs/contracts/openapi-input.yaml is the machine-readable inbound contract: the single POST /stats operation, every status code stats-input is built to return (200, 400 with its field-code catalogue, 413, 429, 503, and 401/403 reserved for Q8a-2), the X-Trace-Id header on every response, and the three candidate security schemes under security: []. It $refs the read contract's Envelope and ProblemDetails rather than restating them, so the envelope is described once. Problem examples use the type URI ErrorCode.typeUri() actually emits (https://stats.example/errors/<wire_code>, underscore and all), because Input is built and these are the bytes Heimdal will receive. The file is linted in CI under redocly.yaml with one recorded suppression, no-unused-components, because an unreferenced candidate scheme is the contract being accurate about a deferred decision, not a leftover; the suppression is removed the day Q8a-2 selects a scheme. 2. One page, two sources. docs/contracts/build-html.sh renders both YAMLs into stats-api-v1-draft.html — redocly join (dedupes the shared refs, refuses to guess on a genuine clash: three responses were renamed Ingest* for it), a small merge-info.py (join keeps only the *first* info, which would have dropped the read API's principles from the page), then redocly build-docs, all pinned at 2.51.2. The read API is *not* folded into the ingest YAML and vice versa: the SCOPE notes in each file — different caller, different authentication, no shared version segment — still hold. ZC is the audience for *both* confirmations, which is why they get one document. 3. The HTML stays uncommitted (DL-117 stands). CI builds it on every push and uploads it as the stats-api-v1-draft workflow artifact, so a reviewer without Node downloads it from the run. The output is byte-deterministic for a given input (verified: two consecutive builds cmp equal), so a future drift gate is a one-line addition if the decision is ever reversed. 4. heimdal-input-contract-v1.md §0 "Handover" is the table ZC fills in: URL, success code, retry/never-retry sets, limits, replay cap, credential, ForwardRule maxRetry. Each row says *built*, *PROVISIONAL* or OPEN, and only Q8a-2/Q8a-3 are open. §5's replay paragraph is corrected — it still said *"our proposal, not yet agreed"* fourteen days after DL-109 recorded Heimdal's agreement.

What the transport confirmation does and does not settle. HTTP is what the endpoint was built for, so nothing in code changes. It narrows Q8a-3 rather than closing it: a Heimdal ForwardRule of type HTTP is now the likely mechanism, and if so the credential is basic-in-URL (a mode stats.input.auth.mode does not offer) and retry is bounded by maxRetry. The handover table asks both questions in one row so they are answered together, as DL-139 intended.

Rejected. *One YAML for both APIs* — would put a POST into a contract whose first principle is "GET only, ever", and would tie the ingest path's release cadence to API consumers'. *Committing the HTML* — reverses DL-117 for a 350 KB file that changes on every contract edit; the workflow artifact gives the same reviewer the same file. *Inlining the Redoc viewer* to make the page fully offline — the prerendered text is readable without it; only the sidebar and expanders need cdn.redocly.com, and carrying a 1 MB vendored script for that is not worth it for an internal draft.

One inconsistency found and not fixed here. The read contract's problem examples use hyphenated type URIs (…/errors/invalid-time-range) where stats-common's ErrorCode.typeUri() emits underscores (…/errors/invalid_time_range). The read API is Phase 4 and not built, so today this is contract-versus-library drift, not contract-versus-behaviour; it is recorded here so the Phase 4 controllers are written against one spelling and the examples corrected in that change, not discovered by a client matching on type.

DL-147 — The main queues are watched: oldest-message age is sampled at delivery from an Input-stamped timestamp, and the Q13b histogram lives in Input

19 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-002 §12, ADR-003 D5b, DL-104, DL-112, DL-128, DL-137, DL-144 · STA-27A, NFR-05 · T-050

Trigger. T-060 closed the DLQ half of "nobody is watching" (DL-144) and recorded the other half as owed: the main queues, records_expired_on_arrival (D5b's signed obligation), records_upstream_status_not_ok (the DL-128 residual), and the ts.server mod 600 histogram DL-104 made the precondition for sizing hardware down. The task brief said every series T-050 reads was built by T-027. Three of the four were; the fourth was not, and writer-metrics-v1.md said so in as many words: main-queue depth "is a diagnostic, not the oldest-message-age alert T-050 requires". DL-144 named the missing precondition — "a broker-side timestamp on every message". So T-050 is one new series, one histogram, two counters and seven Prometheus rules, not a rules-only task.

Decision 1 — Input stamps the standard AMQP timestamp property on every publish, and the retry/DLQ copy carries it forward. A property, not a header: it rides with the copies the way correlationId does (DL-134), and the body stays byte-identical (ADR-004). FailedDeliveryRouter.copyWithHeaders now preserves it — one line, and the half of DL-144's precondition that makes a broker-side DLQ age reader possible later without a second change to what is published.

Decision 2 — queue_oldest_message_age_seconds{type} is sampled at delivery, and the poll zeroes it on an empty queue. app_statsstore.<type> is FIFO, so the delivery the Writer is about to process *was* the oldest ready message when the broker handed it over; its age at listener entry is the queue's oldest-message age at that moment. QueueAgeMetrics records it; OperationalMetrics registers the gauge for every served type (NaN until observed, for DL-137's reason) and sets it to zero whenever a poll finds no ready message — an empty queue has no oldest message, and without the reset the gauge would hold the last backlog value through a quiet hour. Two exclusions, both deliberate: a delivery carrying x-stats-attempt is a retry re-entry whose age *is* the tier delay by design, and counting it would make every transient database failure look like the Writer falling behind; a delivery with no timestamp — published by something other than Input, or by an Input from before this change — is skipped rather than guessed at. Negative ages (host clock skew) clamp to zero.

*Rejected: reading head_message_timestamp from the management API.* It is the truer measurement — it moves while the consumer is absent — and it needs a management credential and an HTTP client in the Writer, which DL-137 chose not to take on. The stamp is the part of that design that costs nothing and is needed either way; the reader can be added later against the same stamp, and the DLQ would then get its age from it. *Rejected: scraping RabbitMQ's own Prometheus plugin.* A second scrape target with per-object metrics, outside this repository's contract, whose behaviour for paged-out messages nobody here could verify.

Decision 3 — the gauge's blind spot is covered by rules, not by inventing a value. A gauge sampled at delivery stops moving when deliveries stop — the objection DL-137 raised against a polled retention gauge applies here too. ops/prometheus/writer-alerts.yml therefore reads the age together with what the poll *does* know: StatsAppMainQueueAging (warning, > 300 s for 5 m — a fresh record has waited longer than tier 2's delay) and StatsAppMainQueueAgingCritical (> 1800 s, longer than the longest tier) for the running-but-behind case; StatsAppMainQueueNoConsumer (consumer_availability == 0 and the poll succeeded, 2 m, critical) for the absent consumer; StatsAppConsumerStalled (queue_depth > 0 and no records_consumed increase in 10 m, critical) for the registered-but-wedged one; and StatsAppBrokerObservationStale (collection success 0 for 5 m) because a NaN compares false to everything and the other four go *quiet* rather than wrong when the poll fails. The three rules on the gauge are one alert; the README says to read them together. Thresholds are engineering defaults, recorded as such.

Decision 4 — late arrival alerts on the hourly increase; upstream status on a PROVISIONAL ratio. D5b says "alert when non-zero"; a counter is non-zero forever once it has moved, so StatsAppLateArrival fires on increase(records_expired_on_arrival_total[1h]) > 0 — one late record is one alert, a fleet doing it every cycle keeps it firing — and its annotation names the ~90-day redrive trap (DL-138). StatsAppUpstreamStatusNotOk is a one-hour ratio of not-OK to consumed above 1 %, sustained for an hour, and the 1 % is written as provisional in the rule, the test and the README: DL-139 found ["ok"] on documents the value-agnostic counter would count, so the baseline may be non-zero and the number is set from the first 24 h of ingest, per the DL-128 residual. It is *not* set at zero, and it is not left unbuilt waiting for the number.

Decision 5 — the ts.server mod 600 histogram is Input's, not the Writer's. records_ts_server_cycle_offset_seconds{type}: twenty cumulative 30-second buckets across the cycle, recorded on *acceptance* (after the publisher confirm, so a 503-then- resend counts once). Input because it sees every type from day one, while the Writer is deployed per type (ADR-003 D8) and today serves two of five — five partial views to sum would answer the wrong question about hardware. Under jitter each bucket holds ~5 %; a synchronised fleet fills one or two. The README gives the query. In the same place, records_ts_device_corrected{type} counts DL-146's meta.tsDeviceCorrected flag — the number to put beside Q15f — and GateResult.Accepted carries tsServer and the flag out of the gate so nothing re-parses the body.

What changed in the lorastats slice, and why it is allowed. LorastatsConsumer takes QueueAgeMetrics and calls it once at entry. That is T-050 adding a cross-cutting observation to the consumption path, which the reference slice defines; every sibling copies the line. It is not the T-035 smell (DL-148), which is a *new type* editing an existing one.

Verification. promtool check rules and test rules (pinned prom/prometheus:v3.5.0) over eleven scenarios: healthy, warning-not-critical, critical-and-warning, replica max, NaN-never-fires plus stale-observation, no-consumer, stalled-versus-quiet, late-arrival fires-then-clears, ratio breach after the pending hour, and baseline-below-threshold with zero-traffic not dividing to an alert. QueueAgeMetricsTest, the poll-reset case in OperationalMetricsTest, delivery sampling in both consumer unit tests, the scrape in ManagementSurfaceTest, IngestMetricsTest for the bucket edges, and the publisher, gate and controller tests for the stamp and the two Input observations. CI runs both rule files.

DL-149 — Partition pre-creation covers the whole retention window, not only the days ahead

20 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-23A, STA-23B, STA-23C, STA-25A, ADR-003 D1/D3, DL-096, DL-132, DL-133 · found by the first end-to-end demo against the compose stack

Trigger. The first demo posted the production-dated fixtures (27 Aug 2026, 23 days old) into a stack rebuilt that morning. Input returned 200 for every one; the Writer dead-lettered every one at attempt=1, permanent=true with SQLSTATE 23514 — PostgreSQL's *no partition of relation … found for row*. Pre-creation was forward only: [today, today + pre-create-days]. RetentionGuard only handles records *past* the window. A valid record inside the window but before the deployment's first day — a gateway back from three weeks offline, the path §6 of CLAUDE.md and STA-23B call normal — had nowhere to go, and DataIntegrityViolationException is permanent, so it went straight to a DLQ that never expires, where a redrive re-derives the identical failure. records_persisted stayed at zero and records_write_failed moved; nothing said *why*, and the row it names is the one ops most wants after an outage.

Decision. Every maintenance pass — the startup one (DL-096) and the daily one — ensures partitions for [cutoffDay, today + pre-create-days], both ends from RetentionWindow. PartitionMaintenance computes the range; PartitionPrecreator gains no new behaviour, only a wider bound (MAX_ENSURED_DAYS). This makes the RetentionWindow contract symmetric: the guard admits exactly the days that have a partition, and the sweep drops strictly before the same day, so the two halves of one pass cannot fight over a partition even when the pass straddles midnight UTC.

Why this and not the alternatives. *Lazy creation on the first 23514* was already rejected by DL-096 — DDL on the consume path, racing across replicas, in the one code path that is already failing. *A DEFAULT partition* was rejected by ADR-003's late-arrival analysis — it degrades pruning and grows unwatched. *Treating 23514 as transient* would push the record through three tiers to the same DLQ, slower. *Doing nothing because production will have grown its own history* is wrong twice: the window before first deployment is 90 days of exactly this loss, and a scheduled run missed for longer than pre-create-days leaves a permanent hole afterwards — which the daily backfill now closes on the next pass. The cost is ~retention-days empty partitions per type, each a catalogue entry with two empty indexes; pruning ignores them and the sweep drops them on schedule. Empty partitions are free; a lost record is a lost record.

What the demo does instead, and why it is not the fix. docker/demo.sh still re-stamps the valid fixtures to *now*, because a fixture dated 27 Aug 2026 will be past the window by late November and the demo has to keep working. It also posts one record re-stamped to ts.device = now − 21 d, which is the DL-149 case demonstrated on purpose.

Tests. PartitionMaintenanceTest now verifies the exact range handed to the precreator — cutoff day, retentionDays + preCreateDays — including under a host zone twelve hours ahead of UTC. PartitionBootstrapIntegrationTest boots the real module against real PostgreSQL with a 30-day window and asserts 38 partitions, that a record measured 21 days ago inserts on a deployment minutes old, and that the cutoff day itself is retained. 73 retention tests, 0 failures.

Residual. None open. One observation: the fixture set's 29 Aug production timestamps are now a *feature* of the demo rather than a hazard — but any new integration test that posts them raw into a fresh context depends on this entry.

DL-163 — Ingest success includes a JSON acknowledgement after publisher confirmation

6 October 2026 · `INPUT` · STA-01A, STA-04A, STA-06A, DL-118, DL-134, DL-151

Trigger. Mukesh observed an empty HTTP 200 response while ingesting a record in Postman and requested an explicit acceptance status. The existing response relied on the HTTP code and X-Trace-Id alone.

Decision. Both /internal/stats and /external/stats keep one handler and return application/json with {"status":"accepted","traceId":"<X-Trace-Id>"} only after PublishOutcome.CONFIRMED. The status remains configurable as 200 (default) or 202; both codes return the same body. The per-request trace ID is the existing header and AMQP correlation ID. Failed validation and unconfirmed publication keep their existing status codes and Problem Details responses. Publication remains byte-identical.

Why. An explicit acknowledgement makes a successful handoff clear in Postman and provides a correlation ID in the response body. “accepted” describes RabbitMQ confirmation precisely: Writer validation, retention handling and database commits happen asynchronously and can still produce a DLQ entry or retention skip.

Rejected. Saying “persisted” or returning the submitted record would imply a stronger outcome or echo unnecessary device data. Changing every deployment to 202 would alter the existing configurable status contract without adding information. Returning acceptance before broker confirmation would violate STA-04A.

Verification. All 78 Input tests passed, including confirmed and unconfirmed publication, byte-identical forwarding, matching acknowledgement/header trace IDs, and both ingest paths under configured 200 and 202. Both OpenAPI contracts passed Redocly 2.51.2 linting. The four Postman ingest requests now assert JSON acceptance and matching trace IDs in addition to the status code and header.

Delivery consequence. Source contracts and Postman documentation describe this change. The immutable v3 archive still returns an empty success body; the new v4 bundle is required to reproduce the acknowledgement from the packaged services. The v3 setup guide explicitly distinguishes the two versions. Consumers that ignore the success body retain the same status and retry behavior. Consumers that require an empty body must adjust to the new JSON response.

Phase three

Type expansion

All five types implemented locally; LTE release policy and upstream feed confirmation remain open

3.1 Use the working LoRa slice as the template

The project deliberately finished one stat type end to end before expanding. System statistics became the second persisted type on 19 September and OMS the third on 21 September. New consumers, properties, mapping, Flyway tables and API readers are additive. A poison record in one type is tested alongside successful persistence in another.

The decision

System statistics first then OMS

Decision. Rehearse fan out on sysstats, then add omsstats from that slice.

Why. System statistics had no remaining policy blocker; LTE carries identifier handling decisions and network statistics require a more flexible storage shape.

Effect. OMS required no shared business logic changes, demonstrating that the fan out boundary works.

DL-097 · DL-148 · DL-153

3.2 Preserve differences between payloads

System statistics may carry numeric strings; OMS counters are integers. LTE accepts measured payloads, including 2G records with absent signal fields, or an etxt-only error shape. Measured-plus-etxt records are rejected. Network statistics now use checked counters in dynamic interface JSONB with non-interface extensions in data_extra. A shared envelope does not imply one payload parser.

The decision

One range checked numeric helper

Decision. Route all persistence coercion through WireNumbers; check spelling and range before conversion.

Why. Three private helpers disagreed about signed values and classified a LoRa overflow as retryable.

Effect. Integer overflow becomes a permanent mapping failure on its first attempt; an architecture test prevents a second helper.

DL-154

3.3 Complete the LTE and network slices with explicit release gates

ltestats and netstats gained dedicated consumers, V8/V9 partitioned tables, batch writers and registered API readers on 8 October (DL-164). Both use the shared delivery, retention and cursor mechanisms. LTE stores IMSI, PLMN and hexadecimal LAC/CID as strings under the current local contract, preserving leading zeroes. Range checks precede numeric conversion. Empty lists and UID 404s now mean no visible record, rather than an unimplemented reader. D-12 P3 remains a gate for LTE merge/shared release; Q15g remains upstream network-feed confirmation.

The trap

Set identifier handling before releasing LTE

Decision. Resolve sensitive identifier handling before LTE merge/shared release. The local implementation preserves current wire strings and does not adopt masking or keyed hashing.

Why. A rejected LTE payload may remain indefinitely in a no TTL DLQ, so later masking is a remediation problem across several sinks.

Effect. The data sensitivity inventory frames the questions; it does not approve a masking policy.

DL-102 · DL-114 · DL-146

The type expansion decisions

Expand a dated record for the complete rationale, alternatives, consequences and verification. These records belong to this phase. Later amendments remain visible in their historical context.

DL-097 — Message processors are selected by stat type, never by bean uniqueness

The first implementation resolved "the" processor with ObjectProvider#getIfAvailable, which works only while exactly one exists. Phase 3 registers five: the second one makes that call throw NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException, so adding sysstats would break lorastats — the additive-change rule (rule 6, STA-14A) inverted. The failure would also have appeared during fan-out, far from the line that caused it.

3 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · T-019/T-020, rule 6, STA-14A, STA-22

Decision. StatsMessageProcessor declares statType(), and the writer resolves the processor for a type by matching it. Two processors claiming one type is a startup error, not a silent choice.

Why. The first implementation resolved "the" processor with ObjectProvider#getIfAvailable, which works only while exactly one exists. Phase 3 registers five: the second one makes that call throw NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException, so adding sysstats would break lorastats — the additive-change rule (rule 6, STA-14A) inverted. The failure would also have appeared during fan-out, far from the line that caused it.

Rejected. @Primary or @Qualifier on each processor — makes the wiring depend on annotation bookkeeping that a new type can forget, with the same failure mode. A Map<StatType, StatsMessageProcessor> bean — a second registry to keep in step with the beans themselves.

Consequence. Adding a stat type adds a processor and touches nothing existing, which is the point. The listener's autoStartup SpEL needs the wire literal as a compile-time constant, so the descriptor carries one alongside the enum; a test asserts the two never diverge, because a divergence would leave the listener permanently and silently stopped.

DL-110 — No IPv6 today; the schema stays strict and the metered rejection is the reopening trigger

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q4b, provisionally) · Q4a, ADR-007 §6, netstats-v1.schema.json · T-060, T-050

Decision. ZC's answer to Q4b is *"for now consider no."* netstats addresses stay IPv4-only and strict. The answer is explicitly provisional — *"for now"* is not *"never"* — so this closure ships with its own reopening mechanism:

* An address failing the IPv4 $def produces a distinct, metered per-field code, not a generic type error, so an IPv6 gateway appears as a named validation-failure spike rather than as silence. * A non-zero count on that code reopens Q4b. That is the trigger. There is no calendar review, because a calendar review is a thing people stop doing. * invalid/netstats-ipv6.json stays in invalid/. If Q4b reopens, one $def widens and that fixture moves to valid/ — which was always the entire change.

Why a provisional answer needs a detector. Closing "for now consider no" with a plain schema rule and nothing else reproduces trap 16 exactly: an undecided question with a default answer baked into a passing test, invisible because the test is green. The metered code is what makes the assumption observable when production disagrees with it. Q4b closes.

DL-146 — First look at live data: ltestats becomes two closed shapes, the envelope gains three things Heimdal actually sends, and the maintenant trap is re-worded

18 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-007 §6 / Amendment B, ADR-006 Amendment, ADR-003, DL-106, DL-139 · STA-03, STA-03A, STA-23B, T-035

Trigger. Hasan pointed engineering at POST https://one.bmz.cloud/datastore-stats/aggregate — the Datahub's own store of the records Heimdal forwards, queried with a Mongo aggregation pipeline — as the way to *"see what kind of data is coming."* It is the first sight of production beyond the fifteen records of 29 August (ADR-007). 300 records per type were pulled (natural order, so mostly 13 August), plus the full 36-day history of one device (1,680 ltestats records) and a handful from the current hour, and every one was run through the same validators CI uses — the Python gate's registry over docs/schemas/. The endpoint returns MongoDB Extended JSON (_id.$oid, ts.expiresOn.$date); _id was stripped as the export artefact DL-107 says it is. Full collection scans ($group, $regex, $exists) time out at the proxy; $match on type/clsbox/ts.server plus $limit works. The samples stayed in the session scratchpad: ltestats.data.imsi is a subscriber identifier (D-12), and the fixtures below are hand-authored from the observed *shapes* with uid, clsbox and imsi synthesised.

What passed. All 300 lorastats, all 300 sysstats and all 300 omsstats — both gates, no exceptions. The lorastats sample was 100% dialect B (string counters), which is the dialect DL-089 originally dead-lettered. Six findings did not fit the documents.

Decision 1 — maintenant ≠ tenant on the minol tenant, and only there; the trap is re-worded, not the rule. 29 of 300 lorastats, 28 of 300 ltestats and 35 of 300 sysstats carried tenant: minol-gateway with maintenant: minol. Naman, from live data: this is minol only; on every other tenant tenant = maintenant. DL-139 decision 1 said "equal on every record this system will receive" and is corrected in place. Nothing about isolation moves: tenant is the field (ADR-006), and the *-tenant-isolation-pair.json fixtures remain the only test that catches a wrong filter *for every tenant but one*. What the wording now has to carry is that the two cases fail differently — on most tenants a filter on maintenant returns the right rows and can never be caught by data; on minol it silently *drops* one row in ten, which is a different wrong answer, not a safe one. §4, §6 and the sensitivity inventory say so. Two new fixtures use the real minol pattern deliberately.

Decision 2 — ltestats data is a closed oneOf of two shapes (ADR-007 Amendment B1). 6 of 300 live records would have dead-lettered: five with access_Technology: "2G" and no rsrp/rsrq — LTE reference-signal measurements a 2G modem does not have — and one carrying only {"etxt": "unknown technology"}, a modem that never registered. Both are the degraded-device records ops most needs, in a queue that never expires; this is the trap §6 names ("a required field that is absent because the device is broken") and the rule ADR-007 §6 applied to netstats wwan. So: shapeMeasured = the 29 Aug shape with rsrp/rsrq optional; shapeUnknownTechnology = etxt required and additionalProperties: false, so etxt beside a partial measurement matches neither and dead-letters. Closed at two for the reason DL-105 closed lorastats at three. *Rejected:* making everything optional — an empty data would then validate and the schema would stop describing the record; making only rsrp/rsrq optional — leaves the never-registered modem as a permanent DLQ entry that a redrive re-rejects forever. invalid/ltestats-missing-required.json now omits access_Technology, because the field it used to omit stopped being required.

Decision 3 — ts.expiresOn is accepted in both spellings seen (Amendment B2). The datastore returns {"$date": "2026-08-13T10:30:01Z"}; the 29 Aug sample had the plain string; which one the HTTP forward emits is not known and is Q15e. The envelope schema is a oneOf of the two, Timestamps.expiresOn is a JsonNode, and the passthrough column keeps whichever arrived — because the value is never read (ADR-003), and guessing wrong would have 400'd 100% of ingest for a field nobody uses. An epoch integer stays invalid. *Rejected:* keep string and wait for Hasan — the failure mode is loud (Heimdal retries a 400… maxRetry times, Q8a-3), but it is 100% loss of a day's ingest to protect nothing. *Not a Mongo dependency:* one JSON object with one string member; no driver, no type, no library.

Decision 4 — meta.tsDeviceCorrected and ts.deviceOrig are passthrough members (Amendment B2). On some records Heimdal rewrites ts.device to equal ts.server and keeps the gateway's clock as ts.deviceOrig (5–12 s behind on the records seen), flagging it with meta: {"tsDeviceCorrected": true}; other records carry uncorrected +3 s / −1 s skews. The typed view used to drop both silently (ignoreUnknown), which is the D14b class of loss. StatsEnvelope.meta and Timestamps.deviceOrig are added, LorastatsBatchWriter writes both into passthrough, and the round-trip fixture test now proves it. Never read. It nuances DL-106 — ts.device is *not always* device-assigned, and retention/D9 see the corrected value — and the rule for the correction is Q15f. Metering the flag (records_ts_device_corrected{type}) is a sensible T-050 addition and is not built here.

Decision 5 — netstats does not exist in the datastore: recorded as Q15g, nothing changed. type: "netstats" returned zero records across the retained window. The schema, fixtures, queue and table stay exactly as designed; where those records go is ZC's to answer before the fan-out reaches that type.

Decision 6 — two observations, fixtures only. lorastats dialect B sometimes carries util_restarted as an integer among fourteen strings and omits rx_type_arol (7 of 300): valid today, because LorastatsDialects.valueShapeOf reads only the thirteen required counters and the extra lands in data_extra, metered by records_unmapped_data_field — a fixture pins that so nobody "fixes" the mixed-type rule to reject it. And one device's ltestats cadence is every ~29 minutes, not 10 — one data point for T-050's histogram, not a capacity change.

Gates moved, and why. validate-fixtures.py 96 → 103 (six fixtures, one of them carrying writer-dlq); Java parity 84 → 90; the valid-directory round trip 40 → 45; fixture files 79 → 85; markers twelve → thirteen. check_schema_drift.py mirrors the ltestats oneOf into openapi.yaml (LtestatsMeasured, LtestatsUnknownTechnology) and its mutation tests now mutate inside the $defs branch. StatsSchemaValidatorTest shows a oneOf's error report names what *both* shapes wanted (/data/access_Technology and /data/etxt) — still pointers and static messages, never a value.

Verification. validate-fixtures.py 103/0; check_schema_drift.py passes; test_schema_drift.py 6/0; redocly lint 2.51.2 clean on both contracts; ./gradlew build 593 tests, 0 failures against Testcontainers — including the extended passthrough proof and the new Extended-JSON expiresOn proof in LorastatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest.

Reference. The endpoint, its limits and the sampling method are in claude/DEVELOPMENT.md §11 and docs/contracts/heimdal-input-contract-v1.md §0, so the next person does not rediscover that a $group 502s. The working documents (CLAUDE.md 1.14, FLOW.md, SKILLS.md, PROJECT_STATUS_AND_PLAN.md, IMPLEMENTATION_RULES.md, CODE_START_CHECKLIST.md, README.md) and the Developer Guide outside the repo (68 pages, reprinted 18 Sep 2026) were updated in the same change.

DL-148 — sysstats is the second stat type: the fan-out is additive, and the three places it was not are fixed in the reference slice

19 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-14, STA-14A, STA-21A/B/C, STA-22, STA-04C, ADR-003 D2/D7, ADR-007 §6, DL-115, DL-126, DL-133, DL-143 · T-035

Trigger. T-033 was the fan-out's entry criterion and merged on 17 Sep 2026. sysstats goes first, not ltestats (blocked on D-12 P3): 300 of 300 live records validated on 18 Sep (DL-146), and it carries the one wire-type trap worth proving on the second type — timespan is the string "300" here and an integer everywhere else.

What was added, and nothing else was meant to change. In stats-writer: SysstatsConsumerDescriptor, SysstatsConsumerProperties (stats.consumer.sysstats.*), SysstatsDeploymentCondition, SysstatsConsumerConfiguration (main queue, three tiers, DLQ, four bindings, container factory, consumer — all from AmqpTopology), SysstatsConsumer, SysstatsMessageProcessor (gate, then guard, then write — the T-057/T-026 order), SysstatsBatchWriter, and V6__sysstats_table.sql. The table has the whole V1–V5 shape from its first row: PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid), RANGE (ts_device), the two ADR-003 indexes and not the unreachable V2 one, passthrough, data_extra, and the canonical-clsbox CHECK. Column types follow the wire: memfree/temp/rtt as bigint, the five real numbers as double precision, runable and the two strings as text — runable stored as the gateway's own "2/139" rather than split, so the API returns what was received — and timespan as bigint parsed from the digit string with the range check made against BigInteger before the narrowing conversion, exactly as LorastatsDialects does (DL-105 rule 3, STA-04C). An overflow, a timespan sent as a number, or a non-finite double is unmappable (DL-126) and dead-letters on attempt one; nothing is ever zeroed or truncated. Unknown members go to data_extra and are metered. The retention layer needed nothing: PartitionMaintenance discovers parent tables from the catalogue, so V6 alone gives sysstats bootstrap, pre-creation, the sweep and the guard. Fixtures: valid/sysstats-timespan-overflow.json (writer-range-reject) joins the set; the API contract already carried SysstatsData.

The design smell, raised and fixed. The brief said: if this touches lorastats, that is the smell to raise. It did, in three places, and each one is a shared thing that had been living in the first type's file because there was no second type to notice:

1. LorastatsMessageProcessor injected StatsBatchWriter by interface. With a second implementation in the context that injection is ambiguous and the context fails — *adding sysstats would have broken lorastats* in all mode, which is the exact failure rule 6 forbids and StatsMessageProcessorAvailability already guards for processors ("selection is by stat type, never the only bean"). Each writer now declares its bean name from its descriptor (<type>BatchWriter) and each processor qualifies on it. The interface stays, so G3's "replace the implementation without changing consumers" still holds. 2. The two exchanges and the processor selector were beans of LorastatsConsumerConfiguration. A sibling that redeclared them would clash on the bean name; one that did not would depend on the lorastats file existing. They moved to WriterAmqpConfiguration, with the two four-line helpers that turn an AmqpTopology definition into a Spring object, and each per-type configuration declares exactly its own resources. 3. stats.writer.stat-type and all were constants on LorastatsDeploymentCondition, read by the guard and about to be read by the sysstats condition. They are now StatTypeDeployment, with the one serves(env, type) reading both conditions call; the lorastats constants stay as aliases.

One test changed for the same reason: LorastatsMigrationIntegrationTest asserted the Flyway history was *exactly* V1–V5, which every later type would have broken; it now asserts V1–V5 lead it. StatTypeFanOutTest and VersionIsNotADiscriminatorTest gain the sysstats descriptor and writer as justified entries — the reviewed-decision point both guards exist to create. No lorastats *behaviour* changed; the T-050 line in LorastatsConsumer is DL-147's.

Verification. The sysstats siblings of every lorastats test: descriptor, deployment condition, configuration, activation (both consumers in one all context, each activating only for its own deployment), consumer ACK ordering, processor ordering, column mapping against the normative schema, SysstatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest (the full and minimal production records, "300" → 300, 9223372036854775808 refused with nothing written and counted as unmappable not write_failed, the BIGINT maximum accepted, an integer timespan refused, cross-tenant duplicate suppressed and metered, data_extra residue, the DL-146 passthrough shape, canonical clsbox, and a full value-for-value round trip), SysstatsMigrationIntegrationTest (partition key, three indexes on parent and child, every column's type and nullability, the 23505 duplicate and the 23514 CHECK), SysstatsTopologyDeclarationTest (T-032 for the second type, one declaration of each exchange), and SysstatsPersistenceConsumerIntegrationTest — both real consumers live in one context: a sysstats record committed and ACKed, a redelivery absorbed, and a poison sysstats payload in the sysstats DLQ on attempt one with a healthy lorastats record committed beside it and the lorastats DLQ untouched. ManagementSurfaceTest scrapes the sysstats series at zero from startup.

DL-153 — omsstats is the third stat type: a copy of the sysstats slice, and the copy needed nothing shared to move

21 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-14, STA-14A, STA-21A/B/C, STA-22, STA-04C, ADR-003 D2/D7, ADR-004 (as amended by ADR-007 §2), ADR-007 §6, DL-115, DL-126, DL-148, DL-149, DL-150 · T-036

Trigger. T-036 is the next row on Next Up since DL-148: the fan-out's second copy, and the first made from the *sysstats* slice rather than from lorastats. omsstats has the simplest payload of the five — five required integers, no optional member, no string-typed number — and the one rule worth proving on it is ADR-004's: ts.converter arrives on every production record and is accepted, preserved and never acted on. The old acceptance criterion on this row (*"a record carrying ts.converter is rejected"*) was reversed on 29 Aug 2026 because it would have rejected 100 % of ingest; the row's job now is to show that accepting the field is not the same as normalising on it.

What was added, and nothing else changed. In stats-writer: OmsstatsConsumerDescriptor, OmsstatsConsumerProperties (stats.consumer.omsstats.*), OmsstatsDeploymentCondition, OmsstatsConsumerConfiguration (main queue, three tiers, DLQ, four bindings, container factory, consumer — all from AmqpTopology), OmsstatsConsumer (with the DL-147 queueAge.observe line at entry), OmsstatsMessageProcessor (gate, then guard, then write; @Qualifier on omsstatsBatchWriter), OmsstatsBatchWriter, and V7__omsstats_table.sql — the V6 shape from the first row: PRIMARY KEY (ts_device, uid), RANGE (ts_device), the two ADR-003 indexes and not the unreachable V2 one, passthrough, data_extra, the canonical-clsbox CHECK, and every payload column a NOT NULL bigint. There is deliberately no converter or normalised-timestamp column: ts.converter lives inside passthrough beside ts.datastore, ts.expiresOn, ts.deviceOrig, status and meta, and ts_server/ts_device hold exactly the wire values — OmsstatsMigrationIntegrationTest asserts no column name contains *converter* or *normal*, and OmsstatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest asserts second-for-second that neither stored clock moved toward the converter value. The demo (docker/demo.sh step 1) posts the production omsstats record and prints its passthrough.ts, so the preserved-not-applied rule is visible against the real stack.

The parse is per type, and this type's trap is the absence of one. timespan is an integer here; the string "300" is sysstats' spelling. A copy that kept the sysstats digit-string parser would refuse every production omsstats record with unmappable — so OmsstatsColumnMappingTest asserts against the normative schema that every member's wire type is integer, and the writer refuses "300" as a mapping failure rather than guessing (refusesATimespanSentAsAStringRatherThanGuessing). The range check is the same rule as DL-105 rule 3 in its integer form: Jackson holds a literal wider than 64 bits as a BigInteger node and canConvertToLong() is false, so the check is made before any narrowing and an overflow is unmappable (DL-126), dead-lettered on attempt one, never wrapped or truncated (STA-04C). valid/omsstats-counter-overflow.json (writer-range-reject, $expectCode: bigint_overflow) pins it — the third overflow fixture and the first whose overflow is a JSON *integer* rather than a digit string, which the two string-typed ones could not cover. Gates 105 → 107, Java parity 91 → 92, valid records 46 → 47, files 86 → 87, markers 14 → 15.

Nothing shared moved this time. DL-148 predicted that the sysstats slice, written after WriterAmqpConfiguration, StatTypeDeployment and the <type>BatchWriter qualifier were lifted out of lorastats, would copy cleanly. It did: no lorastats or sysstats main source changed, no test of either type changed, and the retention layer needed nothing (PartitionMaintenance discovers parent tables from the catalogue, so V7 alone gives omsstats bootstrap, whole-window pre-creation, the sweep and the guard — DL-149). The two things the tracker note said the copy must *not* re-add were not re-added: no per-type pre-creation, and no addKeyValue of a key WriterLogContext owns — LogsDoNotShadowMdcKeysTest passes over the new processor and writer. The only edits outside the new files are the additive ones every type makes: the two guard whitelists (StatTypeFanOutTest, VersionIsNotADiscriminatorTest), the properties block, ManagementSurfaceTest's five scraped series under type="omsstats", the fixture counts, and the demo step. That is what STA-14A asks for, and it is the first fan-out row that met it without a design-smell paragraph.

Rejected. *Sharing requiredLong/optionalLong between the sysstats and omsstats writers now* — it is the right thing and it is T-062's, the next row: extracting one range-checked coercion helper for all three writers and the two unbuilt types in one change, rather than a second helper today and a third under T-062. *A timespan >= 0 CHECK on the table* — the schema says minimum: 0 and the gate enforces it before the writer; a table CHECK would make the two gates disagree about whose refusal it is. *Adding omsstats to stats-api* — Phase 4 is held on D-09 (DL-152); the contract already carries OmsstatsData.

Verification. The omsstats siblings of every sysstats test — 64 tests: descriptor, deployment condition (sysstats and all and nothing else), configuration, activation (three consumers in one all context; a sysstats deployment creates no omsstats consumer and a lorastats deployment creates neither), consumer ACK ordering, processor ordering, column mapping against the normative schema, OmsstatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest (the production record; ts.converter preserved with both clocks unmoved; expiresOn as {"$date": …} verbatim; the overflow fixture refused with nothing written and counted as unmappable not write_failed; the BIGINT maximum accepted; "300" refused; cross-tenant duplicate suppressed and metered; data_extra residue; canonical clsbox; a full value-for-value round trip), OmsstatsMigrationIntegrationTest (V7, partition key, three indexes on parent and child, sixteen columns' type and nullability, no converter column, 23505 and 23514), OmsstatsTopologyDeclarationTest, and OmsstatsPersistenceConsumerIntegrationTest — all three real consumers live: an omsstats record committed and ACKed with its passthrough intact, a redelivery absorbed, a poison omsstats payload in the omsstats DLQ on attempt one with a lorastats record committed beside it. ManagementSurfaceTest scrapes the omsstats series at zero from startup; validate-fixtures.py reports 107.

DL-154 — The wire-number range check is one helper, WireNumbers, and the build refuses a second copy

21 September 2026 · ENGINEERING · STA-04C, ADR-003 D2, ADR-007 §6, DL-105 rule 3, DL-124, DL-126, DL-127, DL-148, DL-153 · T-062

Trigger. T-062's own note predicted this entry: *"one helper, not three; helpers written weeks apart are how the range check goes missing from one of them."* By 21 Sep there were three — LorastatsDialects.coerce (8 Sep), SysstatsBatchWriter.optionalDigitString plus its optionalLong (19 Sep), and OmsstatsBatchWriter's copy of the latter (21 Sep, DL-153 *Rejected* deferred the sharing to here) — and the audit before extracting them found two disagreements no test could see:

1. LorastatsBatchWriter threw IllegalArgumentException on a dialect-A integer wider than BIGINT, where the sysstats and omsstats writers threw PermanentFailureException. FailureClassifier defaults to *retry* for anything it does not recognise (DL-122), so a lorastats integer overflow ran through all three tiers — 35½ minutes of queue time — before reaching the DLQ with the identical failure, while the same overflow on the other two types was unmappable on attempt one as DL-126 requires. Every lorastats test passed, because acceptsTheBigintMaximumAndRejectsAnythingWiderThanIt asserted the message and never the classification. 2. LorastatsDialects.coerce accepted a negative string (it compared against BIGINT_MIN), where the dialect-B schema pattern ^(0|[1-9][0-9]{0,18})$ admits no minus. Unobservable in production only because the gate refuses the record first — a mapping wider than its gate is a second contract nobody reviewed.

Decision. One class, stats-writer/…/persistence/WireNumbers, with four readers and a bare parse: requiredInteger/optionalInteger for a JSON integer (Jackson holds a literal wider than 64 bits as a BigInteger node; canConvertToLong() is the check, made before longValue() could narrow), requiredDigitString/optionalDigitString (Sign.UNSIGNED | Sign.SIGNED, the schema's pattern repeated by the caller; -?[0-9]+ is the only admissible spelling, so "+5", " 5", "1e3", "0x1F" and non-ASCII digits are refused even though the JDK would parse them; the range check is against BigInteger before conversion), and parseDigitString for LorastatsDialects, which coerces while it is still normalising names. Every refusal is a PermanentFailureException and names the field, never the value (guard P2). All three writers and the dialect normaliser now call it; each lost its private copy and the two BigInteger constants. WireNumbersIsTheOnlyCoercionTest in stats-common's architecture package scans the persistence package for BigInteger, canConvertToLong, Long.parseLong and Long.valueOf(String) outside WireNumbers and fails the build on the next copy — scoped to persistence because that is where a member becomes a column; the redrive's envelope-timestamp read and CLI parse are not coercions.

What the two unbuilt types get. WireNumbersTest covers their shapes now, so T-034 and T-037 have nothing to write here: ltestats' signed signal strings ("-97", the BIGINT minimum, one below it refused), netstats' nineteen-digit counters at and one above the maximum. What it deliberately does not cover: ltestats' lac/cid hex strings (^[0-9A-Fa-f]{1,16}$ — sixteen hex digits exceed a signed BIGINT, and whether they are numbers or location identifiers stored as text is D-12 P5's question, not a parser's) and imsi (an identifier, never a number; D-12 P4). A hex reader is added to WireNumbers with its test on the day a signed answer needs one, not before.

Behaviour change, stated. One: a lorastats dialect-A record with an integer counter wider than BIGINT now dead-letters on its first attempt instead of after three retry tiers. It is the behaviour DL-126 specified and the other two types already had; the lorastats integration test now asserts PermanentFailureException and classifier.isPermanent. Nothing else observable moved — every other refusal message is byte-identical, and the three overflow fixtures (lorastats-dialect-string-overflow, sysstats-timespan-overflow, omsstats-counter-overflow) are the regression set.

Rejected. *Putting the helper in stats-common* — the coercion is the Writer's business (ADR-007 §5: the payload is the Writer's gate), Input never converts a number, and stats-common holds five things by decision (D15). *A Sign inferred from the schema at runtime* — the writer already names each member; repeating the sign beside the name is one more thing <Type>ColumnMappingTest can check against the schema, and one less thing that can silently be wrong. *Keeping IllegalArgumentException for lorastats* — it was the bug.

Verification. WireNumbersTest (26 cases: both boundaries on both readers, every non-digit spelling, sign, absent/null, no value in any message, permanence via the real FailureClassifier); WireNumbersIsTheOnlyCoercionTest (the scan, and that it recognises the four shapes); LorastatsDialectsTest, LorastatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest (+ permanence), SysstatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest, OmsstatsBatchWriterIntegrationTest and the three ColumnMappingTests unchanged in outcome.

Phase four

The read API

Implemented on 26 September ahead of contract confirmation; independent review remains due

4.1 Build the read contract with tenant isolation

The isolation field is tenant, never maintenant. Tests deliberately make them different because production samples where they happen to match conceal the bug. A moved gateway's historical rows retain the tenant Heimdal stamped at measurement time; ownership changes do not rewrite history.

The decision

Tenant comes from the token on every query

Decision. Filter on tenant, never maintenant, using the validated token claim.

Why. The two fields often match in samples, hiding a filter mistake; gateway ownership can also move.

Effect. Historic rows remain with their measurement time tenant, and adversarial fixtures make the two fields different.

DL-060 · DL-142 · DL-162

API validates token signature, expiry, issuer and audience, then requires realm role stats.readOnly and a nonblank string tenant claim. Invalid authentication is 401; a valid caller missing role or tenant is 403. There is no fallback to all tenants. Every SQL statement starts with a predicate bound to the token's tenant. Request parameters, headers and cursor contents cannot choose a tenant.

A foreign UID is indistinguishable from a nonexistent UID and returns 404. Lists, including device history, return an empty 200 page when nothing is visible. Returning 403 for a foreign identifier would reveal its existence. The API database role stats_ro has SELECT capability without write, schema creation or temporary table privileges; database tests prove a forbidden write fails. Input receives no database credentials and API's write credentials are blanked in Compose.

The decision

Do not reveal another tenant through errors

Decision. Return the same 404 for a foreign UID and a nonexistent UID; return empty 200 pages for lists without visible records.

Why. A different status would disclose that another tenant owns a record or device.

Effect. A valid token without role or usable tenant gets 403, while invalid authentication gets 401.

DL-061 · DL-062 · DL-151 · DL-162

All non GET methods on /v1/** return 405 with Allow: GET before authentication, including unmapped paths. The API is a stateless bearer token resource server. Errors use shared RFC 9457 Problem Details, reviewed safe messages, underscore based wire codes and trace IDs. Exception types can be logged; offending values and arbitrary throwable messages cannot leak into error or Writer log text.

Read shapeBehaviour and reason
GET /v1/stats/{type}Requires from and to; maximum one hour window limits tenant wide reads
GET /v1/stats/{type}/{uid}One tenant visible record, otherwise indistinguishable 404
GET /v1/stats/{type}/{clsbox}/{from}Device scoped history from a time, no upper bound
GET /v1/stats/{type}/{clsbox}/{from}/{to}Bounded device history; shares query, security and cursor mechanisms

Pages sort by ts_device DESC, uid DESC and use keyset pagination, avoiding offset instability under concurrent inserts. Defaults are 100 rows, maximum 1,000. A signed cursor stores position and expiry; an HMAC binds it to operation, type, tenant and filters without embedding the tenant in its visible payload. A changed query, caller or signature yields 400 invalid_cursor. Cursor TTL is 15 minutes and all replicas need the same signing key of at least 32 bytes. This supersedes the early plan to carry filters visibly in the cursor.

The decision

Bind continuation to the caller and the query

Decision. Use a signed keyset position with the token tenant and filters bound into its HMAC.

Why. Offset pages shift under inserts, and a transferred or tampered cursor must never widen access.

Effect. A changed query or tenant returns invalid_cursor; pages sort by device time and a UID tie breaker. The later MAC design replaces the early visible filter plan.

DL-072 · DL-162

The Keycloak local realm supplies clients, roles and deliberate negative test users. The issuer remains localhost for host clients while container API signing key retrieval uses the internal Keycloak hostname. Separating issuer validation from key lookup solves host versus container addressing without changing token identity. Security relevant configuration has no guessed code defaults; missing values stop startup.

Inbound authentication is still a separate open decision. The current local mode is guarded and warns, and the auth seam is a servlet filter pending an agreed mechanism. Two ingest doors share current behaviour. Trusting a tenant supplied to the external door is not the production solution; T 068 depends on an agreed identity mechanism and tenant binding.

4.2 Record the choice to proceed before confirmation

On 26 September the requester authorised building the API against the drafted contract before external confirmation. The shared query builder, per table readers, record reconstruction, security filters and signed cursors were implemented together. DL 162 records 228 API tests and mutation checks. Contract rework and an independent security review remain release qualifications.

The decision

Build ahead of D 09 with explicit rework risk

Decision. Proceed with Phase 4 while external contract confirmation is still open.

Why. The requester judged the draft stable enough to complete the usable read path.

Effect. Later external changes may require YAML and code changes; Mukesh still needs to independently review the isolation and security tests.

DL-162

The API decisions

Expand a dated record for the complete rationale, alternatives, consequences and verification. These records belong to this phase. Later amendments remain visible in their historical context.

DL-060 — Isolation is on tenant, from a validated token claim, applied in the query

This is the highest-risk item in the project, and the reason is that the wrong implementation *passes every test*. The supplied samples very often set tenant and maintenant to the same value, so code filtering on maintenant is green in CI and leaks in production. maintenant is the *managing* tenant and has no security meaning. *(Sharpened by DL-139: on a tenant change both fields take the new name, so they are equal in production too — no real record can reveal a wrong filter, only a fixture built to differ can. The security meaning is unchanged: none.)*

Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-006, STA-35A

Decision. The isolation field is tenant, never maintenant. The effective tenant comes only from a validated Keycloak claim. The predicate is applied in the database query — every query, count, cursor and future aggregate. Never filter across tenants in application memory.

Why. This is the highest-risk item in the project, and the reason is that the wrong implementation *passes every test*. The supplied samples very often set tenant and maintenant to the same value, so code filtering on maintenant is green in CI and leaks in production. maintenant is the *managing* tenant and has no security meaning. *(Sharpened by DL-139: on a tenant change both fields take the new name, so they are equal in production too — no real record can reveal a wrong filter, only a fixture built to differ can. The security meaning is unchanged: none.)*

In-memory filtering fails differently and just as badly: it means the database returned another tenant's rows, so pagination counts, cursors and limits are all computed over data the caller may not see — and one missed filter is a full leak.

Rejected. A tenant query parameter or header — never trusted; controllers accept no override on any normal path. Filtering after fetch — above.

Consequence. Every fixture must have tenant != maintenant. A test using the supplied samples as-is proves nothing about isolation. This needs an integration test that is a release gate, not a unit test.

DL-061 — A missing or empty tenant claim is 403, not 401 — and never "all tenants"

The distinction is real: the caller *is* authenticated (401 would be wrong), but is not authorised to see anything, because there is no tenant to scope to. The load-bearing half is what an absent claim must not mean: "unscoped" or "all tenants". Fail-closed here is by construction — there is no default value to fall back to — rather than by a default that a later refactor could change.

Aug 2026, corrected 29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-006 §6

Decision. A token that authenticates but carries no usable tenant claim — absent or empty — is rejected with 403. openapi.yaml said 401; ADR-006 wins and the contract is corrected.

Why. The distinction is real: the caller *is* authenticated (401 would be wrong), but is not authorised to see anything, because there is no tenant to scope to. The load-bearing half is what an absent claim must not mean: "unscoped" or "all tenants". Fail-closed here is by construction — there is no default value to fall back to — rather than by a default that a later refactor could change.

Rejected. 401 — misstates what happened and invites a token refresh that will not fix it. Treating an absent claim as unscoped — a full cross-tenant leak from a misconfigured client.

Consequence. The Keycloak tenant claim is single-valued String on purpose. A multi-valued claim turns isolation into tenant IN (...), and one misconfigured user then reads several tenants with every test still green.

DL-062 — A cross-tenant identifier returns 404, indistinguishable from not-found

A 403 for "exists but not yours" versus a 404 for "does not exist" turns the API into an oracle: an attacker enumerates uids and learns which records exist in other tenants without ever reading one. The information leak is the *difference*, so the responses must not differ — including timing class, which is why this cannot be a late check on a fetched row.

Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-006 §5, STA-35B

Decision. A uid belonging to another tenant returns 404, identical in every observable respect — status, body, headers, timing class — to a uid that does not exist at all.

Why. A 403 for "exists but not yours" versus a 404 for "does not exist" turns the API into an oracle: an attacker enumerates uids and learns which records exist in other tenants without ever reading one. The information leak is the *difference*, so the responses must not differ — including timing class, which is why this cannot be a late check on a fetched row.

Rejected. 403 for cross-tenant — the oracle. A distinct error body — the same oracle in the payload.

Consequence. Cross-tenant lookup by uid is a named release gate. An authenticated caller with no data in range gets an empty items array and 200 — never 403 or 404, either of which would disclose the same thing from the other direction.

DL-070 — GET only; every other method is 405 with no side effect

Rule 1, and it is structural rather than stylistic: Module 2 is the only component with database write access, so a write path in the API is not a feature to add later — it is a violation of the architecture. Returning 405 explicitly (rather than 404, or nothing) makes the boundary observable and testable.

Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · ADR-006 §8, STA-32A

Decision. stats-api exposes GET only. POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE return 405 and do nothing.

Why. Rule 1, and it is structural rather than stylistic: Module 2 is the only component with database write access, so a write path in the API is not a feature to add later — it is a violation of the architecture. Returning 405 explicitly (rather than 404, or nothing) makes the boundary observable and testable.

Rejected. Leaving non-GET undefined — untestable, and framework defaults vary.

Consequence. A test asserting 405 for every non-GET method on every route is mandatory, not optional.

DL-071 — from and to are required; there is no unbounded query

An unbounded scan over a table taking up to 115.2 million records a day is not a query anyone recovers from — it is an outage with a 200 status. The contract refuses to express one. Making it *required* rather than defaulted means a caller cannot omit it by accident and receive a default window they did not reason about.

21 Aug 2026 · ENGINEERING · openapi.yaml

Decision. GET /v1/stats/{type} requires from and to. limit is 1–1000, default 100.

Why. An unbounded scan over a table taking up to 115.2 million records a day is not a query anyone recovers from — it is an outage with a 200 status. The contract refuses to express one. Making it *required* rather than defaulted means a caller cannot omit it by accident and receive a default window they did not reason about.

Rejected. A default time window — hides the constraint until it surprises someone. Unbounded with a server-side cap — the caller believes they got everything.

Consequence. Every caller must pass a range. The maximum query window is still provisional inside the contract.

DL-072 — Keyset pagination, fixed ts.device DESC, uid DESC, cursor carries its filters

Three separate reasons converge. Correctness: without an explicit deterministic sort with a unique tie-breaker, a paginated query can return the same row on two pages or skip one entirely — and it does so silently. Performance: OFFSET at page 10,000 makes the database count and discard 10,000 rows; keyset seeks directly. Alignment: this sort matches the (ts_device, uid) primary key and the partition key, so the index serves it without a sort step.

21 Aug 2026 · ENGINEERING · openapi.yaml, ADR-003

Decision. Keyset (cursor) pagination on a fixed, always-applied ts.device DESC, uid DESC sort. The cursor carries the filters it was issued for; changing one mid-walk returns invalid_cursor.

Why. Three separate reasons converge. Correctness: without an explicit deterministic sort with a unique tie-breaker, a paginated query can return the same row on two pages or skip one entirely — and it does so silently. Performance: OFFSET at page 10,000 makes the database count and discard 10,000 rows; keyset seeks directly. Alignment: this sort matches the (ts_device, uid) primary key and the partition key, so the index serves it without a sort step.

The cursor carrying its filters closes the last hole: a cursor reused with a changed from would otherwise return a page from a *different query* and look entirely valid.

Rejected. OFFSET/LIMIT — the skip/duplicate bug and the deep-page cost. Sorting only when the caller asks — the failure appears exactly when they do not. An opaque cursor without filter binding — silently wrong results.

Consequence. The sort is not negotiable per request. Follow nextCursor, resending from, to and clsbox unchanged; null or absent means the last page.

DL-073 — RFC 9457 Problem Details for every error

A standard beats an invented envelope: consumers can parse it without bespoke code, and it has a defined place for both a machine-readable type and field-level detail. Putting the catalogue in stats-common is what makes Input and the API produce the *same* shape — two hand-written error formats across two modules is a contract with itself.

Aug 2026 · RATIFIED · stats-common/error, openapi.yaml

Decision. All errors use application/problem+json per RFC 9457, with a shared ErrorCode catalogue in stats-common and FieldError for field-level detail.

Why. A standard beats an invented envelope: consumers can parse it without bespoke code, and it has a defined place for both a machine-readable type and field-level detail. Putting the catalogue in stats-common is what makes Input and the API produce the *same* shape — two hand-written error formats across two modules is a contract with itself.

Rejected. A custom error envelope — no ecosystem support, and it drifts between modules. Bare status codes — nothing for a client to branch on.

Consequence. Every new failure path adds a catalogue entry, which is deliberate friction — it forces the question of what the caller should do about it. Combined with DL-053, field errors carry pointers and codes, never values.

DL-131 — The API's lorastats examples are real production records, shown post-normalisation

8 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · DL-105, DL-127, D14b, ADR-007 §1, STA-35A · openapi.yaml, T-063

> Renumbered 10 Sep 2026 (was a second DL-130). Two entries were written on 8 Sep and > both took the number 130. Every citation outside this file — three build.gradle.kts > files and two application.properties — resolves to the *other* one (configuration > metadata), so this entry is the one that moves and nothing else needed editing. It is the > same defect DL-129 itself caught and fixed twice on the day it was written: a duplicate > identifier that the file loads without a murmur, where the second definition is simply > unreachable by reference.

Decision. Both lorastats examples in docs/contracts/openapi.yaml are now drawn from the three production records of 29 August 2026 — one per dialect — rather than from valid/lorastats-valid.json, the synthetic fixture that predates them. LorastatsRecord mirrors valid/lorastats-production-canonical.json; LorastatsPage carries all three, in ts.device DESC, uid DESC order, as the API returns them: dialect B's quoted counters as integers and dialect C's lora_-prefixed names stripped, because that is what DL-127 does to them before the INSERT. Exactly one deviation from the source fixtures is permitted, and it is now stated in the file: tenant and maintenant are made to differ.

Why the old example was wrong without being invalid. It carried the thirteen required counters and nothing else — no rx_short_pkt, no fsk_received, no timespan, no version, no status, and two of the five ts members. Every one of those omissions is legal, and the composite is a record production has never sent. A reader comparing the contract against a real payload concludes the contract is wrong about the payload, which is the more expensive mistake here: the last time a document set disagreed with production, production won and four of five payload schemas were rebuilt (ADR-007). An example is the only part of a contract most people read.

Why the page shows three dialects and not three copies of one. The question this answers is *"why does the response not look like what Heimdal sends?"*, and the answer is only convincing side by side. A prefixed name or a quoted counter in a response example would be worse than the thin example it replaced: it would describe a response this API cannot produce, and invite a client that parses for lora_rx_nr_pkts. The dialect is a metric label — records_lorastats_dialect{dialect} — and the examples are where that stops being an abstract claim.

Rejected. *Adding the optional members to valid/lorastats-valid.json* — the originally chosen option, withdrawn on inspection. That fixture is load-bearing because it is bare: StatsEnvelopeTest.omitsAbsentOptionalMembersRatherThanSerialisingThemAsNull proves JsonInclude.NON_NULL keeps a pre-29-Aug record from round-tripping with four null members, which byte-identical publication depends on (DL-119), and PayloadGateTest.doesNotMeterAnAbsentStatus needs a record with no status at all (DL-128). Enriching it would have left both green while testing nothing — the silent failure both guards exist to prevent. *A new production-shaped fixture* — unnecessary; valid/lorastats-production-canonical.json already was one. *Editing only the YAML* — breaks the rule stated above the examples block.

Consequence. redocly lint 2.51.2 clean; 91 fixture assertions, 0 failed; no fixture added, moved or re-marked. The stale $comment on the canonical fixture — which still said the other two dialects "dead-letter until then" — is corrected, and RequestLimitTest's two byte counts are re-measured (1,462 → 1,758; 425 → 429) since the fixture that comment describes grew. lorastats-valid.json is untouched.

DL-142 — D16 is ratified: a row's tenant is the tenant at measurement time, and a moved gateway's history stays where Heimdal stamped it

17 Sep 2026 · ENGINEERING · ADR-006 (amended), ADR-007, DL-139 · T-034, T-040

The question. On the Datahub a gateway can be moved to another tenant (PUT /inventory/generic/tenant/change). The move sets both tenant and maintenant to the new name and appends to the object's moved audit array. Records arriving after the move carry the new tenant; rows this system wrote *before* the move carry the old one, and ADR-006 puts tenant in every query — so the new tenant's token cannot see the gateway's pre-move history, and the old tenant's still can. DL-139 raised this as D16 with a recommended position and left it unratified.

Decision. Yes — that is the intended reading, and it is closed. Ratified by Naman Vashishtha, 17 Sep 2026. A row's tenant is the tenant at measurement time, exactly as Heimdal stamped it. The new tenant sees the gateway from the moment of the move onward; the old tenant keeps what was measured while the gateway was theirs. Nothing is built, nothing is rewritten, and ADR-006 gains an amendment that says so.

Why this and not re-labelling. Three reasons, in the order they decide it.

1. ADR-007 forbids rewriting the record. Publication is byte-identical (DL-020, DL-050) and the stored row is the wire record plus typed columns. Changing tenant on historical rows would be the first place this system alters what Heimdal sent, and it would do so on the *isolation* field. 2. Re-labelling needs a dependency this project does not have and should not acquire. To know a gateway moved, the Writer — or a maintenance job — would have to read the inventory service's moved array. That is a second upstream, a second failure mode, and a job that UPDATEs the isolation column across partitions under retention. Every one of those is a new way to lose or leak a record. 3. The alternative leaks in the other direction. Re-labelling history to the new tenant would hand the *new* tenant data measured while the gateway belonged to someone else — the exact cross-tenant exposure ADR-006 exists to prevent. "The old tenant can still see their own past" is correct; "the new tenant can see the old tenant's past" would be a security defect.

What it does and does not change.

* No code change. The query predicate, the index, the cursor and the 404 behaviour are untouched. A new tenant asking for a clsbox that only has pre-move rows gets 404, indistinguishable from not-found — which is the STA-35B contract working as written. * One support answer, written down. When a tenant reports "the gateway moved to us and its history is missing", the answer is *by design*: the history belongs to the tenant it was measured for. The Datahub's own moved array is where the move itself is recorded; this system does not duplicate it. * maintenant is not an escape hatch.
