It moves with tenant (DL-139), so it carries no pre-move information either. The rule "isolation on tenant, only ever tenant" stands with no exception for this case. * Out of scope, explicitly. A privileged cross-tenant read (an operator following a gateway across tenants) is the "privileged cross-tenant access" row of ADR-006's register — *out of scope until separately specified* — and this decision does not reopen it.

Where it lands. ADR-006 *Amendment — 17 September 2026* replaces the 12 Sep residual; the decisions register gains a row; claude/CLAUDE.md §7 moves D16 from *Ours to decide* to decided and the ADR-006 status row drops its residual; the tracker moves D16 from *Open Questions* to *Decisions*. docs/architecture/adr/ADR-006 is the record.

DL-151 — Two ingest doors with one behaviour, and a device-scoped read path: the 20 September management ask

21 September 2026 · MANAGEMENT ASK → ENGINEERING DESIGN · STA-01A, STA-03A, STA-14A, STA-32, STA-33A, STA-35A, STA-36A, ADR-006, ADR-007 §1, DL-115, DL-118, DL-121, DL-134, DL-145 · approver: Naman Vashishtha, relaying Timo (20 Sep 2026)

Trigger. Management, after a conversation with Timo on 20 September, asked for three things: *"two endpoints for stats-input: one for internal calls (coming from heimdal-core) and one for external calls"*; *"in stats-api you can stay with stats/{type}/{uid}"*; and *"a GET call with mac address and timestamp — stats/{type}/{mac}/{from} (from to now) and stats/{type}/{mac}/{from}/{to}"*. The third is the device-first endpoint CLAUDE.md 1.13 recorded as *raised 17 Sep, not designed, ask before building*. This entry is the design, and the first two parts are built; the read half is contract-only because stats-api has no controller yet (Phase 4, T-038…T-046).

Quizzed before building (DL-005). Three readings of the ask were put back before a line was written, and the answers are the decision:

1. *What makes the second Input endpoint "external"?* — Same authentication, two paths. The split is for routing and ingress restriction, not a second mechanism. 2. *Do the MAC endpoints stay tenant-scoped from the token?* — Yes. The one harmless reading of 17 Sep is the one that was asked for: per type, tenant from the token, served by (tenant, clsbox, ts_device DESC, uid DESC). 3. *How far now?* — Contract for the read path; build the Input split.

Decision 1 — stats-input has two doors and one handler. stats.input.endpoint.path becomes internal-path (/internal/stats, Heimdal's) and external-path (/external/stats, everyone else's); both are D-03 drafts and both are properties. StatsIngestController.ingest is bound to both on purpose — the rules are identical, and a second handler would be a second copy of the same three steps waiting to drift. Every ingest filter — correlation, size, authentication, rate — is registered on both patterns from IngestEndpointProperties.paths(), and the rate limit is one bucket: the limit protects the instance, and the instance did not gain capacity by gaining a path. The two paths must differ and neither may sit under /actuator; both are refused at startup. IngestLoggingFilter resolves the door once from the path and publishes it as the ingestSource MDC key and a request attribute, so every metric carries the same answer.

What makes the split worth anything is the label. Every Input series except one gains source="internal"|"external" (unknown for a request the filter never saw); records_unroutable stays unlabelled because the publisher increments it without knowing the door and a missing binding is the same incident either way. Sum over source for the old totals. "External callers are producing all the 400s" and "Heimdal changed its envelope" are different incidents, and without the label the two doors would be indistinguishable from one.

Decision 2 — the read API gains two device-scoped paths and loses one query parameter. GET /v1/stats/{type}/{clsbox}/{from} and GET /v1/stats/{type}/{clsbox}/{from}/{to}, tenant from the token, ordered ts_device DESC, uid DESC, keyset-paginated with limit/cursor, [from, to) in Unix seconds. {clsbox} is the MAC address; the path parameter keeps the wire name because ADR-007 §1 forbids a rename layer — the URL bytes are the same either way. Four rules, each chosen against a specific wrong answer:

* "From to now" is open-ended, not to = now. Input admits ts.device up to max-clock-skew (5 min) ahead of arrival (ADR-003 D9), so a gateway with a fast clock has records in the near future; a literal now would hide its newest record on every call and nobody would notice. An open bound also keeps the cursor stable — a moving to is a different filter on every page. * An unknown or foreign clsbox is an empty 200, never 404. Identical to the list; a 404 would disclose whether another tenant's gateway exists (STA-35A). * No query_window_exceeded on device-scoped reads. The covering index makes the scan proportional to the result and the page bounds each request; a single gateway's whole retention window is ~13,000 rows. The window limit stays on the tenant-wide list only, where it was always the point. * The clsbox query parameter on /stats/{type} is removed. With the path forms in place it was a second spelling of the same query. One query, one spelling.

The uid path and the device paths are told apart by segment count — three against four or five — never by inspecting the value.

Rejected. *A mac path parameter* — a second name for clsbox is the rename layer ADR-007 removed. *to = now for the one-argument form* — see above. *Cross-tenant lookup by MAC* — collides with rule 3 and ADR-006; the answer to quiz question 2 closed it. *Different auth per door today* — nothing to implement it against until Q8a-2 (and Q8a-3's basic-in-URL) is answered; stats.input.auth.mode stays one seam. *Keeping the clsbox query parameter for compatibility* — nothing is deployed against the draft.

Residual — read it before relying on the external door. With identical authentication on both paths, /external/stats has no tenant binding on writes: a caller that can reach it can submit a record under any tenant's name, and once Q8a-2 selects Heimdal's mechanism an external caller would present Heimdal's credentials. Until then the protection is the ingress rule in front of the path and the source label behind it. When Q8a-2 lands, the external door is where a per-door stats.input.auth.<door>.mode and a *token tenant = record tenant* check belong — the seam is now a path, so that is a filter plus a property, not a rewrite. Second residual: the read paths are a contract, not code. They join T-038…T-046 in Phase 4; Stats_App_Tracker.xlsx ~~needs a row for them~~ — T-067 since DL-152, with the external-door residual above as T-068 — and the tenant-wide window limit remains PROVISIONAL.

Gates. stats-input: 75 tests, 0 failures (67 before; the two-door, source-label and path-validation cases are new). redocly lint clean on both contracts; check_schema_drift.py passes; build-html.sh renders. Fixture gate unchanged — no schema moved.

DL-162 — Phase 4 is built ahead of the contract's confirmation: three read operations, the tenant from the token on every page, and a cursor that can only fail to verify

26 September 2026 · API · SECURITY · STA-30A…STA-38A, STA-31A, STA-32A, STA-33A, STA-34A, STA-35A, STA-35B, STA-36A · ADR-006 (and its 17/21 Sep amendments), ADR-007 §1, DL-115, DL-124, DL-135, DL-142, DL-145, DL-151 · T-038, T-039, T-040, T-041, T-042, T-043, T-044, T-045, T-046, T-067, T-028 (read half)

Trigger. Naman asked for T-039…T-046 to be completed. D-09 — ZC's confirmation of openapi.yaml — has gated the whole phase since 21 Aug and is still open. Starting without it is a decision, taken by the approver on 26 Sep 2026: the contract is stable enough to build against, and a change ZC asks for is now rework in stats-api as well as in the YAML. T-038 (below T-039) and T-067 (the device paths, sharing every mechanism) came in with them; so did the read half of T-028, which could not exist without a query path.

Quiz (IMPLEMENTATION_RULES §9, DL-005). Three questions on the traps this change can fall into. *Isolation field* — passed first time. *Cursor and tenant* — passed on the third asking: the SQL's tenant comes from the token on every page and a cursor issued to another tenant is invalid_cursor, never a fallback. *Foreign uid* — not passed; waived at the requester's instruction after four askings ("tell correct and skip"). The answer is recorded here so the waiver is not mistaken for agreement: a uid that exists only under another tenant is 404, byte-identical to a uid that exists nowhere, because the query is WHERE tenant = :tokenTenant AND uid = :uid — a 403 would disclose existence and a 200 would disclose the record. The tests below assert it; they are what holds the rule now.

Decision.

1. T-038 — resource server, fail-closed. Boot validates signature, expiry, iss (issuer-uri) and aud (spring.security.oauth2.resourceserver.jwt.audiences — Boot's own property, so the parked stats.security.audience is not revived). Any failure is 401 with the RFC 6750 challenge and a problem body. stats.security.read-role, stats.security.tenant-claim, the audience and every stats.api.* value are properties with no default in code; an unset one stops the context. A keycloak health contributor probes the URL the decoder fetches keys from (JWK set if configured, else discovery) and joins readiness, never liveness — closing the T-006 residual DL-103 carried on this task. 2. T-039 — ReadAccess, before any controller. Every GET /v1/ needs the stats.readOnly realm role (read from realm_access.roles on the token itself, not from Spring's authority mapping, which a mocked test would bypass) and a usable tenant claim: one non-blank String. Absent, empty, blank, multi-valued or non-string is 403 — the same 403 as no role, and the body does not say which. CallerTenant is the one place the tenant is read; no handler parameter is a tenant, so ?tenant=, headers and bodies are ignored by construction. 3. T-040/T-041/T-067 — one SQL builder, tenant = ? first. StatsQueryBuilder is pure and builds every statement the API runs; the first predicate is always tenant = ? bound to the token's tenant, including the uid lookup. maintenant is selected and returned and appears in no predicate or ordering. clsbox = upper(?) (DL-115); ORDER BY ts_device DESC, uid DESC on every statement; keyset as (ts_device, uid) < (?, ?); LIMIT n+1 to know whether a next page exists. The one-argument device form has no upper bound (DL-151). The tenant-wide list's window is stats.api.max-query-window=PT1H, the contract's provisional value; the device paths have none. 4. Records are rebuilt, not stored JSON. RecordAssembler rebuilds the production envelope from the typed columns, passthrough (ts.datastore, ts.converter, ts.expiresOn in whichever spelling arrived, ts.deviceOrig, status, meta) and data_extra; an unset column is omitted, never null; sysstats.timespan goes back as the string it arrived as. One StatReader per table, each its own bean — a new type adds a reader and touches none. 5. Types with no table answer honestly, and a guard stops that outliving the reason. ltestats and netstats are valid types with no table (T-034, T-037): an empty page, and 404 by uid — literally true of the database. StatReadersCoverEveryTableTest compares the readers with the partitioned tables the Writer's migrations create and fails the day a table appears without a reader. 6. T-042 — the cursor carries a position, not a tenant. base64url(payload).base64url(HMAC-SHA256); the payload is the last row's ts_device and uid plus an expiry. The binding — operation, type, tenant, canonical clsbox, from, to — is not in the payload: it is inside the MAC, recomputed from each request. A changed filter, another path, another type or another tenant's token all fail verification exactly as a tampered cursor does: 400 invalid_cursor, never a page. Key stats.api.cursor.signing-key (≥ 32 bytes, no default, identical on every replica), TTL PT15M. 7. T-043 — ReadOnlyMethods before authentication. Every method but GET on /v1/, mapped or not, with a token or without, is 405 with Allow: GET and a problem body. Left to the framework the same question had three answers (405 mapped, 404 unmapped, 401 unauthenticated — and with CSRF on, 403 for an authenticated POST). CSRF is disabled: a stateless bearer-token API has no cookie to forge. 8. T-044 — one error spelling. Problems are built only from the shared ErrorCode catalogue and FieldError.SafeMessage (three new reviewed messages in stats-common: inverted range, window, cursor). Type URIs use the wire code, underscores and all — what ErrorCode.typeUri() emits and Input already ships; the read contract's six hyphenated examples are corrected (closes the DL-145 residual). Problem bodies are written straight to the response so Accept: application/json still receives its application/problem+json. A database outage is 503 and logs the exception *type* only (DL-124's rule, applied here). Every response carries X-Trace-Id, minted as Input mints it; the problem's traceId equals it. 9. Tests, against production artifacts. The API is tested as stats_ro against docker/postgres-init/10-read-only-user.sql and the Writer's real Flyway migrations (passed as a directory, never put on the classpath), with real RS256 tokens from a local JWK set — nothing mocks the decoder. stats-testsupport is not used: it exports spring-amqp, and NoBrokerConnectionTest asserts no broker client in any scope. 228 tests in stats-api (was 12): generated-SQL isolation (78), cursor (22), security suite (18), GET-only matrix (56), reads and record shapes (25), tenant isolation on crafted fixtures (9 — beta's maintenant is alpha's tenant name; the minol shape; a moved gateway; one uid in two tenants), pagination under a concurrent writer (2, four rows per second so the uid tie-breaker is exercised). Mutation-checked: filtering on maintenant fails 53 tests; dropping the tenant from the MAC fails 2; letting a non-GET through fails 56; dropping the uid tie-breaker fails 19.

Rejected. *Waiting for D-09* — the approver's call, recorded above. *A tenant field in the cursor, checked against the token* — equivalent protection, but a cursor in a log line would then name the tenant; inside the MAC it names nothing. *Clamping an out-of-range limit* — the parameter's schema says 1–1000, so outside it is invalid_parameter; the page's limit echoes the value applied after defaulting. *unknown_stat_type for ltestats/netstats* — the contract admits all five; "not built" is not "not a type". *503 for them* — a 503 invites a retry that can never succeed. *Answering a foreign uid with 403* — an existence oracle (STA-35B). *Opening /actuator/prometheus* — see residuals. *Using stats-testsupport* — puts a broker client on the classpath (Q3a).

Residual. * Separation of duties (T-028, T-045): the isolation and security suites were written by the author of the code they test, which the task text asks not to happen for a security control. They need an independent review — Mukesh — before Phase 4 is released. The mutation results above are what a reviewer should re-run. * D-09 is still open. If ZC changes the contract, stats-api changes with it. * Scrape access: /actuator/prometheus stays behind the bearer token, so Prometheus reads the stats-api target as down (401). Nothing reads an API series yet; the first panel or rule that does decides it (a scrape client credential, or an open metrics path on a management port). * Deployments need STATS_API_CURSOR_SIGNING_KEY in .env — stats-api refuses to start without it. .env.example documents it; a local .env predating this entry must gain it before docker compose --profile apps up. * Q8b-2 unchanged: issuer, audience, role and claim names are still the proposal. * Not measured: query latency at production volume. The tenant-wide list relies on partition pruning plus the primary key within a 1-hour window, which T-048-style load should confirm before the window is widened.

Phase five

Operational readiness and v3 delivery

Recorded local drills and monitoring work, followed by the recipient bundle

5.1 Observe the outcome of every delivery

Observability describes outcomes rather than activity alone

Structured JSON logs go to stdout. Scoped MDC carries UID, type, tenant, gateway and trace context without shadowing keys. Payload logging is controlled by DEBUG level. Secrets are injected from environment configuration rather than committed. Build guards catch duplicate configuration keys, unused or inconsistent properties, unsafe logs, numeric coercion copies and architectural dependency violations.

Metrics distinguish attempts from commits, duplicates from new records, retries and dead letters, payload rejection, late arrivals, unknown data fields and upstream status. Broker gauges are polled and cached; a scrape performs no broker I O. Replicas observe the same broker state, so shared gauges aggregate with max, not sum. Poll status is shown alongside cached values. Queue urgency is oldest observed message age, not queue depth alone; the age is sampled at delivery using an Input stamped timestamp, which is not a direct measurement of an untouched queue head.

The decision

Age says more than queue depth

Decision. Watch oldest observed message age and show broker poll status alongside cached gauges; aggregate replica observations with max.

Why. A large replay backlog can be healthy, while a small stuck queue can be urgent. Summing the same broker observation counts it repeatedly.

Effect. Age is sampled at delivery, so its measurement boundary is explicit rather than pretending to inspect an untouched queue head.

DL-082 · DL-137 · DL-147

DLQ alerts use nonempty duration as a proxy for message age. Dashboards cover Writer, Input and PostgreSQL. They are generated, provisioned and checked for metric spelling and stale output. A first live render found that PostgreSQL's enum archive_mode was never exported, silently disabling alerts; a read only custom exporter query now creates that series. Input counters were registered at startup so an empty graph no longer means a missing metric. An unused privileged WAL collector was disabled rather than granting extra database privileges.

API readiness includes Keycloak; liveness stays separate from dependency health. Since 9 October (DL-166), the local Compose configuration enables STATS_SECURITY_PROMETHEUS_PUBLIC_ACCESS=true for the API metrics endpoint only. Prometheus/exporter start with the apps profile and startup verifies all four up samples plus pg_up=1. The source property defaults to false; stats reads and other protected actuator routes retain authentication. Shared deployments still need their own scrape access arrangement.

Recovery targets are signed and partially measured

Signed targets are RPO at most five minutes, RTO one hour per partition and 24 hours for the full cluster. Compose enables WAL archiving with a 300 second timeout and fixes the archive volume's ownership before PostgreSQL starts. Archiving failures, staleness, disabled mode and exporter failure are monitored. A local WAL volume is not off host disaster recovery: operations still needs retained external backup, base backup and restore procedures at deployment scale.

Partition restore loads a standalone table, verifies bounds, adds a matching CHECK and attaches it only after loading and indexing. A plain dump restore attached too early, exposed partly loaded data and then reported duplicate index errors. The matching CHECK avoids scanning the entire day under an attach lock. A 200,000 row local drill completed in 1.229 seconds, with 6,517 concurrent inserts and no failed writes. That does not prove the one hour target for a roughly 23 million row production day or full cluster PITR. Nightly partition dump scheduling remains an operations responsibility.

The decision

Restore a complete day before attaching it

Decision. Load and index a standalone table, validate its bounds, add a matching CHECK, then attach.

Why. Early attachment exposes a partly restored day and duplicates index creation; an unchecked attach scans rows while holding a lock.

Effect. The 200,000 row local drill completed in 1.229 seconds, but production sized recovery and external backup still need rehearsal.

DL-111 · DL-155

Recorded load measurements have explicit limits

Recorded drillResultInterpretation
Burst at shipped 4,000 per second limit80,000 HTTP 200; confirm p99 15.7 msLocal per instance baseline
13,333 offered per second with limit134,263 HTTP 429; zero 503; accepted confirm p99 49.5 msBackpressure worked; offered load was not all admitted
13,333 offered with limit lifted11,426 per second sustained; zero 503; confirm p99 49.8 msServlet concurrency, rather than broker confirm timeout, limited this local run
Replay plus live traffic2,667 replay plus 1,333 live per second for 60 seconds; 240,000 HTTP 200; zero 429 or 503Byte identical replay was absorbed and metered; no new alert; queue drained within 40 seconds after replay

Gateways were confirmed to jitter their reporting, so the 10 times burst target of 13,333 per second remains configured headroom rather than the expected average. A first 24 hour arrival histogram still needs to validate real traffic before reducing capacity. Heimdal agreed a two times replay cap, but Stats App cannot prove the upstream sender honours it.

The drills used one laptop and one instance of each service, generally one stat type. They demonstrate mechanisms and bottlenecks, not production capacity certification. Load generation is open loop, so it keeps offered rate fixed rather than slowing down with the server. Replay reuses identical bodies; changing timestamps or UID would create new records and test the wrong behaviour.

The decision

Measure overload and replay with the real shape

Decision. Use open loop offered traffic and byte identical replay, then compare persistence, duplicate and alert outcomes.

Why. A client that slows itself under overload hides the ceiling; restamping replay turns duplicates into new records.

Effect. The local burst found servlet concurrency as the ceiling. Replay at 2,667 plus 1,333 live per second added no new alert; neither result certifies production scale.

DL-104 · DL-109 · DL-159 · DL-161

Tests protect failure paths and isolation

CI builds with the Gradle wrapper, real PostgreSQL and RabbitMQ Testcontainers, schema fixtures, OpenAPI lint, schema parity, alert rule tests and dashboard checks. Lint validates contract structure; parity separately proves the inlined type schemas agree with the authoritative catalogue. Assertion counts are checked so a shortened test set cannot appear green. Database capability tests exercise real grants, and the API uses actual signed RS256 tokens and the real decoder rather than authentication mocks.

DL 162 records 228 API tests, covering generated SQL, cursors, security, GET only behaviour, reads, reconstruction, adversarial tenant fixtures and pagination during concurrent writes. Mutation checks showed that changing the tenant filter to maintenant failed 53 tests, dropping tenant cursor binding failed two, allowing non GET failed 56 and dropping the UID tie breaker failed 19. These are recorded implementation results, not tests rerun while preparing this handover.

The current source Postman suite contains 46 request definitions: three readiness checks, three token requests, ten ingest/replay requests, twenty-two reads, six negative API checks and two broker/metrics checks. It covers all five types, LTE measurement/error variants, duplicate LTE/network replay, four read route shapes, 25 quorum queues and five persistence counters. The 9 October run of the earlier 40-request collection recorded 99 assertions with zero failures, independently of the repository build tests. Those figures do not represent a run of the updated 46-request collection.

The decision

A passing dashboard test is not enough

Decision. Confirm all panel and alert metrics exist on the deployed scrape.

Why. The first live render found that the enum archive_mode was absent even though alert unit tests passed.

Effect. A custom read only exporter query supplies the metric; known Input counters now exist at startup.

DL-158 · DL-160

5.2 Package the working system for recipients

The 29 September v3 bundle delivered three persisted types and six running services. The later v4 added explicit accepted JSON, v5 added LTE/network persistence, and the current stats-app-docker-bundle-v6-prometheus-autostart includes ten images, monitoring configuration and offline Grafana assets. Eight containers run by default. The current startup always loads supplied images, refuses new credential generation against existing data volumes, checks existing healthy PostgreSQL credentials, waits for health, then verifies monitoring. See the updated v6 setup guide; its legacy filename is retained for existing links.

The trap

Keep the evaluation boundary explicit

Decision. Use unauthenticated Input only for isolated local evaluation; inspect RabbitMQ and Keycloak with generated local credentials.

Why. The offline bundle should be easy to run, but the local auth mode does not provide a production ingestion identity.

Effect. Both ingest doors accept locally. Ordinary shutdown retains database and broker volumes; Keycloak container state is reimported from the realm template.

DL-063 · DL-064 · DL-151

The operational readiness decisions

Expand a dated record for the complete rationale, alternatives, consequences and verification. These records belong to this phase. Later amendments remain visible in their historical context.

DL-082 — Sized for a 10× synchronised burst; alert on oldest-message age, never queue depth

Whether 160,000 gateways report on the boundary or spread across the cycle is worth 10× on hardware, and it is unanswered (Q13b). Sizing for the pessimistic case is recoverable; sizing for the optimistic case is an outage.

> AMENDED 4 Sep 2026 by [DL-104](#dl-104--gateways-jitter-across-the-cycle-so-the-10-burst-becomes-headroom-rather-than-the-expected-case). > Q13b is answered: gateways jitter across the cycle. 13,333/s is now > configured headroom, not the expected case — expected steady state is the > average, ~1,333/s — and hardware is not cut until the ts.server mod 600 > histogram confirms it (T-050). One reading inverts: a cyclic depth spike was > *the buffer working*; under jitter it is evidence the answer is wrong.

18 Aug 2026 · ANSWERED (Q13a; amended by Q13b, 4 Sep 2026) · ADR-003, 2B-ANSWERS-18AUG2026.md

Decision. Assume synchronised 10-minute reporting boundaries until Heimdal telemetry proves otherwise: design target 13,333/s (10× average — 800,000 records per cycle landing in 60 s). Input sized for peak, Writer for drain (~2,000/s per type), prefetch 500, max_wal_size sized for the burst. Alert on oldest-message age, never on depth.

Why. Whether 160,000 gateways report on the boundary or spread across the cycle is worth 10× on hardware, and it is unanswered (Q13b). Sizing for the pessimistic case is recoverable; sizing for the optimistic case is an outage.

The alerting rule is the operationally important half: queue depth spiking each cycle is the buffer working. An alert on depth would fire every ten minutes forever and be muted within a week. Oldest-message age distinguishes a buffer absorbing a burst from a consumer that has stopped — which is the thing anyone actually wants to know.

Rejected. Sizing for the average — the boundary case is a 10× under-provision. Depth alerts — guaranteed alert fatigue on healthy behaviour.

Consequence. 503 is the backpressure channel and is the mechanism working, not an incident. Q13b remains the single most valuable outstanding question.

DL-084 — RPO/RTO is a product decision, and engineering will not substitute for it

RPO is the tight one because lost data is unrecoverable: Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so anything we returned 200 for and then lost is gone. RTO is deliberately loose — this is 90-day telemetry, not financial records, and at multi-terabyte scale a tight RTO may not be purchasable at any sensible price.

> RATIFIED 4 Sep 2026 — see [DL-111](#dl-111--rpo--5-min-rto-1-h-per-partition-and-24-h-full-cluster--signed). > The proposal below is signed as written by Naman Vashishtha. T-051 is > unblocked, and archiver lag joins T-050's alert set — a stalled archiver > silently converts RPO from 5 minutes to *"however long ago it stopped"*.

29 Aug 2026 · RATIFIED 4 Sep 2026 (was PROPOSED) · ADR-003 D5a

Decision. Engineering proposes RPO ≤ 5 min via continuous WAL archiving, and RTO 1 h for a single partition, 24 h for a full cluster restore. This is input to a product and operations decision, not a replacement for one. T-051 stays blocked.

Why. RPO is the tight one because lost data is unrecoverable: Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so anything we returned 200 for and then lost is gone. RTO is deliberately loose — this is 90-day telemetry, not financial records, and at multi-terabyte scale a tight RTO may not be purchasable at any sensible price.

Why it was not closed with the other five on 29 August: the other decisions were engineering's to take. This one has a cost that engineering does not pay.

Rejected. Adopting the proposal unilaterally — sets a production commitment nobody agreed to fund.

Consequence. Take it to product as a question about consequences: *"if we lost the last 15 minutes, or took 8 hours to come back, what actually breaks?"*

DL-104 — Gateways jitter across the cycle, so the 10× burst becomes headroom rather than the expected case

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q13b) · DL-082, ADR-003, Q13a · T-048, T-050

Decision. ZC confirms gateways spread their reporting across the 10-minute cycle rather than firing on the boundary. Three consequences, and one deliberate non-consequence:

1. Expected steady-state ingest is the average — roughly 1,333/s across five types, not 13,333/s. Q5a's 115.2M records/day is unchanged; only its distribution across the cycle is. 2. DL-082's 13,333/s survives as configured headroom, not as the provisioning baseline. Prefetch, consumer concurrency, replica count and max_wal_size stay externalised, so reaching the old figure is a property change and not a redeploy. 3. T-050 gains a day-one verification: a histogram of ts.server mod 600 over the first 24 hours of real ingest. Hardware is not cut until that histogram exists. 4. Alert on oldest-message age, never on queue depth — unchanged. It was never a consequence of the burst shape.

Why the histogram still matters after an answer. This is the question the 29 Aug status note recorded as *not actually askable-only*: it is answerable from our own first day of ingest, and a measurement outranks a statement about firmware behaviour across 160,000 devices of several generations. Cutting capacity on the statement and discovering the boundary case in production is a 10× under-provision during the exact minute it matters.

What inverts. Under DL-082 a cyclic queue-depth spike was *the buffer working as designed*. Under jitter it is evidence this answer is wrong — the same graph, the opposite meaning. That reading belongs in T-050's runbook note, because nothing in the metric itself says which regime you are in.

Rejected. *Cutting the sizing immediately* — the saving is real but unverified, and it is recoverable in one direction only. *Leaving DL-082 unamended* — it would have left the repository asserting a 10× synchronised burst as fact after ZC said otherwise, which is how the SRS field names survived for months.

Consequence. Q13b closes. It was the highest-value outstanding question on the sheet; what it bought is permission to provision against the average once measured, not an immediate change to any code. DL-082's decision text is amended in place.

DL-109 — Heimdal agrees the 2× replay cap, and it is still not something we can enforce

4 Sep 2026 · ANSWERED (Q13c) · ADR-005, Q13a, ADR-002 §12 · T-049, T-052, T-018

Decision. ZC agrees to cap post-outage replay at 2× nominal (2,667/s). T-049 (replay drill) is unblocked and rehearses that figure: a 6-hour outage is 28.8M messages and roughly 3 hours of replay at the cap, holding total ingest near 4,000/s.

Why not one defence changes. An agreement is a commitment, not a control. Nothing in the Stats App can make Heimdal honour it, and the damaging failure mode is not the breach — it is tuning our own backpressure away as redundant because a commitment exists. So all of it stays: 503 is the backpressure channel, prefetch stays bounded, and T-018's rate limit remains the only control we actually own. A 503 during replay is the mechanism working, not an incident.

Consequence. T-049 asserts that records_duplicate_suppressed spikes and that no alert fires on it — replayed records are duplicates by definition and ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING absorbs them (ADR-005). T-052's redrive runbook states the 2× figure as an agreed number and states plainly that it is unenforced on our side, so that a future operator does not read it as a guarantee. Q13c closes.

DL-111 — RPO ≤ 5 min, RTO 1 h per partition and 24 h full cluster — signed

4 Sep 2026 · RATIFIED (D5a) · ADR-003 D5, DL-084, BR-30, BR-35, NFR-04 · T-051, T-050

Decision. The engineering proposal of 29 Aug 2026 is signed by Naman Vashishtha on 4 September 2026: RPO ≤ 5 minutes via continuous WAL archiving; RTO 1 hour to restore a single partition, 24 hours for a full cluster restore. DL-084 moves from PROPOSED to ratified and T-051 is unblocked.

Why RPO is the tight number and RTO is deliberately loose. Lost data is unrecoverable: Heimdal retries only on a non-2xx, so anything we answered 200 for and then lost is gone, and no amount of restore time recovers it. RTO is loose because this is 90-day telemetry rather than financial records, and at multi-terabyte scale a tight RTO may not be purchasable at any sensible price.

What signing obliges, beyond a number in a document. A target nobody measures is not a target:

* Continuous WAL archiving must actually be configured and its lag monitored. A stalled archiver silently converts RPO from 5 minutes to *"however long ago it stopped"* — the failure is invisible until the restore. Archiver lag joins T-050's alert set. * T-051 must be timed, not merely completed. It restores a single partition and re-attaches it *without taking the parent table offline*, and the elapsed time is compared against the 1-hour figure.

Consequence. T-051 moves BLOCKED → TODO. D5a leaves the open-questions sheet.

DL-143 — Consumer shutdown finishes the delivery in hand and returns the rest to the broker; an interrupt is lifecycle, never a failed record

14 Sep 2026 (built) · 17 Sep 2026 (merged, recorded) · ENGINEERING · ADR-005, NFR-06, DL-122 · T-053

Decision. The lorastats listener container is configured forceStop=true with a validated, externally configured stats.consumer.lorastats.shutdown-timeout (5 s locally, no zero or negative value accepted). On stop, the delivery currently executing is allowed to finish inside that grace; every merely *prefetched* delivery is returned to RabbitMQ unacknowledged when the channel closes. The Compose writer container gets stop_grace_period: 10s — longer than the listener's grace — so Spring can close the channel before the runtime sends SIGKILL.

The rule that matters. LorastatsConsumer catches InterruptedException *before* the general Exception arm, re-sets the interrupt flag and rethrows. An interrupt during shutdown is not a failed record: it must not be routed to a retry tier (which would spend an attempt on a record that did nothing wrong) and it must not be ACKed (which would discard the broker's only copy). Leaving the original unacknowledged is what makes the broker requeue it. ADR-005's ordering is preserved — persistence precedes ACK, and an unconfirmed hand-off is never followed by an ACK.

Why forceStop. Without it the container drains its whole prefetch window (500 records at 1 consumer) before stopping, which turns every rolling deploy into a wait of one prefetch window per replica and, under a stop_grace_period shorter than that, into a SIGKILL mid-batch — the crash window T-030 rehearses, taken on purpose on every deploy.

Proved. LorastatsConsumerIntegrationTest against a real broker: work completed inside the grace is ACKed while prefetched work is requeued; work exceeding the timeout returns to the main queue and never enters retry tier one. Unit, activation and configuration tests cover the interrupt contract and the property validation.

Consequence, recorded on merge. The branch shipped without a decision entry; this is it. The catch (InterruptedException) arm is the one place in the consumer where an exception escapes without a retry or a DLQ copy — that is intentional and it is shutdown-only. If a future change introduces a *non*-shutdown interrupt, the delivery it was handling stays unacknowledged until the channel closes; that is the trade accepted here, and it is the safe direction.

DL-144 — DLQ alerting is three Prometheus rules on dlq_depth, and "continuously non-empty for N days" stands in for oldest-message age

16 Sep 2026 (built) · 17 Sep 2026 (merged, recorded) · ENGINEERING · ADR-002 §12, DL-137, DL-138 · T-060, T-050

Decision. ops/prometheus/dlq-alerts.yml evaluates max by (type) (dlq_depth): StatsAppDlqContainsMessages (info, immediately on > 0), StatsAppDlqUndisposedWarning (for: 7d) and StatsAppDlqUndisposedCritical (for: 12d) — half of and two days before the 14-day manual-disposition window ADR-002 §12 sets. Replicas aggregate with max, never sum, per DL-137. Monitoring is passive: no Writer code inspects, consumes or requeues a DLQ message, and there is no application property that pretends to configure an external evaluator. promtool check rules and promtool test rules (pinned prom/prometheus:v3.5.0) run in CI over a behaviour suite covering empty, immediate, per-type, multi-replica, warning, critical and reset states.

What this substitutes, and why it is recorded. ADR-002 §12 says *"alert on depth > 0 and oldest-message age."* The Writer exposes no oldest-message-age gauge for the DLQ (DL-137 built dlq_depth, and a polled age would need a broker-side timestamp on every dead-lettered message), so T-060 alerts on how long the queue has been continuously non-empty instead. The two agree whenever the DLQ is only ever drained by a redrive that empties it — which is the runbook's procedure — and disagree when an operator redrives *some* records and leaves others: the duration timer keeps running from the oldest surviving record's arrival, which is the conservative direction. The residual is that the for: clock lives in Prometheus, not in the data: a Prometheus restart restores for state from ALERTS_FOR_STATE only within its lookback, so a 12-day timer can be shortened by an evaluator outage. Oldest-message age remains the better signal and stays owed — it is the same gauge T-050 needs for the main queues, and when T-050 builds it the DLQ gets it for free. ADR-002 §12's wording is left as the target, not amended down to what was built.

What T-060 does not cover. The main-queue oldest-message-age alert (T-050) — under jitter (DL-104) depth is a diagnostic, not the alert. records_expired_on_arrival non-zero (D5b, DL-112) and records_upstream_status_not_ok after the first 24 h (DL-128 residual) are T-050's too. This entry closes the DLQ half of "nobody is watching".

DL-155 — The per-partition restore is rehearsed, timed and written down; WAL archiving is on and watched

21 September 2026 · OPERATIONS · ADR-003 D1/D5, DL-111 (RPO ≤ 5 min, RTO 1 h per partition), DL-132, DL-149 · T-051

Trigger. T-051 unblocked in full on 10 Sep (T-025 built the partitions it restores) and was the first Phase 5 row on Next Up. D5a is signed at *RTO 1 h per partition*, and a target nobody measures is not a target.

What was found before anything was written. Three things a runbook written from the documentation alone would have got wrong, each checked against PostgreSQL 17 in a container before the procedure was recorded:

1. A plain pg_restore of a partition dump works and reports failure. pg_dump -t <partition> orders its entries CREATE TABLE, ATTACH PARTITION, COPY, then the child's PRIMARY KEY and CREATE INDEX. Restored as-is the empty child is attached to the live parent *first* — PostgreSQL creates the child's indexes at that moment — the rows load one by one into a partition the API can already read, and the dump's own index statements then fail: *"multiple primary keys … are not allowed"*, *"relation … already exists"*, "errors ignored on restore: 2", exit 1. Correct data, alarming exit, a half-visible day for the length of the load. 2. Attach without a matching CHECK scans every row under the lock. With one, the attach is a catalogue change: 0.66 ms on a 600,000-row day in the probe, 1 ms in the drill. 3. A fresh Compose volume is root-owned, so archiving fails silently. archive_mode=on with the archive on a new named volume: failed_count=7, archived_count=0, nothing else wrong anywhere. The archiver retries forever and reports only in pg_stat_archiver.

Decision.

1. docs/runbooks/partition-restore.md — the ADR-003 D5 shape, load-then-attach: pg_restore -l, drop the TABLE ATTACH and INDEX ATTACH TOC entries, restore the child standalone (indexes built after the load), verify every row is inside the day the name says (the same invariant PartitionRetention checks before it drops), add a CHECK matching the bound, ATTACH PARTITION (takes SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE on the parent — inserts continue), drop the scaffolding, then four post-attach checks (parent count, inhdetachpending = false, three valid attached indexes, no leftover constraint). Four preconditions, the first of which is the trap the tracker carried in: pause the sweep or confirm the day is inside RetentionWindow, or the next maintenance pass drops the evidence. Two sources — the nightly per-partition dump and PITR to a scratch instance — converge on one .dump file, so the procedure is the same. 2. PartitionRestoreDrillIntegrationTest — the runbook's commands run inside the real PostgreSQL 17 container against a 200,000-row lorastats day, with a writer inserting into the neighbouring day throughout. Asserts every step-7 check, zero writer failures, no insert slower than one second, and the total under one hour; prints the timing table. Measured: 1.229 s end to end (dump 0.29 s, standalone restore 0.88 s, attach 1 ms); 6,517 concurrent inserts, p99 0.3 ms, worst 55 ms, none failed. The runbook's numbers are the drill's output, and the drill runs in every build, so they cannot drift apart. 3. WAL archiving on in Compose — archive_mode=on, archive_timeout=300, the archive on its own postgres-wal-archive volume, and the entrypoint wrapped to chown that directory before the stock entrypoint runs (finding 3). Verified: archived_count advancing, segments present on the volume. 4. ops/prometheus/postgres-alerts.yml — StatsAppWalArchiveFailing (failures in each of five minutes, critical), StatsAppWalArchiveStale (nothing archived for 15 min while archive_mode is on — a *hanging* command counts no failure, critical), StatsAppWalArchiveDisabled (30 min, warning) and StatsAppPostgresExporterDown (pg_up == 0; without it the other three go silent rather than fire). Five promtool scenarios, including the measured root-owned-volume failure and a scratch instance with archiving off that must not page. Wired into CI beside the other two files. 5. A Compose observability profile — postgres_exporter (read-only identity, mapped inside the container because the compose file carries no ${…} by design) and a Prometheus scraping the three modules and the exporter with all three rule files. Grafana joins it under T-056.

Rejected. *Restoring inside PGDATA or with the row-by-row pg_restore* — finding 1. *Skipping the CHECK because the drill's day is small* — finding 2 is the difference between a millisecond and minutes on a 23-million-row production day, and the RTO depends on it. *A postgres_exporter sidecar in the default profile* — it is observability, not the stack; and its image could not be pulled on 21 Sep (no registry access), so the rules are tested against the documented series names and flagged in ops/prometheus/README.md to be verified on the first live scrape.

Residual. The drill measures a 200k-row day on a laptop; a production day is ~23M rows per type, and PITR replay on a scratch instance — not the steps here — is where a real restore spends its hour. The nightly per-partition dump job the runbook's first source assumes is operations' to schedule; the drill's step 1 is the command it runs.

DL-156 — Advisory-lock contention is drilled between two replicas and two processes; a sweep that never runs is now visible

21 September 2026 · ENGINEERING / OPERATIONS · ADR-003 D3/D8, STA-23C, DL-132, DL-137 · T-054

Trigger. T-054: PartitionRetentionIntegrationTest proved a replica that loses the lock changes nothing, but it held the lock from a second connection of the *same* pool. The lock is session-level, so the unit PostgreSQL distinguishes is the pool. And D8 runs N replicas per type, so two replicas firing the same cron at the same second is the production default.

Decision — the drill, in two halves.

1. AdvisoryLockContentionIntegrationTest builds two complete maintenance stacks — each its own HikariDataSource, PartitionMaintenance, registry and clock — against one database. Four cases: (a) replica B's cron fires while A is mid-maintenance (deterministic, A holds the lock on one of *its* sessions behind a latch): B skips both halves at DEBUG, drops nothing, and does the work on its next run; (b) twenty simultaneous rounds behind a barrier: every partition dropped exactly once across both registries, no retention_failed or partition_precreation_failed, no line at WARN or above — and in all twenty rounds the loser genuinely had to skip, because whole-window pre-creation (DL-149) holds the lock long enough to collide; (c) the failure path: work that throws leaves no advisory lock behind and the other replica runs immediately; (d) the pools are clean: every physical connection of both pools is borrowed and checked — session TimeZone back to the server default, auto-commit on, no backend of either pool idle in transaction. That last assertion is the one the tracker asked for by name: a session-level lock leaked on a pooled connection outlives the borrow and starves the type on every replica. 2. ops/drills/t054-advisory-lock-contention.sh — the literal two-*process* reading: two extra Writer containers from the stack's own image, both serving lorastats with the cron at the top of every minute, two ticks against the live Compose PostgreSQL. Run 21 Sep 2026: on both ticks the replicas collided; each won once and lost once (ensured=1 skipped=1 on both, retention applied/no-op versus skipped); no failure, no ERROR, no advisory lock held afterwards, no drill backend idle in transaction.

What the drill found on the live stack, and the fix. The stack's own Writer had logged nothing for 29 hours — including the 03:30 UTC pass it should log at INFO — while retention_oldest_partition_age_days read 0.0 and readiness was UP. Cause: the host had slept across the cron slot, and a paused Docker VM freezes the JVM's monotonic scheduler delay, so the pass never fired. A laptop artefact — but it exposed a gap the T-054 note predicts: a leaked lock would produce the same picture, and nothing would report it. retention_oldest_partition_age_days was registered at zero and set only by a completed sweep, so a sweep that never runs reads as a partition zero days old. DL-137's rule for the broker gauges — *never observed is NaN, never zero* — now applies here: both retention gauges are NaN until the replica's first completed sweep, and the sweep stamps a new retention_last_sweep_timestamp_seconds{type} as its last act (a run that dies half-way reports nothing). StatsAppRetentionSweepMissing (critical, for: 30m) reads it max by (type) — only the lock winner stamps it, and max ignores NaN unless every replica is NaN, which the second arm catches once process_uptime_seconds exceeds a schedule period plus slack. Three promtool scenarios, including a replica that never wins staying quiet beside one that sweeps. The retention integration test asserts NaN before the first sweep, the stamp after it, and that the loser does not stamp.

Rejected. *Two Spring contexts in one JVM* for half 1 — heavier, and no closer to what the lock distinguishes than two pools. *A polled "last sweep" reader* — DL-132 rejected polling for the age gauge for the same reason it would be wrong here: a poll reports a healthy number from a replica whose sweep never ran. *Alerting on the age gauge alone* — a never-run sweep leaves it wherever it was; only a timestamp the sweep itself writes can say "stopped". *Treating the host-sleep finding as a product defect* — it is not one; it is recorded in DEVELOPMENT.md §3.1 as a local gotcha, and the alert is what covers the production causes.

Verification. AdvisoryLockContentionIntegrationTest (4), the drill script's eight checks against the live stack, PartitionRetentionIntegrationTest (+2 assertions), promtool test rules writer-alerts.test.yml (8 rules, 3 new scenarios), full build.

DL-157 — DETACH CONCURRENTLY against a live writer is measured, with a control that can block

21 September 2026 · OPERATIONS · ADR-003 D1, STA-23C, DL-132 · T-055

Trigger. T-055: *"Measure. A plain detach takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock against the writer."* The sweep has used CONCURRENTLY since T-025 on the strength of the documentation; nothing had watched a writer while it ran.

Decision. DetachConcurrentlyLatencyIntegrationTest runs a writer inserting into the live day as fast as the round trip allows, recording every statement's latency, and a sampler counting backends waiting on a lock ten times a second — distinguishing waiting *inserts* from the waiting DDL — across four phases: baseline; the real sweep dropping a 300,000-row day; the real sweep with a reader holding an open transaction on the parent (the case the tracker warns turns a measurement into a mis-diagnosis); and a control, a plain DETACH behind the same reader. The control is what makes the test honest: if it did not stall the writer, "the sweep did not stall the writer" would prove nothing.

Measured. Baseline worst insert 5.4 ms. Sweep: 17 ms, worst insert 1.9 ms, nothing waiting. Sweep behind the reader: the sweep waited 1.56 s — DETACH CONCURRENTLY waits for transactions that reference the partition — while 13,436 inserts continued, worst 13 ms, no insert ever queued; the one waiter was the detach itself. Control: worst insert 1,515 ms, an insert seen waiting on the parent's lock. Assertions: the sweep's worst insert under five times the baseline's in both phases, no insert waiting in phase 3, the control's worst insert at least the reader's hold minus slack and an insert waiting in phase 4, and — every phase — pg_inherits.inhdetachpending at zero (trap 27).

Rejected. *Measuring only the sweep's duration* — phase 3 shows it is the wrong number: 1.56 s for a 17 ms job, and the cause was the harness's reader. *Skipping the control* — see above. *A bigger day* — 300,000 rows is enough for the detach to be non-trivial (the drop is a catalogue operation either way); the lock behaviour does not scale with rows, the validation of a reader-blocked detach does.

Consequence. ops/drills/README.md indexes the Phase 5 drills with their measured numbers; T-051, T-054 and T-055 run in every build.

DL-158 — Dashboards are generated and gated, and every series they read exports from startup

21 September 2026 · OPERATIONS · NFR-05, DL-136, DL-137, DL-147, DL-151, DL-156 · T-056

Trigger. T-056: per-type throughput, errors, latency, queue depth, DLQ depth, readiness; owners named. Its note carried two rules (aggregate broker gauges with max; render the poll status beside them) and one gap: six Writer series were asserted only at the registry, never at /actuator/prometheus by their exported names.

Decision.

1. Three dashboards — Writer (throughput, errors, latency, queues, retention, readiness), Input (the two doors, HTTP outcomes, the Q13b heatmap), PostgreSQL (the archiver) — generated by ops/grafana/build-dashboards.py into committed JSON and provisioned read-only by the Compose observability profile, which gains Grafana. Owners are named in ops/grafana/README.md: an owner is who the same series page and who answers "what does this mean", not who edits JSON. 2. ops/grafana/check-dashboards.py gates in CI: the JSON matches the generator; every metric name in every panel *and every alert rule* is a series the code exports (string literals in main source plus Micrometer suffixes, one composed name with its reason) or a named infrastructure series; no sum over a shared gauge (DL-137). Mutation-tested: a stale JSON, a typo'd series and a sum by (type) (queue_depth) are each a failing build. A panel over a series that does not exist renders as an empty graph — indistinguishable from "nothing happened" — which is the one thing a dashboard must never say by accident. 3. The six series export from startup, and are asserted at the endpoint. records_payload_rejected{type,reason} and records_upstream_status_not_ok{type} were created on first event; records_expired_on_arrival, the two retention counters and the age gauge were registered by the startup maintenance pass, which a deployment that hands partition DDL to a separate job (pre-create-on-startup=false) never runs. Both now register per type at writer construction — a deployed type is exactly one with a writer — so they export at zero (the gate's closed reason set is three series, not a cardinality risk) or NaN (DL-156) from the first scrape. ManagementSurfaceTest asserts all seven by exported name, NaN included.

Rejected. *Hand-written JSON* — 32 panels of it, unreviewable, and the two rules would be re-derived per panel. *A dashboard sum over dlq_depth "because there is one broker"* — there are N replicas polling it. *Pre-registering records_dead_lettered{reason} and records_retried{tier}* — reason is an exception class name and cannot be enumerated; the README says so and points at dlq_depth. *A confirm-latency panel now* — Input exports no such histogram until T-048; a panel over a missing series is the defect the checker exists for.

Residual. Grafana and postgres_exporter images could not be pulled on 21 Sep (no registry access), so the dashboards were validated by the checker and by reading, not by rendering; first render is the first task on a networked machine. Rendered 22 Sep 2026 — DL-160, which found one series the checker had taken on trust and one rule this entry had applied to the Writer but not to Input.

DL-159 — The burst is measured at the headroom: the confirm holds, the servlet threads are the ceiling, and Input exports the histogram that says so

21 September 2026 · ENGINEERING / OPERATIONS · NFR-04, STA-04A, ADR-003 D8, DL-104 (Q13a), DL-109 (Q13c), DL-121 (T-018), DL-158 · T-048

Trigger. T-048: *"13,333/s design target, not the 1,333/s average. Measure p99 publisher-confirm latency under burst — confirm latency, not throughput, is what turns a burst into 503s."* Input exported no confirm timer, so the number the task asks for did not exist to be read.

Decision.

1. records_publish_confirm_seconds{type,outcome} — a Micrometer Timer in IngestMetrics with fixed edges 1 ms … 5 s (the confirm timeout at the top), recorded in ConfirmingStatsPublisher for every outcome — confirmed, nack, returned, timeout, error — from the hand-off to the broker to the answer that decides the HTTP status. No source label: the publisher does not know the door and a slow confirm is the same incident from either. The confirmed series is registered for all five types at construction so the dashboard's p99 exists before the first record (DL-158's rule); ManagementSurfaceTest asserts it at the endpoint. The Input dashboard gains the p50/p99 and outcome panels. 2. ops/load/BurstLoad.java — a dependency-free, single-file Java 21 generator: open loop at a fixed offered rate on virtual threads (a closed loop slows down when the server does and measures the client's patience; a Heimdal replay does not slow down), unique uid and re-stamped ts.* per record (no ON CONFLICT absorption, no clock-skew or late-arrival rejection), per-second 503s, client percentiles, and a before/after scrape of the histogram reduced to p50/p99 the way histogram_quantile does. ops/drills/t048-burst-load.sh runs the three-run shape. 3. docs/runbooks/load-test-burst.md records the first measurement (one instance, this laptop, sharing CPU with the generator and the whole stack): * A 4,000/s (the shipped per-instance limit): 80,000 × 200, confirm p99 15.7 ms. * B 13,333/s against that limit: 134,263 × 429, zero 503s, confirm p99 49.5 ms on the ~4,250/s that got through — the backpressure channel working (DL-121). * C 13,333/s with the limit lifted: 11,426/s sustained, 230,219 × 200, zero 503s, confirm p99 49.8 ms — one hundredth of the timeout — and client p50 127 ms. The gap is queueing in front of the confirm: Tomcat's default 200 request threads, each held for one ~17 ms confirm, is ≈ 11,700 req/s, which is where it stopped.

What was learned. The broker is not where a burst breaks; the servlet thread pool is, and it is sized *with* the rate limiter: at 4,000/s × 50 ms p99 = 200 concurrent = the default. The two defaults agree, and the trap is raising one without the other — recorded beside max-requests-per-second in application.properties, with the sizing rule threads.max ≈ rate × confirm p99 (measured at that rate). ADR-003 D8's answer to the headroom is instances, and this measurement says four at the shipped limit.

Rejected. *k6 / vegeta / hey* — none installed, none pinned, and the histogram read-back needs code anyway. *A closed-loop generator* — measures the wrong thing under overload. *Raising server.tomcat.threads.max now* — the shipped limit and the shipped pool agree; a change is a sizing decision for the deployment shape (D8), with this runbook as its input. *Running with the limit lifted as the only run* — run B is the production behaviour; run C is the answer to the question.

Residual. One instance, one laptop, generator and stack sharing CPU: the shape is the finding, the absolutes are conservative. Run C left 244,371 records for the single local Writer to drain — the T-050 alert's job, watched, not this test's. The DLQ took nothing.

DL-160 — The first render: an enum setting is not a metric, and DL-158's rule reaches Input

22 September 2026 · OPERATIONS · NFR-05, ADR-003 D5, DL-111, DL-155, DL-158, DL-159 · T-051, T-056 (first render — Next Up row 7)

Trigger. The Compose observability profile was built on 21 Sep on a machine with no registry access (DL-155, DL-158 residuals). First task on a networked machine: pull the images, render the three dashboards, confirm every panel draws, and confirm the pg_stat_archiver_* names on a live scrape. Done against the running stack with images rebuilt from the current checkout — the containers that were up were two days and eleven commits old, and a render against them would have proved nothing about DL-156…DL-159's series.

Found.

1. pg_settings_archive_mode was never exported. postgres_exporter's settings collector exports numeric and boolean settings only; archive_mode is an enum (off | on | always) and is skipped. StatsAppWalArchiveStale is gated and on (instance) pg_settings_archive_mode == 1 and StatsAppWalArchiveDisabled is pg_settings_archive_mode == 0: a comparison against an absent series matches nothing, so both alerts were silent by construction — with promtool test rules green, because the unit tests *supply* the series. The PostgreSQL dashboard's "archive_mode on" stat was empty. This is the failure the rule file's own last comment warns about (*"an absent series compares equal to nothing"*), one rule up from where it was looking. 2. Five Input counters appeared only on their first event — records_validation_failed, records_clock_skew_rejected, records_publish_failed, records_unroutable, records_ts_device_corrected. DL-158 registered the Writer's gate and retention series at construction and DL-159 the confirm timer, and stopped there. On a fresh Input the dashboard's "Rejected / s" and "Not accepted after the broker (503) / s" panels were empty, which is the same picture as *this instance exports no such series*. The first check missed it: Prometheus's five-minute staleness window was still answering rate(...[5m]) from the previous container's series. 3. The wal collector fails every scrape under the read-only role (pg_ls_waldir() needs pg_monitor) and logs an ERROR in PostgreSQL every fifteen seconds. Nothing reads it. 4. The stats-api scrape target is down (401). By design until T-038: ApiSecurityConfiguration keeps /actuator/prometheus behind the bearer token and says scrape access is that task's decision. No panel or rule reads a stats-api series.

Decision.

1. ops/postgres-exporter/queries.yml defines pg_settings_archive_mode as a custom query (current_setting('archive_mode') IN ('on','always') → 1/0, run as stats_ro, which needs no privilege for it); the Compose profile loads it with --extend.query-path. The rules and the dashboard are unchanged — the fix is to make the series exist, not to re-derive the alerts from a proxy. check-dashboards.py and ops/prometheus/README.md say where the series comes from, because on any other exporter deployment the query has to travel with the rules or the two alerts are decoration again. 2. IngestMetrics registers every per-record counter for every type and both doors at construction (sixty-five series; both label sets are closed). unknown type and source stay first-event — they mean *could not tell* and have no pair to enumerate. ManagementSurfaceTest asserts every name × type × door at /actuator/prometheus before any event; four assertions elsewhere that used *the counter does not exist* to mean *the counter did not move* now say the latter. 3. --no-collector.wal on the exporter. The boring fix: nothing reads the collector, and granting pg_monitor to the read-only role is a privilege change for a series nobody asked for. 4. The stats-api target stays in prometheus.yml, commented as expected-down until T-038, so its absence is not rediscovered as an outage when the API lands.

Result. 51 panel expressions across the three dashboards, 0 errors; empties reduced to the two first-event-by-design series (records_retried_total, records_dead_lettered_total) plus the retention gauges that are NaN until the first sweep (DL-156). Three of the four pg_stat_archiver_* names confirmed on a live scrape; pg_settings_archive_mode = 1 from the custom query; pg_stat_archiver_failed_count = 0 with archive_mode=on, archive_timeout=300 after the T-051 recreate. Active alerts: StatsAppDlqContainsMessages firing on residue from earlier drills (lorastats 6, sysstats 3) — the rule working. stats-input 77 tests, 0 failures.

Rejected. *Gating the stale alert on pg_settings_archive_timeout_seconds > 0* — a proxy: archive_mode=off with a timeout set is exactly the silent case, and the dashboard stat would still read nothing. *Granting pg_monitor to stats_ro for the wal collector* — a role change for a series nothing reads. *Registering unknown series at startup* — the label means the pair could not be determined; enumerating it would invent one. *Opening /actuator/prometheus on the API for the scrape* — T-038's decision, not a render's side effect.

What was learned. A rule file that is unit-tested with hand-supplied series proves its *logic*, never that the series exist — the same gap DL-158 closed for panels with check-dashboards.py, which took the exporter's names on documented trust and said so in a comment. The names the checker calls "infrastructure" are the ones it cannot verify from source, and the first live scrape is the only test of them. Second: "exports from startup" is a rule for *every* module's counters, and applying it to one module and one histogram left the next module's panels reading empty on a healthy day.

Residual. The first-24-h histogram reading and the upstream-status threshold (Next Up row 1) are what the rendered dashboards are for and remain owed. The Prometheus staleness window (5 min) means a panel check straight after a container restart can still see the previous container's series — wait it out or check /actuator/prometheus directly. --extend.query-path is deprecated upstream in favour of collectors; if a future exporter release drops it, the query becomes a custom collector and the metric name must not change.

DL-161 — The replay is rehearsed: the same bytes at the agreed cap, absorbed and metered, and nothing alerts

22 September 2026 · OPERATIONS · NFR-04, STA-25A, ADR-005, DL-109 (Q13c), DL-118, DL-121, DL-133, DL-159 · T-049

Trigger. T-049: *"2,667/s replay on top of live traffic. records_duplicate_suppressed will spike — assert NO alert fires. A 503 during replay is the backpressure channel working, not an incident."* Unblocked since DL-109; the last Phase 5 row that needed only the stack.

Decision.

1. The replay is byte-identical. BurstLoad gains --save-bodies (every sent body, kept as sent, RS-separated) and --replay (those bodies again, cyclically, at the target rate, no re-stamping). A replayed record with a fresh uid or ts.* is a new record to the Writer and measures nothing about ON CONFLICT (ts_device, uid); Heimdal replays the record it has, so the drill does too. 2. ops/drills/t049-replay.sh — history at the live rate, drain, then replay *and* live concurrently, drain, and four assertions from the Writer's own counters: duplicates suppressed equal the replayed 200s; persisted equals the new 200s; nothing dead-lettered, failed, or in the DLQ; and the set of active Prometheus alerts afterwards is the set from before. Equality, not "at least": a replay that stored one record twice or lost one live record would pass a weaker check. 3. docs/runbooks/replay-drill.md records the first measurement and how a real replay reads on the dashboards — the duplicate/persisted gap is the replay and is healthy; watch the *age*, never the depth; a DLQ that moves is the only thing that is wrong; and a late-arrival alert during a replay after a long outage is D5b working.

Measured (22 Sep 2026, one instance of everything on this laptop). 2,667/s replay + 1,333/s live for 60 s: 240,000 × 200, zero 429s, zero 503s, confirm p99 36.5 ms. records_duplicate_suppressed +160,020 — exactly the replayed 200s; records_persisted +119,970 — exactly 39,990 history + 79,980 live; DLQ 6 → 6; no new alert. The queue peaked at 85,555 with the oldest message 29 s old (warning: 300 s for 5 min) and drained within 40 s of the replay ending. One Writer consumed at a peak 3,137/s on one type, 2,092/s of it duplicates — above the ~2,000/s per type Q13a sized for.

What was learned. *4,000/s at exactly the limit does not clip*: the token bucket holds one second of burst and refills continuously, so a steady rate at the cap passes and only a rate above it (T-048 run B) is answered 429 — recorded so a healthy "4,000/s, no 429s" is not read as a broken limiter. And a suppressed duplicate is not free: it is a round trip to an INSERT that does nothing, which is why the Writer's consume rate during a replay is higher than its persist rate and why the drain is the number to size on.

Rejected. *A closed-loop replay* — slows down when the server does (DL-159's reason). *Asserting duplicate_suppressed > 0* — proves a duplicate was seen, not that every one was, and not that no live record was lost. *Alerting on the duplicate counter during the drill "to see it fire"* — ADR-005 says not to, and this drill is the evidence for that rule. *Purging the DLQ residue first so assertion D starts from zero* — the drill must work on a stack that already has alerts; before/after set difference does.

Residual. One type, one instance of each module; a five-type replay is five of these, D8's shape is N Writers on one queue. The corpus is minutes old when replayed; a replay after a multi-week outage meets the late-arrival guard (DL-133), by design — the runbook says so, the drill cannot show it. Whether Heimdal honours the cap stays unmeasurable from here (DL-109).

Phase six · 6–13 October 2026

Five-type delivery, current functions and v6 monitoring

Current implementation traced to code; test figures below are dated evidence from 9 October, not a newly executed regression run

6.1 Make HTTP acceptance explicit

DL-163 adds {"status":"accepted","traceId":"…"} to both ingest paths, after confirmed routed publication. The default response stays 200, configurable to 202, with the same JSON body and matching X-Trace-Id. Publication bytes and error behavior remain unchanged. Acceptance precedes Writer validation, retention decisions and database commit.

6.2 Preserve LTE and network wire shapes

DL-164/165 complete the missing persistence paths. LTE’s V8 table stores typed signal values and identifier strings; the reader maps access_technology back to access_Technology, returns signals as strings and omits absent optional fields. An unknown modem returns only its etxt. Leading zeroes and hexadecimal LAC/CID spelling survive. Measured-plus-error records fail the schema gate.

Network V9 stores an integer root timespan and dynamic interfaces JSONB. Known counters on all accepted interfaces are checked before exact BIGINT conversion and restored as strings on read. DNS arrays, addresses, driver, accepted additional interfaces and nested extensions remain intact. Non-interface extensions are kept separately. Stored SQL keys are internal details and do not replace the wire payload.

6.3 Current classes and functions

Code location / functionImplemented responsibility
InputAmqpConfiguration.statsInputExchange()Declares only the shared durable input topic exchange. Writer owns queues, retries, DLQs and bindings.
InputAmqpConfiguration.statsReturnsCallbackCustomizer()Installs an ERROR logging callback for unroutable returns with trace/message/routing context. HTTP outcome is decided by the confirming publisher, not the callback thread.
InputAmqpConfiguration.confirmingStatsPublisher()Registers ConfirmingStatsPublisher using RabbitTemplate, configured confirm limits and metrics.
ConfirmingStatsPublisher.publish()Sends persistent received bytes unchanged; waits for correlated confirm and checks returned messages. NACK, timeout, interrupt, broker error or unroutable return leads to failed acceptance/503.
StatsIngestController.ingest()One handler for both paths; envelope/clock checks, publish outcome, metrics, then accepted JSON. Payload validation belongs to Writer.
LtestatsBatchWriter.write() / NetstatsBatchWriter.write()Qualified per-type persistence, checked numeric mapping, conflict suppression and shared transaction/delivery outcomes.
LtestatsReader.putData()Rebuilds case-sensitive wire fields and string signal values; omits optional missing members; etxt exits the measured branch.
NetstatsReader.putData()Expands stored interface JSONB into data; restores checked counters as strings and root timespan as integer.
StatsController.list(), record(), deviceFrom(), deviceRange()Exposes four GET route shapes; passes token-derived tenant into read service; missing/foreign UID is 404.
PartitionBootstrap.start() / PartitionMaintenance.ensureBeforeIngestion()Creates the retained window and seven-day horizon before consumers begin.
PartitionMaintenance.maintainOnSchedule()Daily 03:30 UTC pass with per-type stagger/advisory locks; precreates first, then handles expired partitions.
docker/check-monitoring.shRequires exactly four expected up samples at 1 and PostgreSQL pg_up=1 before startup reports ready.

6.4 Monitoring belongs to the application project

DL-166 corrects a real Docker network mismatch: monitoring and applications had different Compose projects, preventing service-name resolution. The apps profile now starts Prometheus and PostgreSQL exporter on the same project network. Grafana remains optional but its image and provisioning are in the offline archive. Local metrics-only public API access is explicit and defaults off in source. Anonymous stats reads and /actuator/info remain 401.

6.5 Recorded verification and remaining observations

9 October recorded checkResult and scope
Full build1,154 tests: common 273, Input 78, Writer 536, API 251, support 16; zero failures/errors/skips.
Schema and contract117 fixture assertions; five schema projections and six drift-detector mutation checks passed.
